#5748. Biden's first-year fundraising fails to crack 1% of Obama's library haul as questions swirl over legacy

Ohio State accounting professor Brian Mittendorf examines the Obama Foundation's financial health, assets and endowment as construction costs hit $1 billion.

Former President Joe Biden has raised a shockingly small amount of funds for his presidential library compared to former President Barack Obama.

The figures are fueling skepticism about how history will remember Biden's single term in the White House.

In 2017, Obama’s first year out of office, the two-term president managed to bring in $232.6 million in donations and contributions to fund the foundation behind his presidential library. By comparison, Biden secured just $1.5 million in 2025 for his presidential library foundation, per tax filings.

This is less than 1% of what his former boss raised in the same amount of time after leaving office.

Even before leaving office, President Donald Trump’s library foundation has already dwarfed that of his predecessor by raising tens of millions of dollars from a variety of sources, including companies that settled lawsuits with him.

OBAMA CENTER ISN'T A TRADITIONAL PRESIDENTIAL LIBRARY. CRITICS SAY IT'S AN ACTIVISM CENTER.

Former US presidents Barack Obama and Joe Biden shake hands during the opening of the Barack Obama Presidential Center in Chicago, Illinois, on June 18, 2026. (Kent NISHIMURA / AFP via Getty Images)

Though the Joe and Jill Biden Presidential Library Foundation has yet to decide on a construction site, it’s already running a deficit, spending $600,000 more than it raised in 2025. A Biden library official claimed that it wasn’t actively fundraising in 2025 in a statement shared with CBS News.

The lack of enthusiasm for Biden’s legacy has led some to claim that Americans may simply forget about his presidency as the years go on.

"Presidencies usually fade," Axios’ Zachary Basu wrote of his legacy. "But in just one year, Biden’s has been reduced to a footnote by a successor committed to dismantling every pillar of Washington’s old liberal order."

Heritage Action press manager Doug Blair told Fox News Digital, "Joe Biden spent half a century in Washington and did nothing to benefit the American people."

"The anemic fundraising for his presidential library is a fitting bookend for a failed presidency Americans are eager to forget," he added. "Joe Biden left Americans worse off by every conceivable measure and his legacy will be a southern border blown wide open, the disgraceful image of him checking his watch as fallen American troops returned home from Afghanistan, and endless scandals and investigations surrounding him and his corrupt family.'

BIDEN'S BATTLE WITH ADVANCING CANCER RENEWS FOCUS ON PROSTATE DISEASE

Allies of the former president point to the fact that Biden presided over the end of the COVID-19 pandemic, influential pieces of domestic legislation and America’s withdrawal from Afghanistan, parts of his legacy that could contribute to historical staying power.

A January 2025 Gallup poll found that 54% of U.S. adults believed history would remember Biden as a "below-average" or "poor" president.

Former U.S. President Joe Biden attends the dedication ceremony for the opening of the Barack Obama Presidential Center in John Lewis Plaza (Photo by Scott Olson/Getty Images)

A source close to Biden’s foundation told CBS News that it ramped up its fundraising in 2026, pulling in multiple multi-million-dollar contributions, including two $5 million donations.

Another source told the outlet that some of Biden's close allies are frustrated that Democratic donors aren’t stepping up to support the former president’s library.

Further complicating Biden’s fundraising efforts, and his ability to nurture his public image, is his advanced age and Stage 4 prostate cancer, which has metastasized to his bones.

OBAMA CENTER OPENS AFTER YEARS-LONG SAGA AS LOCALS WARN ‘MONSTROSITY’ COULD PRICE THEM OUT

Former President Barack Obama and the Obama Presidential Center in Chicago, where construction costs are now projected to reach approximately $1 billion, nearly triple the upper end of the original estimate. (Taylor Hill/Getty Images; Talía Sprague/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

While the increase in funds is no doubt encouraging for Biden’s allies, it still puts them far behind Obama, who raised $164.8 million for his foundation in 2018.

Obama ultimately spent nearly $1 billion on his presidential library in Chicago, a sum Biden likely will not be able to reach given his current fundraising levels.

The Joe and Jill Biden Presidential Library Foundation and the Obama Foundation did not respond to requests for comment when reached by Fox News Digital on Tuesday.

#5746. Tech Giants Are Effectively Ungovernable. Here’s How to Change That.

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Argument: Tech Giants Are Effectively Ungovernable. Here’s How to Change That.

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

American business has minted a new brand of executive: the CEO who is too big to fire. On Nov. 17, Nvidia’s board declared that it had accepted the resignation of Jensen Huang, felled by an affair with an alleged Chinese spy and a romance with one of the executive’s own subordinates. Six days later, Huang was back, restored after a unanimous vote by the same board. Nvidia’s shares soared. Many investors celebrated the return of the leather-jacketed founder credited with making the company the engine of the artificial intelligence revolution.

Three years earlier, OpenAI’s board had dismissed Sam Altman, citing a lack of candor with directors. Five days later, Altman returned, and most of the directors responsible for his removal were out.

American business has minted a new brand of executive: the CEO who is too big to fire. On Nov. 17, Nvidia’s board declared that it had accepted the resignation of Jensen Huang, felled by an affair with an alleged Chinese spy and a romance with one of the executive’s own subordinates. Six days later, Huang was back, restored after a unanimous vote by the same board. Nvidia’s shares soared. Many investors celebrated the return of the leather-jacketed founder credited with making the company the engine of the artificial intelligence revolution.

Three years earlier, OpenAI’s board had dismissed Sam Altman, citing a lack of candor with directors. Five days later, Altman returned, and most of the directors responsible for his removal were out.

In both cases, directors discovered that formal authority to remove a chief executive does not mean the power to do so in practice. The episodes exposed a three-pronged weakness in corporate governance: the challenge of maverick founders, the extraordinary complexity of AI, and the growing dependence of the U.S. economy and national security on a handful of companies at the technological frontier.

Both Altman and Huang have personas, know-how, and market-moving power that made them unfireable. Yet if the boards of the country’s most consequential technology firms cannot exercise ultimate authority over management, these companies are effectively ungovernable. This reveals a major gap in corporate stewardship, underscoring the imperative of robust regulation to ensure that the vulnerabilities of powerful U.S. companies do not become fatal weaknesses for the country’s democracy and society writ large.

The iconic corporate founder is a hallmark of U.S. business culture. Henry Ford and Andrew Carnegie shaped society-altering industries around their personalities and distinctive working styles. Steve Jobs provides the most celebrated historic example of this type of figure in tech. Forced out of Apple in 1985, Jobs returned in 1997 when the company was in steep decline. His track record of inventiveness helped win back employees, customers, investors, and developers. He also persuaded Bill Gates to invest $150 million in Apple and commit to developing Microsoft software for Macintosh computers, building an ecosystem whose fortunes were intertwined with his own and that has dominated the market for decades.

These bold founders may have as much in common with an artist or creator than they do with fellow CEOs. A great architect or film director works with large teams but puts an unmistakable stamp on their work. Although a studio or tradition can continue after its originator dies or departs, investors, collaborators, and audiences do not always stay on.

This analogy helps explain why succession at frontier technology companies is so fraught. Investors bet on a founder’s judgment about technologies whose commercial potential is unproven. Engineers are inspired by a particular visionary. Customers and business partners invest in the future that the founder promises. As these forces rally, they reify perceptions of an individual’s genius, which attracts still more talent and capital. Confidence becomes self-perpetuating.

Such devotion is measurable. Huang topped Glassdoor’s 2026 ranking of CEOs with a 99 percent approval rating among employee reviewers. Thousands of employees reportedly signed a letter seeking his reinstatement this month. His reportedly cruel behavior toward certain employees seemingly did little to puncture that aura.

OpenAI’s 2023 crisis demonstrated the power of such fealty. After its board fired Altman, more than 95 percent of the company’s employees signed a letter threatening to resign unless he came back. Microsoft, OpenAI’s largest commercial partner, rolled out a red carpet to Altman, promising to allow him to rebuild his team there. Faced with losing the workforce that was OpenAI’s principal asset, the board folded. The employees, investors, and commercial partners whose confidence had helped make Altman successful overpowered those charged with supervising him.

These examples do not prove that founders are irreplaceable. Apple’s revenues more than doubled during the early years of John Sculley’s post-Jobs tenure in the 1980s; more recently, the company has thrived under Tim Cook since Jobs’s death. When Uber’s Travis Kalanick was forced out in 2017, investors worried about losing the transportation innovator’s aggressive founder. His successor, Dara Khosrowshahi, now presides over a company that generated $193 billion in gross bookings and $10 billion in free cash flow in 2025.

Board members are a little like grandparents. They admire and care about the baby, comforted that the parent—the CEO—does the daily grind. When the parent disappears, the board is left holding a crying, demanding infant. Sedate quarterly meetings suddenly turn into frantic round-the-clock Zooms. Having lost the one person who they trusted to raise the child, boards want nothing more than to once again pass the progeny into capable hands.

When it comes to AI companies, the problem is magnified by the extraordinary gap between a CEO’s knowledge of frontier technologies and what even accomplished directors can understand. Nvidia’s 10-person board includes Melissa Lora, the former president of Taco Bell; Stephen Neal, a distinguished trial lawyer; and Dawn Hudson, a former Pepsi-Cola and National Football League marketing executive. Three of the 10 have served on the board for more than 28 years each. Just one is under the age of 60. While several board members have technology backgrounds, the emergence of Nvidia’s chips and financing as lynchpins of AI competition has transformed the demands on a group of directors who were chosen at least in part because Huang trusted them not to get in his way. Alongside bleeding-edge technologies, the board is now dealing with matters of foreign espionage, regulatory capture, securities fraud, and export controls. It’s not hard to imagine Nvidia’s directors feeling out of their depth.

The governance challenges created by Nvidia’s technological complexity are compounded by the company’s centrality to the U.S. economy and national security. A 2026 investment analysis estimated that 42 AI-related stocks accounted for 60 percent of the S&P 500’s gains in 2025. By September 2026, Nvidia alone represented approximately 8.4 percent of the index. Huang’s reinstatement on Monday propelled a broader market rally, underscoring how closely U.S. financial fortunes have become tied to confidence in the AI industry. The national-security dependence is at least as consequential. AI is increasingly incorporated into military intelligence, surveillance, cyber operations, logistics, and command-and-control systems. Access to advanced computing is at the heart of competitive tensions with China.

These dependencies help explain U.S. President Donald Trump’s extraordinary intervention in Nvidia’s leadership deliberations. According to Semafor, the president called into a Nvidia board meeting to press for Huang’s return, suggesting that China might recruit him if Nvidia failed to take him back. Though no investigation has been completed and the allegations of Chinese plotting to compromise Huang are alarming, Trump personally vouched that his return would pose no national security risk. It is hard to know who possesses greater leverage in the Trump-Huang relationship—the president who controls regulatory decisions or the corporate titan whose technology is essential to domestic prosperity and national security.

Reliance on individual technology entrepreneurs for vital national functions can compromise public interests. In October, Ukrainian forces experienced serious disruptions to Starshield satellite communications, impairing military operations. Elon Musk subsequently acknowledged that his company, SpaceX, had restricted certain uses of the service, though responsibility for the disruptions remains contested. Society depends on interconnected technological systems that are vulnerable to malfunction and compromise; the power vested in a handful of tech moguls who control these networks is staggering.

U.S. institutions have failed to reckon with this amalgamation of power. Congress remains divided over how to regulate AI. Federal agencies possess fragmented authority over specific risks and no mandate from the White House to address them. Companies develop capabilities faster than public institutions can assess them. CEOs with immense technical resources and public influence end up guiding the officials responsible for overseeing them.

The consequences of this glaring void will reverberate internationally. U.S. corporate leadership is predicated on the perception that U.S. companies operate within a system of enforceable laws, strong oversight, and credible government institutions. This distinguishes U.S. technology from Chinese alternatives, whose governance remains vulnerable to direct state intervention. But if U.S. boards cannot meaningfully restrain powerful executives, and the president can intervene in their internal deliberations, those differentiators collapse. Governments considering whose AI infrastructure to trust will ask whether U.S. companies offer genuine institutional safeguards.

The obvious response is to strengthen corporate oversight. Some mechanisms already exist. Nvidia has an independent board, audit and governance committees, and a confidential reporting hotline. It has now added a specialized national security committee and is upgrading its compliance function. OpenAI maintains a safety and security committee overseeing its operations. But all these arrangements depend on directors whose authority may be subsumed by founder loyalty, commercial pressure, and technological complexity.

Other highly regulated industries offer more robust, proven oversight models. Under Federal Reserve rules, large bank holding companies must maintain independent risk committees, including directors with relevant expertise; chief risk officers report directly to those committees as well as management. Britain requires senior executives at financial firms to be approved by regulators, makes it clear what each is responsible for, and can hold them personally accountable if they fail to take reasonable steps to prevent wrongdoing. U.S. pharmaceutical regulation provides another precedent. Federal rules give drug manufacturers’ quality-control arms the authority to approve or reject products and manufacturing procedures, so that decisions do not lie simply with a CEO’s commercial preferences.

These models suggest an approach to governing companies whose technologies pose exceptional public risks. Congress should establish an independent federal supervisory authority for designated frontier AI companies, including critical infrastructure providers and developers of exceptionally capable models. Its jurisdiction should depend on the risks and systemic importance of technology companies, rather than their size or public-company status. Now that corporate interests have captured the U.S. executive branch, legislative oversight is essential.

Such an institution would need permanent technical staff with expertise in advanced computing, model capabilities, cybersecurity, and intelligence threats. It would require secure access to proprietary information, the power to inspect governance systems, and the authority to compel corrective action. Its independence from day-to-day political intervention would be essential.

Meanwhile, regulated companies should have dedicated board-level risk committees staffed by directors with technical and national-security competence. Those committees should retain their own expert advisors and receive regular reports from both the CEO and independent risk, safety, and compliance officers. Boards should be required to document how they evaluate consequential decisions, oversee potential conflicts of interest, and prepare for abrupt leadership transitions.

A further priority is genuine protection for whistleblowers. Nvidia’s formal policies prohibit retaliation, yet recent reporting suggests that employees fear speaking openly. In technology companies, confidentiality is increasingly difficult to preserve. Employers can potentially reconstruct a source’s identity from records of device use, badge access, communications, and document activity, even when a reporter or investigator promises anonymity. One shudders to think what this might mean for Nvidia employees who recently spoke out about mistreatment or alleged misdeeds, believing that Huang’s tenure was over for good.

A modern whistleblower regime should offer secure channels outside the company, enforceable restrictions on attempts to identify confidential sources, and meaningful penalties for retaliation. An independent regulator should interview employees and business partners without requiring them to expose themselves to management. Such protections are particularly important during investigations involving a powerful founder.

It may be tempting to conclude that Nvidia’s crisis ended happily once Huang returned and the market rallied. The OpenAI episode invited a similar reaction, especially as the company continued its rapid expansion after Altman’s reinstatement. But neither recovery resolves the institutional weakness that allowed directors’ decisions to be reversed under pressure.

U.S. technological leadership cannot depend on a system in which the most influential executives are effectively irreplaceable. A company that is too big to lose its founder may also be too powerful to govern itself. As AI becomes embedded in the economy, national security, and public life, the United States needs oversight institutions capable of withstanding the demands of employees, investors, politicians, and even the most celebrated innovators. The long-term credibility of U.S. technological leadership will depend on it.

Suzanne Nossel is a columnist at Foreign Policy, the Lester Crown senior fellow for U.S. foreign policy and international order at the Chicago Council on Global Affairs, and a senior advisor to the Starling Institute. She is the author of Dare to Speak: Defending Free Speech for All. X: @SuzanneNossel

#5744. The Paradox of American Credibility

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

JOSHUA A. SCHWARTZ is Assistant Professor of International Relations and Emerging Technology at the Carnegie Mellon Institute for Strategy & Technology.

How Fighting Weakens Washington’s Reputation—and Backing Down May Strengthen It

In mid-February, after the United States failed to get Iran to renounce its nuclear ambitions, U.S. President Donald Trump issued an ultimatum. The Iranian leadership had to make “a meaningful deal” with Washington. “Otherwise,” he said, “bad things happen.” Barely eight days later, the United States and Israel began the war against Iran that continues today.

That war has been criticized on many grounds, but analysts have often overlooked the basic assumption about U.S. credibility that it has tested. There is a long-held understanding that the United States’ willingness to use force to stand up for its interests is essential to deterring future adversaries—and convincing them to give in to the kind of sweeping demands the Trump administration made of Tehran. As Elbridge Colby, U.S. undersecretary of defense for policy, said in the early stages of the conflict, “I think both our friends and potential foes are seeing the prowess of the joint force and that also contributes to deterrence.”

Going back to the early years of the Cold War, the concept of credibility—a state’s perceived capabilities and its willingness to use them even at high cost—has played a central role in the decisions that led to and sustained many of the United States’ largest military campaigns. The need to bolster U.S. credibility was a key force driving Washington’s strategy in Korea and Vietnam in the mid-twentieth century, and it played an important role in Iraq and Afghanistan during the so-called war on terror. American strategists today continue to assess foreign policy decisions based on how they might strengthen or weaken the country’s reputation.

But as the results of the Iran campaign have shown, a decision to use force does not always strengthen deterrence. After nearly ten months of war, and the expenditure of billions of dollars of munitions, the United States appears little closer to achieving its objectives of definitively ending Iran’s nuclear threat and ability to project power across the region, let alone securing regime change in Tehran. Meanwhile, the war has badly depleted U.S. military resources that could be used elsewhere and has further eroded the American public’s willingness to support military interventions. Rather than sending a strong signal of U.S. resolve, the Iran war risks conveying the opposite message. Vice President JD Vance argued that the reason the Republicans lost the midterm elections was the Iran war, and government officials in Tehran have gloated that “Americans said no to war.”

In fact, the conventional wisdom that standing firm in a military conflict enhances a state’s credibility misses something important. As the U.S. wars in Vietnam and Iraq showed, a prolonged military campaign may leave the country more cautious about using force, and less willing to bear the costs, in the years that follow. Adversaries notice this. Given that prior efforts by the Trump administration to compromise with Tehran were criticized as “appeasement” by figures like former U.S. Vice President Mike Pence, Trump may be tempted to continue or even expand the Iran campaign. But by taxing U.S. capabilities and deepening war-weariness among the American public, escalation in Iran could instead lead Beijing and Moscow to question Washington’s appetite for future interventions.

Policymakers should be far more skeptical about using military force for the sake of demonstrating U.S. resolve, and they should be less averse to backing down. The Cold War competition with the Soviet Union convinced the United States to intervene in costly proxy wars that consumed enormous human and military resources for questionable gain, and now the strategic competition with China and Russia may do the same. These kinds of conflicts are precisely the ones that are likely to harm, rather than help, U.S. credibility. Instead of obsessing about its reputation for resolve, which it cannot control, Washington must refocus its attention on maintaining the military strength and capabilities it needs to deter its major adversaries. Sometimes the best way to preserve both the capability and the willingness to act in the future is to walk away from conflict in the present.

For decades, policymakers and scholars have treated a country’s credibility as the dark matter of international politics—a hard-to-measure attribute that is nonetheless enormously important for maintaining deterrence. In the 1960s, nuclear deterrence theorist and Nobel Prize winner Thomas Schelling went so far as to say that acquiring a reputation for resolve is “one of the few things worth fighting over.” In keeping with this, it has long been assumed that whereas standing firm communicates to adversaries and allies alike that a country is willing to run risks and bear costs to uphold its interests, backing down or seeking to compromise conveys the opposite. As a result, many U.S. leaders and strategists have drawn on the logic of credibility to justify the most hawkish policies toward adversaries: making maximalist demands, using force, and remaining in wars long past the point where their costs exceeded their benefits.

Vietnam was a case in point. When debating whether to send the first group of U.S. troops to Vietnam in 1965, U.S. Secretary of State Dean Rusk told President Lyndon Johnson, “If the Communist world finds out we will not pursue our commitments to the end, I don’t know where they will stay their hand.” Later, as the United States became bogged down in the war, Assistant Secretary of Defense John McNaughton went further, arguing in a formerly top-secret memo that the primary reason for continuing the Vietnam War was to maintain America’s “reputation as a guarantor” and “avoid a humiliating U.S. defeat.” According to this logic, 58,000 U.S. soldiers and likely over a million Vietnamese people were killed not because the outcome of the war was itself crucial to U.S. interests, but because Washington worried how others—especially the Soviets—would perceive it if it withdrew.

Since the Cold War, the same justifications have been used to push the country into, and to sustain, many other conflicts. This was especially true during the so-called Forever Wars the United States was drawn into after the 9/11 attacks. On the night of 9/11, U.S. Secretary of Defense Donald Rumsfeld said, “We need to bomb something else [other than the relatively weak Taliban] to prove that we’re, you know, big and strong and not going to be pushed around by these kinds of attacks.” The logic of credibility not only played a role in Washington launching the Iraq war in 2003 but also locked it into the conflict, even as the costs rose and the raison d’être for the war—weapons of mass destruction—turned out to be false. As President George W. Bush said in 2006, “If we were to leave before the mission is complete, it would hurt U.S. credibility.”

Conversely, strategists have been quick to criticize U.S. leaders who backed down. During the Syrian civil war, many were concerned about the reputational damage to the United States when President Barack Obama declined to respond to the Syrian government’s use of sarin gas to attack a rebel-held suburb of Damascus, thereby violating a red line that Obama had previously declared. In 2021, many foreign policy hawks reprimanded President Joe Biden for withdrawing from Afghanistan and for waiving some U.S. sanctions on Russia’s Nord Stream 2 pipeline project. At the time, Michael McCaul, the top Republican on the House Foreign Affairs Committee, said, “U.S. credibility from Kyiv to Taipei cannot withstand another blow of this nature.” More recently, Trump himself has been accused of backing down against China on tariffs, which some have decried as another example of “Trump always chickens out,” or “TACO.” In each of these cases, critics argued that Washington’s failure to act has compromised the perception that the United States is willing to run risks and bear costs to achieve its objectives.

Despite the persistent assumption that U.S. credibility requires using force and refusing to back down, that thinking has been repeatedly challenged over the past decade. For one thing, experience has shown that a country’s past behavior may be a poor indicator of its present-day resolve. In April, for example, The New York Times reported that Trump believed that Iran was unlikely to attempt to close the Strait of Hormuz based on its past actions—such as its failure to mount a robust response to the U.S. and Israeli bombing of its nuclear facilities in the 12-day war in 2025. In reality, however, Iran has been far less restrained than expected in the current conflict and moved quickly not only to close the strait but also to attack U.S. allies and U.S. military bases across the region. In other words, Tehran’s failure to stand firm in 2025 had little bearing on its resolve in the present war.

In any case, it is far from clear that a country’s reputation for resolve is something that it can control by its actions alone. As Keren Yarhi-Milo has explained in Foreign Affairs, “credibility is in the eye of the beholder.” Even if a state acts decisively to signal strength, an adversary may not interpret that action as reason to reconsider its behavior. For example, despite Washington’s intention to communicate high resolve by bearing tremendous costs in the Vietnam War, the North Vietnamese Communist Party Central Committee concluded in 1965 that “the domestic situation in the United States, however, will not allow them to fully utilize their economic and military power in their war of aggression.” In other words, Hanoi ignored the intended signal from Washington and refused to back down.

Other pitfalls are associated with demonstrating resolve by standing firm and fighting. At the extreme, a state that leans too much on military strength can gain a reputation for aggression in ways that undermine long-term deterrence. Napoleonic France and Nazi Germany both had strong reputations for resolve, but their offensive actions ultimately provoked other countries rather than deterring them. There is certainly a risk that U.S. actions under the second Trump administration—such as decapitating the Iranian government and threatening to annex Canada, Gaza, and Greenland—could similarly backfire. For example, there is growing evidence that U.S. allies and partners in Europe, the Middle East, and Asia are rethinking their reliance on the United States and are seeking other sources of security. Far from conceding to Trump’s demands, Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney has described a “rupture” in relations between Washington and Ottawa and advocated for middle powers to band together to protect their sovereignty.

Even if using force bolsters a country’s reputation for resolve, it may harm the country’s broader credibility. For instance, Russia’s 2022 invasion of Ukraine and its willingness to absorb hundreds of thousands of casualties since then has sent a strong signal about its resolve. Yet its dramatic underperformance on the battlefield against a far smaller and less well-equipped army has likely diminished the international perception of its military effectiveness. The war in Iran could similarly tarnish the United States’ military reputation. Since February, Washington has depleted at least half of its precision-strike missiles and advanced air defense interceptor missiles. It will likely take years to replenish these stockpiles given slow manufacturing times for so-called exquisite platforms. In the meantime, the United States has fewer advanced systems to draw on if China makes a move against Taiwan or if Russia attacks Poland or Estonia. For both Russia’s and the United States’ military campaigns, then, the net effect has been to reduce rather than enhance the countries’ reputation for military capability in the eyes of their respective adversaries.

Quite apart from the negative consequences that often come with decisions to use force, there is an even more fundamental problem with conventional thinking about credibility. In practice, backing down can actually be more helpful to a country’s reputation for resolve than fighting. This becomes especially clear in looking at the long-term effects of engaging in military interventions. Although the United States was victorious in World War I, the conflict produced a lingering war-weariness among the U.S. public and key policymakers. In the years after the Armistice, Americans expressed strong support for the idea that the United States should stay out of any future European war, a sentiment that was ultimately codified in the Neutrality Acts of the 1930s. Only after Japan attacked Pearl Harbor in December 1941, more than two years after war began in Europe, did the United States enter World War II. Fighting in World War I sent a signal of strong resolve to adversaries, but Americans’ war-weariness and resurgent isolationism in the years that followed conveyed that the United States would be less resolved to fight.

This dynamic has been even more apparent in the aftermath of major U.S. wars since then. Following the Vietnam War, President Jimmy Carter declared that “young Americans should never again be sent to die in battle unless our own nation is endangered.” Consider how this kind of thinking affected adversaries’ view of the United States: In deciding to invade Kuwait in 1990, Iraqi dictator Saddam Hussein assumed that the United States was intensely casualty averse and might not intervene or would back down quickly. General Wafiq al-Samarrai, Iraq’s head of military intelligence during the Gulf War, said in a 1996 interview that Saddam “thought that America’s involvement in Vietnam had badly damaged its willingness to use military power.” Although the Vietnam War was primarily fought to buttress the United States’ reputation for resolve, it had the opposite effect in practice.

And again, during and after the U.S. war in Iraq, Americans became skeptical of costly military interventions. As a result, recent administrations have been especially hesitant to commit ground troops in combat operations. Iran is undoubtedly aware of this so-called Iraq syndrome, which may embolden Tehran to act more aggressively without having to fear a large-scale American ground operation.

There is also experimental evidence that backing down may strengthen a country’s long-term reputation for resolve. In a survey I conducted in 2021 with 100 British parliamentarians, respondents were randomly divided into two groups. The first group read about a situation in which a hypothetical country backed down from a prior conflict. The second group read about a hypothetical country that stood firm in a prior conflict but displayed the same signs of war-weariness or “syndromes” that emerged in the United States after the Vietnam and Iraq wars. Both groups were then asked to estimate the likelihood that the country in question would stand firm or back down in a current dispute with the United Kingdom. On average, parliamentarians believed that the country that backed down in the prior dispute would have greater resolve in the current dispute. In other words, if fighting leads to war-weariness, then backing down can actually be better for a state’s future credibility.

As the U.S. campaign in Iran has demonstrated, choosing to fight to maintain a reputation for resolve is an incredibly risky gamble and may backfire. To avoid repeating the same mistake, U.S. policymakers and strategists need to rethink the relationship between credibility and fighting. First, the United States should avoid fighting altogether when the primary objective is shoring up reputation rather than defending a core national issue. If a conflict directly involves key security and economic interests, as did the U.S. Revolutionary War and World War II, then fighting can be a logical choice, assuming the United States has sufficient military capabilities to win the war at reasonable cost. But if the primary argument for the war relates to future credibility, as in Vietnam, then policymakers should be wary.

Second, if Washington determines that an adversary must be confronted with military force, then it must focus on limited, achievable objectives. Wars that drag on because they have much larger aims, or are prolonged simply for reputational reasons, may erode the country’s credibility by draining munitions and making Americans more reluctant to fight in the future. The two U.S. interventions against Saddam Hussein’s Iraq illustrate this point well. In the limited 1991 Gulf War, the United States quickly expelled Iraqi forces from Kuwait at low cost and before war-weariness could set in among the U.S. public. But the U.S.-led invasion of Iraq in 2003, in which Washington pursued the far more expansive goal of regime change, continued for years at high cost and made many U.S. policymakers and ordinary citizens alike less supportive of using force in the future.

Finally, policymakers should focus less on the U.S. reputation for resolve, which they cannot control, and more on the country’s military capabilities, which they can. To deter Beijing and Moscow, Washington needs to convince both adversaries that it has the ability to prosecute a modern great-power war. The problem is that, although the United States continues to have the most advanced military capabilities and weapons systems in the world, they are limited in quantity, extremely expensive, and take a long time to replace. In the course of just a few months of fighting against Iran, the United States depleted huge percentages of its advanced and expensive stockpiles of offensive and defensive missiles.

This alarming shortfall raises new questions about whether the United States could sustain a longer war of attrition of the type Russia and Ukraine are fighting. Washington urgently needs to replenish its stockpiles and rethink its dependence on a small number of exquisite systems. It also needs to avoid costly conflicts that distract it from the principal threats it faces from China and Russia. Rather than making U.S. credibility stronger in the eyes of the world, continuing the war in Iran could well undermine it, just as fighting in Vietnam and Iraq did.

#5743. Boston marks Thanksgiving as ‘Day of Mourning’ while historic Plymouth battles anti-Thanksgiving activists

Boston City Council slams Thanksgiving Day narrative taught in American schools.

The Boston City Council recognized Thanksgiving as a "National Day of Mourning" ahead of the holiday, continuing a recurring acknowledgment from city leaders as nearby Plymouth, home of the "first Thanksgiving," battles activists in court over their Indigenous peoples observances.

Last week, the Boston City Council passed a resolution designating Thursday, Nov. 26, as a "National Day of Mourning" in Boston after it was introduced by Councilor Benjamin Weber and co-sponsored by 11 other councilors. While the resolution carries no legal weight or changes to city operations, it reflects an annual tradition for the Boston City Council, which has passed similar ceremonial recognitions ahead of Thanksgiving in previous years.

"The story of Thanksgiving that so many of us grew up learning in school fails to acknowledge the devastating consequences that colonialization had and continues to have on Indigenous peoples, " Boston City Councilor At-Large Julia Mejia said in favor of the resolution during a city council meeting on Nov. 18.

HOW VIRGINIA SCHOOL DISTRICT SHAMES KIDS TO MARK THANKSGIVING

Boston City Councilor Julia Mejia listens during a Boston City Council meeting at Boston City Hall on June 3, 2026. (Erin Clark/The Boston Globe via Getty Image)

"I think it's important that we recognize that these are not simply injustices of the past," Mejia continued. "And as a city, we have a responsibility to ensure that our commitment to equity and justice includes the Indigenous peoples whose lands we live on."

Meanwhile, in nearby Plymouth — the famed site of the 1621 "first Thanksgiving" feast between the Pilgrims and the Wampanoag people, where the United American Indians of New England (UAINE) has held the annual National Day of Mourning every year since 1970 — town officials and organizers appeared in court Monday over a dispute about this year's event logistics.

ARIZONA MAN THREATENED WITH ARREST FOR PREACHING AT HOLIDAY FESTIVAL FILES LAWSUIT

Illustration of a pilgrim celebration held in Plymouth, Massachusetts on August 01, 1853. (Kean Collection/Getty Images)

The legal battle centers on a 1998 settlement, signed after a clash between police and demonstrators the year prior, that requires Plymouth to make a mobile stage available for the event.

According to CBS Boston, town officials now say they will not set up a stage on state property without proof of insurance or an outside vendor, citing liability concerns, while UAINE argues the demand violates the decades-old agreement.

Demonstrators march on Main Street during the 56th National Day of Mourning on Cole's Hill in Plymouth, Mass., on Nov. 27, 2025. (Boston Globe via Getty Images)

The Plymouth Independent reported that Plymouth Superior Court Judge Brian Glenny took the arguments under advisement Monday, leaving a ruling pending just days before Thursday's 57th annual gathering on Cole's Hill. According to the outlet, Glenny did not express when a ruling might come, though lawyers involved indicated he understands "time is of the essence."

However, regardless, a UAINE leader told the Plymouth Independent that the event would continue as planned. "I can assure you we will be there Thursday," Mahtowin Munro told the local outlet.

#5741. America’s Bad Bargain in Venezuela

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

Javier Corrales is Dwight W. Morrow 1895 Professor of Political Science at Amherst College and the author of Autocracy Rising: How Venezuela Transitioned to Authoritarianism.

U.S. Dealmaking Is Strengthening the Chavista Regime

After the U.S. military captured then Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro in January, the United States set out on a mission to fix what President Donald Trump called a “dead country.” With broad authority over Venezuela’s agenda, officials in the Trump administration promised to free the country from socialism. As Secretary of State Marco Rubio said, when he was called before the Senate in late January, the administration’s ultimate objective was to establish “a friendly, stable, prosperous Venezuela, and democratic, in which all elements of society are represented in free and fair elections.”

Rubio laid out a three-stage framework for managing this democratic transition: stabilization, economic recovery, and, finally, transition. Washington is currently focused on the second stage, recovering the economy, but it is proceeding so quickly and with such little transparency that it is jeopardizing the stated end goal entirely. U.S. dealmaking with the country’s oil sector, in particular, is reifying rather than transforming Venezuela’s economic rentism: a model of governance in which the state controls important sectors of the economy and offers business contracts and market privileges to firms that support its agenda. This is precisely the system that facilitated the Venezuelan state’s rampant corruption over the past 25 years, allowing autocratic leaders to drown out democratic forces and win elections.

As it pursues its economic goals with dizzying speed, the Trump administration is also slow-walking democratization efforts. For months, Washington excluded opposition leader María Corina Machado and her party, Vente Venezuela, from any direct involvement in negotiations. Machado had left the country last December to receive a Nobel Peace Prize after nearly 20 months in hiding, and after Maduro’s removal, the United States did little to help her return. Venezuela’s pro-democracy forces have thus been left feeling like they are pushing the regime in Caracas to democratize with the acquiescence, but not necessarily the full enthusiasm, of the United States.

Last month, Machado returned to Venezuela, and now she has the potential to change the game. Machado could become the greatest asset or, possibly, the greatest disruptor to Venezuela’s democratic transition. If Machado coordinates with the faction of the opposition that is currently negotiating with the government, any outcome will be seen as legitimate. But if she opposes and speaks out against the result, Venezuelans will find themselves in a familiar and dangerous place: with a divided opposition, a government with few constraints on its power, and a transition in purgatory.

So far, Washington has squandered its immense leverage over Caracas. By dragging out the transition and pursuing questionable economic deals, Washington is endangering not only its own end goal but also the greatest opportunity Venezuela has had to overcome more than a quarter century of autocratic governance.

During Trump’s first term, his administration centered its criticism of Maduro on the country’s socialist model. In February 2019, weeks after recognizing the opposition leader Juan Guaidó as Venezuela’s interim president, Trump promised in a speech that the “twilight hour of socialism has arrived in our hemisphere.” That campaign to oust Maduro failed, but Trump’s diagnosis of what ails Venezuela was wrong to begin with. Over the past two decades, Venezuela has not been operating primarily under a socialist model but an extractivist one. And since January 3, the United States has seemed only to reinforce it.

Venezuela is an oil state. Under Chavismo, the ideology named for Maduro’s predecessor Hugo Chávez, the state used oil revenues as a political slush fund to reward allies. Little went toward investments in the oil sector or the rest of the economy. Secret deals with oil magnates at home and abroad guided most governance decisions. Whereas the government’s critics faced legal attacks, the well-connected received impunity. Chávez implemented elements of socialism, of course, with massive nationalizations and price controls, but the key priority was collusion and special deals. Many of the state’s allies were poor Venezuelans whose votes kept the autocrats in power, but less noticed were the economic elites. These private actors shunned opposition figures and refrained from any public criticism of the regime; in exchange, they accumulated regulatory favors, access to preferential exchange rates, or exclusive contracts with the state. This model even applied internationally; around 2007, Chávez began redirecting oil sales from the United States to China, both to reward China for being so friendly toward the regime and to preempt a possible U.S. divestment from the Venezuelan oil sector. Oil booms in the first decade of the century allowed the country’s populist leaders to build vertical patronage systems that rewarded loyalty and starved out dissent.

Eventually, however, this system ran its course, leaving Venezuela’s oil industry in shambles. In the 1990s, before Chávez took power, oil production hovered around 3.5 million barrels per day; by 2019, oil production had dropped to 700,000 to 800,000 barrels per day. The government has recognized this dismal state; acting President Delcy Rodríguez has said that the oil sector will need as much as $76 billion in external capital to recover over the next five years.

The United States is a plausible partner for Venezuela’s oil recovery. Washington is responsive to high domestic and global demand for oil, especially since the war in Iran has disrupted flows from the Middle East. American oil firms have the resources to rebuild Venezuelan production and, just as important, the refineries needed to process Venezuelan oil, which is among the world’s heaviest and most sulfurous.

But rather than replacing, or at least revamping, this Chavista system, the United States seems to have embraced its structure. In August, the U.S. government struck an agreement with North American Blue Energy Partners (NABEP), a private Venezuelan corporation, that Trump called “the biggest oil deal in the history of the world.” According to the White House fact sheet of the deal, the Venezuelan state granted NABEP a 100-year concession to operate 17 oil fields, which could cover one-fifth of Venezuela’s total proven reserves. The company is led by Alejandro Betancourt, a Venezuelan citizen being investigated in Spain and Switzerland for money laundering and widely regarded as having collaborated with Chávez and acting as an intermediary between Trump and Rodríguez. Meanwhile, the agreement gives the U.S. Defense Department a 35 percent stake in NABEP, and the State Department gets the right to buy 20 percent of NABEP’s oil at the cost of production—meaning NABEP makes no profit on that share—and has preferential access, at market prices, to the rest.

Outside of these details, little is known about the arrangement or how it came to be. There was no competitive public bidding on the fields—a practice that is generally recommended every time governments assign contracts to private and foreign actors—and there was no explanation of how Washington will manage the export proceeds or clarity on who will fund the necessary investments. The U.S. Congress has not reviewed the deal, although Democrats, with a new majority starting in January, have promised to investigate it.

This deal is also wildly unpopular across Venezuela’s political spectrum. Although Venezuelans have generally celebrated the return of U.S. capital to the country, nearly 80 percent of Venezuelans did not support the oil deal, according to a poll released in September by Meganálisis. Even Machado, who rarely criticizes the United States, has expressed reservations, saying that Betancourt has been responsible for “obscene corruption.” Hardcore leftists see the deal as capitalist robbery. Nationalists see it as neoimperialism. Democratic forces see it as a bailout for the dictatorship. Ordinary citizens see it as a missed opportunity to spread Venezuela’s wealth more equitably.

These oil and gas deals could generate an oil boom if investment comes quickly, production capacity is restored, competitive bidding expands, and government spending and welfare institutions are reformed. But as Trump said when celebrating the deal at the United Nations in September, “To the victor belong the spoils,” and the victors, in this case, are Washington, the ruling party, and a private businessman.

The Venezuelan state has also shown little interest in helping the Venezuelan people and directing petrodollars toward human capital or public welfare. Instead, it will likely do what Chavismo perfected in previous booms: bankroll patronage networks, buy off corporate allies, deny funds to competitors, and spend on electoral campaigns. These new deals could very well end up financing the ruling party’s political rebirth.

The rest of the Venezuelan economy’s recovery, meanwhile, remains a mystery. There have been no efforts toward establishing universal property rights, modernizing contract law, reforming the financial sector, or dismantling state monopolies. Without such inclusive economic rights and democratic rules, oil windfalls seldom produce equitable growth. They produce skewed gains for the state and its friends.

The White House sees the deal as locking Venezuela into a secure economic relationship with the United States for at least 100 years. This week, he referred to the country as “our new acquisition.” But this might be a false sense of security. The agreement is so distasteful to so many Venezuelans that, rather than inoculate Venezuela against anti-imperialist socialism, it is making a radical backlash more likely.

Almost a year after Maduro’s departure, very few concrete steps have been taken to organize a free and fair election in Venezuela. Instead, the Rodríguez government and an opposition delegation led by Dinorah Figuera, who is president of Venezuela’s exiled National Assembly in Spain, held a series of talks this fall. The parties did make progress, including agreeing to unblock certain banned websites and to allow the government to reclaim sanctioned gold reserves held by the Bank of England. Most significantly, after months of pressure from the Venezuelan opposition, the Venezuelan government agreed in August to replace all 32 judges in the Supreme Tribunal of Justice, accepting nominations from civil society that included the opposition. This is a gigantic baby step. In Venezuela, the Supreme Tribunal of Justice long served at the center of Venezuela’s famous autocratic legalism. It was the regime’s preeminent weapon to disqualify opposition candidates and elected officials, accepting laws that justified repression, refusing to investigate extrajudicial killings, and persecuting opposition figures on fabricated charges. An independent judiciary could invalidate current laws that criminalize dissent and establish new ones to promote fair electoral rules. Establishing even a modest framework for an independent judiciary is indispensable for any transition to minimal democracy.

And yet committing to reform the judiciary, however important, is not enough. A full transition to democracy will require far more: a new constitution with robust institutional checks and balances, new laws promoting civil liberties, and more oversight of intelligence and security organizations, which have been known to engage in arbitrary detentions and torture. This will also require retraining the law and order apparatus, namely the National Police and the military’s internal security forces, the Bolivarian National Guard, to respect civil rights; redesigning the electoral system and naming independent electoral authorities; and rebuilding civil society and a free press. Judicial reform in isolation will not dismantle an entrenched autocratic regime.

This week, Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth announced an expansion of U.S.-Venezuelan military cooperation, mostly targeted at fighting crime. But fighting crime effectively does not necessarily require more brute force but rather good intelligence, a robust criminal justice system, a professional and law-abiding police force, and investments in community building, namely in strengthening ties of trust between neighbors and police. It is not clear that these elements will be part of this new U.S.-Venezuelan security cooperation. Unless new agreements come with commitments for internal state reform, the United States risks helping a dictatorship acquire even more coercive power.

Most important, the transition process has ignored the will of the people. Most Venezuelans want a fair election, plain and simple. In a Meganálisis poll conducted in April, 87 percent of respondents agreed that the “solution for Venezuela is a presidential election in 2026.” Even with so much material deprivation—inflation is in the triple digits, and close to 70 percent of the population still lives in poverty—the people want to vote first.

Rubio and Rodríguez have argued that stabilization is necessary before elections can be held. But there is reason to believe that elections are not the result of stability but the source of stability. Elections produce legitimacy for authorities. The current authorities not only aided the suppression of the last election, in 2024, but they have also passed the limit of 180 days that an interim government can serve, according to Venezuela’s constitution. Any policy that it advances is, thus, also less legitimate than those put in place by an elected government.

Moreover, Machado’s absence from the process so far risks making any major deal between the government and the opposition fragile. She is the most popular politician in the country and the leader of the largest pro-democracy movement that Latin America has seen since the first decade of this century. Across multiple polls, including by Meganálisis and AtlasIntel, she has approval ratings between 57 and 76, compared with Rodríguez’s single digits, and multiple international accounts have shown that her substitute in the 2024 election, Edmundo González, easily won. This popularity makes Machado a threat to the Chavista regime, and her exclusion likely came at least in part as a request by the Rodríguez government.

Rather than inaugurating a democratic and economic renaissance, the United States is potentially enabling the survival of a rentist regime. During the oil boom of the first two decades of this century, the Chavista regime insulated itself from domestic accountability by exchanging oil rents for votes and elite support. China served as its primary geopolitical patron, unconcerned with accountability so long as the crude flowed eastward. The United States’ playbook today eerily imitates this model. Venezuela’s main external partner has now changed, but that partner has similar functions. By prioritizing narrow resource extraction over deep institutional reform, the United States has opted to become the senior partner in a rentier compact. Washington is Venezuela’s new toll collector.

Thus far, Venezuela’s democratic transition has delivered only a commitment to reform the Supreme Tribunal of Justice. Nearly a year after Maduro’s ousting, there is no election calendar, no clear plan to reform the electoral system, and no meaningful internal changes to how the government generates and spends its revenues. Despite all the leverage Trump claims to have over the Venezuelan government, outside of business deals, he has very little to show for it.

If this trajectory persists, the United States will not propel a transition to democracy, market principles, and the rule of law in Venezuela. It will instead re-create the very conditions that produced Venezuela’s autocracy in the first place.

#5740. What’s Holding Up Ukraine’s Air Defense?

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

World Brief: What’s Holding Up Ukraine’s Air Defense?

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Understanding the conflict four years in.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at delayed U.S. Patriot air defense missile shipments to Ukraine, Israeli forces killing a top Hamas official, and yet another purge within China’s leadership.

As Russia batters Ukraine’s cities with missile strikes, U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth has delayed sending scores of Patriot air defense missiles to the embattled country, including those held by European nations, according to a Financial Times report.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at delayed U.S. Patriot air defense missile shipments to Ukraine, Israeli forces killing a top Hamas official, and yet another purge within China’s leadership.

As Russia batters Ukraine’s cities with missile strikes, U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth has delayed sending scores of Patriot air defense missiles to the embattled country, including those held by European nations, according to a Financial Times report.

In total, the U.S. has held up around 60 Patriot air defense missiles, including more advanced PAC-3 missiles as well as around 20 PAC-2 missiles. The missiles include those that are already held in European stockpiles but require U.S. sign-off before their transfer.

The missile transfers were originally set to occur in September, October, and November in batches of around 10 missiles each. A further delivery is set for December. Ukraine began sounding the alarm publicly in October.

Patriot air defense missiles are Ukraine’s primary defense against Russian ballistic missiles, which Russia has used in an accelerating campaign to make Ukraine’s major cities unlivable.

Since September, Russia has attacked Ukraine’s energy infrastructure more than 350 times, frequently resulting in days with no regular supply of power or water, a growing concern as winter approaches. The Pentagon is separately delaying approving a plan to allow Ukraine to produce its own Patriot missiles domestically.

The slowed deliveries follow interruptions in recent weeks to the functioning of Starshield, a version of the Starlink satellite internet service that Ukraine relies on for battlefield communication. Ukraine uses Starshield in its mid-range strike drones, as well as in systems used to defend against jet-powered Russian attack drones. Starshield is owned by Elon Musk’s SpaceX. Musk and the Trump administration have traded blame over who is responsible for the throttling of Ukraine’s Starshield access.

Even as Russia scores wins in its missile campaign against Ukraine, however, recently leaked data and Ukraine’s own strikes illustrate the high price Moscow is paying.

Russia has lost at least 490,000 soldiers, according to data derived from a leaked Russian government database and analyzed by independent Russian news outlet Meduza. Even that may be an underestimate—a further 230,000 are listed as missing in action, many of whom are likely dead but not yet officially recorded as such. That means that as many as 720,000 may have died in Ukraine. The losses place the war in Ukraine as the deadliest conflict for Russia since World War II.

Russia primarily sends volunteers to fight in Ukraine and pays large bonuses as an incentive. While Russia has so far been able to find enough volunteers to staff its army, the losses are an economic and social strain on the country. Data compiled by Janis Kluge, an expert on Russian recruitment, shows a slowdown in recruiting for October as compared to last year.

Meanwhile, Ukraine’s long-range strike capacity continues to develop. On Tuesday, Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky confirmed that Ukraine was behind a strike on a Russian cargo ship in the Gulf of Finland. Ukrainian drone also recently attacked a Russian warship docked at a port in Libya. Ukraine on Tuesday separately launched yet another mass wave of strikes on Russia, with Russia stating that it shot down 699 drones.

Deadly airstrikes. Israeli forces have “eliminated” Naim Abu Nahal, the head of Hamas’s internal security apparatus, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and Defense Minister Israel Katz announced in a joint statement posted on X on Tuesday. Nahal took on the role in September, after Israeli forces seized his predecessor in a Gaza ground operation and took him to Israel. “No terrorist will succeed in evading the long arm of the State of Israel,” the statement added.

The Israeli military also launched two airstrikes that killed six other people in Gaza on Tuesday, including a teenage boy and two police officers, who were killed at a police station, according to Palestinian health officials and first responders. Gaza’s police force slammed what it called “an ongoing campaign targeting the police apparatus aimed at sowing chaos,” the BBC reported; Israeli forces said they struck “military operatives.”

Corruption probe. A top Chinese Communist Party official once considered a possible successor to Chinese leader Xi Jinping is being investigated “for suspected severe violations of discipline and laws,” Xinhua reported on Tuesday, in the latest example of ongoing purges of China’s leadership.

The official, Chen Jining, is the head of the Communist Party in Shanghai, China’s most populous city and a key economic center. He is also a member of China’s Politburo, an elite group of 24 officials that help lead the country. He had been seen as likely to rise within the ranks of China’s government, having previously served as mayor of Beijing.

The move is part of Xi’s long-running anti-corruption campaign that has seen hundreds of top officials, including many within the military, convicted. Since 2025, three other members of the Politburo have also faced corruption charges, and in the last two months, corruption charges were announced against both Beijing’s deputy mayor and the former mayor of major city Chongqing.

Xi’s consolidation of power comes ahead of a likely fourth term as president beginning next year, meaning that he will hold power until at least 2032.

Money up front. Japan will pay its full contribution to the International Criminal ​Court (ICC) ahead of schedule following an appeal by the court after the United States sanctioned it, according to Prime Minister Sanae ‌Takaichi.

Japan is one of the largest contributors to the court, accounting for around 15 percent of its funding. The early payment will give the court a larger financial cushion after U.S. sanctions take effect in April 2027.

The Trump administration launched a campaign against the ICC in July, starting with pressuring other nations to withdraw from it. That effort has been largely unsuccessful, with only four countries—Tajikistan, Chad, Venezuela, and Naoero—leaving the court. In October, the United States sanctioned the ICC itself, which U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio called a “rogue court.”

The United States has long been concerned about the ICC’s ability to prosecute U.S. soldiers. However, the Trump administration’s pressure on the ICC represents the sharpest attack yet on the body.

Sunset today will see the appearance of a supermoon, during which the celestial body will be about 30 percent brighter and nearly 20 percent larger than normal. In North America, a full moon that falls after Nov. 7 is known as a “beaver” moon, as it usually occurs around when beavers are building their dams and stocking up on food. If you happen to miss this month’s supermoon, though, don’t worry: There are three more coming in a row in December, January, and February, according to NASA. Happy moongazing!

#5737. Armenia’s Pivot to Washington

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Interview: Armenia’s Pivot to Washington

The photographs lining the walls of the stairs of the Armenian Embassy in Washington are impossible to miss. There are multiple portraits of Armenian Prime Minister Nikol Pashinyan with U.S. President Donald Trump in the White House last year; pictures of Pashinyan and U.S. Vice President J.D. Vance in Yerevan from February of that same year, when Vance became the senior-most sitting U.S. official to visit Armenia; and photos of U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio during his visit to Yerevan last May. There are also multiple pictures of the Oval Office prayer meeting organized by the embassy in September, where Trump met with leaders from the Armenian Christian community in the United States.

I asked if they change the photographs with each administration, and the embassy’s spokesperson informed me that this is the first time any photographs have been put up—to mark the momentous nature of the Trump administration’s relationship with Armenia.

The photographs lining the walls of the stairs of the Armenian Embassy in Washington are impossible to miss. There are multiple portraits of Armenian Prime Minister Nikol Pashinyan with U.S. President Donald Trump in the White House last year; pictures of Pashinyan and U.S. Vice President J.D. Vance in Yerevan from February of that same year, when Vance became the senior-most sitting U.S. official to visit Armenia; and photos of U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio during his visit to Yerevan last May. There are also multiple pictures of the Oval Office prayer meeting organized by the embassy in September, where Trump met with leaders from the Armenian Christian community in the United States.

I asked if they change the photographs with each administration, and the embassy’s spokesperson informed me that this is the first time any photographs have been put up—to mark the momentous nature of the Trump administration’s relationship with Armenia.

Trump brokered the signing of a joint declaration in August 2025 between Pashinyan and Azerbaijani President Ilham Aliyev, which committed Armenia and Azerbaijan to resolving their decadeslong violent conflict over disputed territories. It’s one of several wars that Trump claimed to have solved during his second term as he lobbied for the Nobel Peace Prize, though the two countries have yet to sign a formal peace agreement more than a year later.

“The final peace deal is not there, but we have peace on the ground,” Armenian Ambassador Narek Mkrtchyan said. “We have trade relations with Azerbaijan right now—we are buying some stuff from them, they are buying from us—and this is very important for the peace. Of course, there are still some issues that need to be addressed, but in general, peace is in the region.”

Monday marked movement on two of the biggest outstanding issues.

Armenia put forward a draft proposal of its new constitution that removes language calling for reunification with the Nagorno-Karabakh region, a disputed territory within Azerbaijan that was controlled by ethnic Armenians until an Azerbaijani military offensive deposed them in 2023. Pashinyan has supported the new constitution—which Azerbaijan has demanded as a condition to reach a permanent peace deal—and has said that he will resign if a referendum to adopt it fails.

The second development was the official launch of the Trump Route for International Peace and Prosperity (TRIPP), a roughly 27-mile transit corridor between Azerbaijan and its exclave of Nakhchivan that was agreed (and rebranded from the “Zangezur corridor”) during the joint declaration in Washington last August. The TRIPP corridor passes through Armenian territory and will be controlled by the Armenian government, though it only retains a 26 percent stake in the corridor’s development—the remaining 74 percent will be held by the U.S. government.

Mkrtchyan, who previously served as the Pashinyan government’s minister for labor and social affairs, was appointed to be the country’s ambassador to Washington in late August 2025, just weeks after the Trump-brokered accord with Azerbaijan was signed.

“The current agenda that we have with the United States of America, we never had before,” he said.

Armenia has high hopes from Trump and TRIPP. “It actually has the potential to change the concept of the South Caucasus. Previously, this region has always been associated with conflict, with instability,” Mkrtchyan said. “This region has the potential to turn into a territory of dialogue, of connectivity, of prosperity, and cooperation, and the TRIPP project is an organic component of this.”

Mkrtchyan also corrected me when I referred to TRIPP as a “corridor,” describing it instead as an “infrastructure project route” with “roads, railroads, fiber optics, transmission lines, possibly pipelines.” TRIPP is now “becoming more than a bilateral project” for peace and connectivity between Armenia and Azerbaijan, he said, and will eventually turn into a key trade passage between Central Asia and Europe—entirely under Armenian jurisdiction. “It’s going to be a new global supply chain,” Mkrtchyan said.

Those ambitions could run headlong into Armenia’s geopolitical reality. The landlocked country also shares a 193-mile land border with Turkey that has remained closed since 1993, when Armenia’s conflict with Azerbaijan first began. “Right now, 80 percent of Armenia’s borders are closed,” Mkrtchyan said, adding that talks to reopen the Turkish border continue in parallel with the Azerbaijan negotiations, “and there is a lot of hope and a lot of potential.”

Then there is Armenia’s 27-mile border with Iran that the TRIPP route runs uncomfortably close to. Iran has bristled at the prospect of having a U.S.-led initiative so close to its territory while Tehran and Washington remain at war, and a senior advisor to Iran’s supreme leader warned this week that “if Trump and his allies want to cross this Iranian red line, we will break their legs.” Armenia’s entire railway system is also still controlled by Russia—a legacy of the erstwhile Soviet Union that both countries were a part of—and the once-tight relationship between Yerevan and Moscow has steadily deteriorated thanks to Russia’s war with Ukraine and a perceived lack of Russian support over Nagorno-Karabakh.

But those conflicts have only spurred Armenia to diversify from its neighbors and traditional partners by aligning more closely with Washington, according to Mkrtchyan. “This is a new reality, and we live in this new reality, but this is our strategic choice,” he said. “But we were very transparent and open to our colleagues in the region—we told them that this is not against you. It’s not against Iran, it’s not against Russia. It’s first of all for Armenia’s national interests, for Armenia’s security, and for regional cooperation,” he added. “. This is a unique opportunity to transform the region.”

Armenia is also hoping to parlay its newfound U.S. links into becoming a technology powerhouse. Earlier this year, a San Francisco company called Firebird established a new data center in Armenia in partnership with fellow U.S. firms Nvidia and Dell, in a deal that will be powered by 70,000 of Nvidia’s advanced semiconductor chips by the end of 2027. “We want to become a key partner of the U.S. in providing sustainable supply chains for future AI systems,” Mkrtchyan said. That ambition is further bolstered by nuclear cooperation and critical minerals agreements signed by Vance and Rubio during their respective visits this year.

“Especially after the peace deal, it gave a strong signal to the American companies that right now is the moment to consider Armenia as a platform for cooperation and investment,” Mkrtchyan said.

Given that our conversation took place just days after a resounding defeat for Trump’s Republican Party in the U.S. midterm election, officially consigning the U.S. president to lame-duck status for the remaining two years of his term, I asked Mkrtchyan how Armenia is thinking about its relationship with Washington heading into a post-Trump world.

“It’s very natural that at some point, President Trump and the Trump administration will not be in power. It is also natural that in the coming 50 years, only Republicans or President Trump will not be ruling the government, because our agreement with the United States of America—the TRIPP agreement—is for 50 years,” he said, adding that historically Armenia has had “bipartisan friends” in Washington.

“Of course, President Trump will always be remembered as someone who brokered this peace deal and the Armenian nation is very thankful to him,” Mkrtchyan added. “But we are building our relationships with the United States of America institutionally.”

Rishi Iyengar is a staff writer at Foreign Policy. Bluesky: @iyengarish.bsky.social X: @Iyengarish Instagram: @iyengar.rishi
