#5725. The Paradox of Iranian Power

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

Why the Islamic Republic Can’t Make Peace

Iran’s theocratic rulers have drawn a Nietzschean lesson from nearly half a century of crises: what has not killed their revolution has only made it stronger. In 2026, the Islamic Republic survived a nationwide uprising and a devastating war with the United States and Israel that eliminated its leaders, decimated its military, and ravaged its economy. Yet rather than reconsider the principles and policies that have stoked nearly five decades of confrontation, isolation, and popular unrest, Tehran’s rulers drew the opposite conclusion: revolutionary ideology was not an albatross weighing the regime down, but a life preserver keeping it afloat.

They are not entirely misguided in that belief. Since the 1979 revolution transformed Iran from a pro-American monarchy into an anti-American theocracy, the regime’s organizing principles have been the imposition of theocratic rule at home and resistance against Israel and the United States abroad. These policies have fueled recurring internal unrest and external conflicts that have impoverished, isolated, and destabilized Iran. Paradoxically, however, revolutionary regimes that survive such challenges often emerge more resilient, cohesive, and confident in their ideological convictions. The Islamic Republic, under the new leadership of Mojtaba Khamenei, appears to be following the same pattern.

The joint U.S.-Israeli war on Iran did not meaningfully rally an impoverished and abused Iranian society around its oppressive regime. It did, however, rally the regime around the revolution. Rather than embrace the possibility of a different political order, Tehran’s rulers closed ranks and extended the Khamenei era—literally, by anointing Mojtaba Khamenei to succeed his father, the slain supreme leader Ayatollah Ali Khamenei. “God’s grace is clear,” supporters chanted, “A young Khamenei is here!”

At home, the war entrenched a revolutionary elite whose priority is resistance, not governance. That will only prolong the country’s crisis. The regime can suppress revolts but cannot address the conditions that produce them, leaving Iran trapped in a downward spiral of despotism, decline, and despair.

U.S. President Donald Trump’s attempt to resolve the Iranian challenge instead exacerbated it. The war, launched in the midst of negotiations, has degraded Tehran’s military capabilities but hardened its revolutionary character, deepened its already profound mistrust of Washington, and taught its rulers a dangerous lesson: violence, not dialogue, secures concessions. Tehran has established new precedents for attacking its neighbors, disrupting global energy supplies, and asserting control over the Strait of Hormuz. Its leaders are determined to rebuild their military capabilities and avenge Khamenei’s death, while Israel is equally determined to prevent Iran from restoring the capacity to threaten it. What Tehran may regard as necessary to rebuild its deterrence, Israel may regard as preparation for another war.

Washington can choose to end its war with Iran, but it cannot end the Islamic Republic’s confrontation with the United States. Israel cannot afford to ignore an adversary it regards as an existential threat. Gulf Arab states may have little say in whether Israel and Iran fight another war, but they could bear some of its greatest costs as their cities, energy infrastructure, and shipping through the Strait of Hormuz are vulnerable to Iranian retaliation. Tehran regards the Gulf’s economic and technological achievements not as examples to emulate, but as vulnerabilities to exploit and targets to threaten. For these reasons, the end of this war is likely to prove an intermission rather than a resolution.

The challenges the Islamic Republic poses to the Middle East, the United States, and the global economy can ultimately be resolved only with political change in Tehran. Yet getting through 2026 has simply reinforced the Islamic Republic’s revolutionary conviction that its survival is best ensured through continuity, not change. The country will remain suspended between war and peace abroad, and between prolonged decay and sudden upheaval at home. Iran is thus left in a paradox. Revolutionary ideology has led the country toward destruction. But abandoning revolutionary ideology could lead the regime toward collapse.

Like all ailing autocracies, the Islamic Republic has the potential to both gradually atrophy or abruptly implode. Russian Tsar Nicholas I first referred to the Ottoman Empire as “the sick man” of Europe in 1853; its end came seven decades later. George Kennan wrote in these pages in 1947 that the Soviet Union “bears within it the seeds of its own decay, and that the sprouting of these seeds is well advanced.” It took another 44 years for the Soviet Union to collapse.

The Islamic Republic of Iran has come to resemble the Leaning Tower of Pisa: seemingly on the verge of collapse but defying political gravity decade after decade. Despite its abundant natural resources and human capital, theocratic Iran has been a case study in squandered national potential. Its economy is trapped in recurring crises, its currency is ravaged by inflation, and generations of educated citizens seek their futures abroad. Nearly half a century after the revolution, Iran’s real GDP per capita remains nearly a quarter below its 1976 peak. That means that many Iranians are materially worse off than their parents and grandparents were before the revolution, an economic regression typically suffered only by failed states or societies ravaged by civil war.

While Iran’s economy has regressed, its society has progressed. Over the last five decades, its population has nearly tripled, and a predominantly rural and traditional society has become increasingly urban, educated, and socially liberal. Since the 2022–23 “Woman, Life, Freedom” protests, mandatory veiling—what Ayatollah Ruhollah Khomeini once called the “flag” of the Islamic Revolution—appears increasingly unenforceable. Women now routinely walk down the streets of Tehran and other cities with their hair uncovered. The breaking of this taboo will embolden Iranians to break others. In short, Iran’s citizens look forward, Iran’s rulers look backward, and Iran’s economy spirals downward. It is a country whose people have moved beyond the revolution, but whose rulers refuse to leave it behind.

The historian Crane Brinton compared revolutions to fevers that eventually break, ushering in a period of moderation known as Thermidor. Yet many decades after the revolution, Iran’s rulers still refuse to lower the temperature. Iranian society has long sought a return to normalcy, but the regime fears that ending the revolution would mean ending its own rule. The growing gap between rulers and the ruled can be sustained only through continued brutality. The Islamic Republic’s aspiring Deng Xiaopings were sidelined for fear they might become the regime’s Mikhail Gorbachevs. Its Vaclav Havels have been imprisoned, exiled, or killed. Edmund Burke warned that “a state without the means of some change is without the means of its conservation.” Yet the Islamic Republic’s rulers appear more willing to risk breaking the regime than to allow themselves to bend, and more willing to risk another calamitous war than to abandon the revolutionary principles that have sustained their power.

Revolutionary regimes are surprisingly resilient. As the political scientists Steven Levitsky and Lucan Way write in Revolution and Dictatorship, such regimes tend to last nearly three times as long as their nonrevolutionary counterparts, despite being twice as likely to be involved in wars. Although their radicalism often triggers counterrevolution or external war, regimes that survive those early challenges commonly develop three pillars of durability: “a cohesive ruling elite,” “a highly developed and loyal coercive apparatus,” and “the destruction of rival organizations and alternative centers of power in society.”

The Islamic Republic has developed these three pillars because of, not in spite of, decades of internal opposition and near-constant hot and cold wars. The 444-day seizure of the U.S. embassy in Tehran beginning in 1979 and the eight-year war with Saddam Hussein’s Iraq, which began the following year, were foundational crises that brought international isolation and economic devastation to Iran but helped entrench Khomeini’s theocracy. Both were prolonged well beyond their initial objectives, in part because they served the regime’s internal consolidation: the hostage crisis marginalized moderates, while the exigencies of the war with Iraq smothered internal dissent and helped build the military-industrial system—namely the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC)—to entrench theocratic government. Just as the Long March forged the bonds and credentials of China’s revolutionary elite in the 1930s, the Iran-Iraq War of the 1980s forged those of the Revolutionary Guard commanders who rule Iran today.

Whereas most states seek normalcy and the resolution of crises, crises in the Islamic Republic provided fertile soil for the revolution. As Abolhassan Bani-Sadr, the Islamic Republic’s first president, observed in 2003, after more than two decades in exile: “With the end of the war, crisis-making did not end. The mullahs’ regime, which was born of crisis-making, could not continue its life without making crises.” The Islamic Republic has made disequilibrium its equilibrium. Indeed, some within the regime privately believe that IRGC attacks on shipping during this year’s cease-fire, as well as the maximalist demands of Iranian negotiators, were meant to prolong the war and entrench the power of hard-liners, much as the Iran-Iraq War helped cement their position.

By attacking Iran weeks after the regime killed thousands of protesters, one of the bloodiest massacres in modern Iranian history, Trump provided Tehran with an external crisis to divert attention from its internal one. Imagine if the United States had attacked the Soviet Union in 1982, as its economy stagnated, its ideology languished, and its aging rulers struggled to preserve a discredited political order. Rather than hastening communism’s demise, such an attack might have revitalized its defenders, discredited its critics, and transformed a crisis of legitimacy into a struggle for national survival. Something similar seems to have happened in Iran. The war allowed Tehran’s rulers to portray their decades of hostility toward the United States not as the source of Iran’s predicament, but as vindication of their worldview. It is telling that while Israeli and U.S. forces have killed many of the regime’s top political and military leaders, no high-level officials have defected.

No one in today’s Islamic Republic has the stature to push for reform or rapprochement with Washington; its many veto men, whose privileges depend on the status quo, have the power to block both. Profound change requires consensus. Inertia can be entrenched by a few.

A great deal depends on the largely invisible Mojtaba Khamenei. It remains to be seen for how long the Islamic Republic’s political and military elite will defer to a supreme leader who has, for whatever reason, withdrawn from public view. Iranian political culture tends toward personality cults, and over much of the last century the country has largely been led by four strongmen: Reza Shah (1925 to 1941), Mohammad Reza Shah Pahlavi (1941 to 1979), Khomeini (1979 to 1989), and Ali Khamenei (1989 to 2026). Few major countries have experienced such extraordinary continuity of personal rule across radically different political systems. The present situation—an absent supreme leader hidden from public view, possibly for health reasons or out of fear of assassination, and competing centers of power within the security establishment—therefore looks like an unstable equilibrium.

After Khomeini came to power, in 1979, outside observers commonly assumed Iran’s revolutionary fervor would fade and that the country’s new leaders could be won over with the right mix of respect and economic inducements. “Khomeini will be somewhat of a saint when we get over the panic,” Andrew Young, a UN ambassador in the Carter administration, predicted in February 1979. “In two years, our relations with Iran will be on a pretty even keel.” But today, the regime still chants “Death to America.” Governments do not cling to policies that they believe endanger their survival. The enduring mistake has been to assume that Iran’s leaders cannot possibly mean what they repeatedly say.

The Islamic Republic has always framed its conflict with the United States not merely as a geopolitical dispute but also as a moral and ideological struggle. Khomeini, who outlined his vision of Islamic government nearly a decade before the revolution and implemented it after seizing power, called the United States “the foremost enemy of Islam” in his last will and testament. His successor, Ali Khamenei, maintained remarkable ideological consistency over nearly four decades, repeatedly describing the rivalry as an “inherent conflict—an inherent incompatibility.” For both men, their publicly articulated convictions proved a far more reliable guide to the Islamic Republic’s conduct than the reassurances of its diplomats or the wishful interpretations of Western observers.

Khamenei’s son and successor Mojtaba—whose face and voice have not been seen or heard since he became supreme leader in March 2026—has echoed his father in his public pronouncements. “The criminal rulers of America and the illegitimate Zionist regime,” he said in one statement, “are not merely enemies of the Islamic Republic, Iran, or the vast front of Islamic resistance; they harbor enmity toward the entire Islamic ummah”—the Muslim community. After the Democratic victories in the U.S. midterm elections in November, a post on Mojtaba Khamenei’s official English-language X account proclaimed that “the process of the US’s collapse has accelerated.” He further warned that neither American elections nor Iranian concessions would alter the “hegemonic system” of the United States and its hostility toward the Islamic Republic.

The elder Khamenei’s assassination at the start of the war continued the transformation of Iran from a country led by clerics into one dominated by current and former IRGC officers. Many observers assumed this shift from turbans to uniforms would move the regime’s focus from religious ideology to national interest. This has yet to happen. Although the regime has adopted more overtly nationalist rhetoric, it still subordinates the welfare and security of Iranians to the goals of the revolution. The motivations of security officials may be more financial than theological, but their interests have remained bound to the same revolutionary order.

Siamak Namazi, an Iranian American who spent nearly eight years as a hostage of the IRGC in Tehran’s Evin prison, describes the Islamic Republic as a “competitive kleptocracy masquerading as a theocracy.” Many of the regime’s elite, he has observed, are driven less by religious dogma than by the spoils of power: preferential exchange rates, privileged loans, import licenses, and a cut of the smuggling trade. “The revolutionary Kool-Aid,” he said, “is reserved for the ranks, because someone still has to believe enough to open fire on their own countrymen.” The true believers also have a great deal to lose. A January 2026 Bloomberg report alleged that Mojtaba Khamenei had used a middleman to build a financial empire in the West, including $138 million in British luxury real estate and Swiss bank accounts.

The IRGC has eclipsed the clergy in both political and economic power, with some estimates suggesting they control more than a third of Iran’s economy. Individual commanders have accumulated fortunes, often by enabling the business activities of their children, known as aghazadehs, or princelings. Yet rather than challenge the supreme leader, the senior command’s public statements suggest continued solidarity with the office. Mohsen Rezaei, a longtime IRGC commander with substantial family business interests who returned to serve as secretary of the Supreme National Security Council, expressed his devotion to Mojtaba in a September 2026 interview with state television. “Let us preserve our unity,” he insisted. “Let us preserve our dignity. Let us march behind the leader of the revolution.”

In a 26-page open letter to the American people published the same month, the IRGC declared that the war had “reinvigorated the Islamic Republic” and would ensure the regime’s survival for decades. Rather than question the ideology that had brought Iran to this precipice, the corps reaffirmed velayat-e faqih, the doctrine of clerical guardianship, as an “embodiment of all godly virtues in the political sphere” that empowered Iran to stand firm against diabolical powers. Revolutionary and religious dogma were not the source of Iran’s predicament, they concluded, but rather the “main factors underpinning Iran’s victory.”

Iran’s rulers are neither pragmatic nationalists guided by traditional state interests nor an apocalyptic death cult. They outsource the politics of martyrdom to regional militias while ruthlessly practicing the politics of survival at home. For the true believers, abandoning the revolution would mean betraying their convictions; for its profiteers, it would mean jeopardizing their power and privileges. When the regime’s survival is at stake, they may change their tactics, but they won’t change their worldview.

The war has left Tehran both weaker and much more aggressive. Before the war, the Islamic Republic resembled a zombie regime: its ideology, legitimacy, and its 86-year-old supreme leader all appeared to be fading. Since the outbreak of the war, it has come to resemble a gangster regime—one that practices extortion as statecraft—and is emboldened to attack its neighbors, the global economy, and the United States to extract concessions.

Tehran has been rewarded for its defiance by Trump. In his dramatic address launching the war in February 2026, Trump vowed to end Iran’s nuclear weapons ambitions, destroy its missile capabilities, and neutralize its regional proxies. He encouraged Iranians to overthrow their “wicked” dictatorship. But the president soon changed his tune. Although Trump believed the war would be a swift “little excursion,” Iran’s attacks on tankers in the Strait of Hormuz and its over 6,700 missile and drone attacks on its Gulf neighbors compelled him to attempt one of the most striking reversals in modern American foreign policy.

In June, Trump signed a memorandum of understanding with Tehran that gave away the store. He pledged to lift all sanctions as part of a final agreement, release frozen Iranian assets, permit Iranian oil exports, and work with regional partners on a $300 billion reconstruction package. The agreement also accepted an Iranian role in determining the future administration of the Strait of Hormuz, committed Washington to noninterference in Iran’s internal affairs, and made no mention of Iran’s missiles or regional proxies.

In just four months, Trump had gone from pursuing the most hawkish Iran policy ever to offering the most generous diplomatic terms since the 1979 revolution. A policy of regime change became one of accommodation. Although the memorandum of understanding was short-lived—each side blames the other for abrogating it—the lesson Tehran learned was unmistakable: restraint projects weakness, whereas aggression is rewarded.

“We seize concessions not through dialogue, but with missiles,” declared Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf, a former IRGC commander, the current speaker of the parliament, and one of the regime’s most powerful figures. “The winner of any agreement,” he added, “is the one who is better prepared for war from the day after.” Tehran is already signaling that its chief postwar priority will not be to rebuild its decimated economy, but to restore—and expand—its ability to impose costs on its adversaries.

The Strait of Hormuz offers the clearest example of this dynamic. What was once an Iranian threat has become, in Tehran’s eyes, an entitlement: the ability to regulate passage through one of the world’s most consequential energy chokepoints. For Iran, this constitutes both economic leverage and a potential deterrent against future attacks on itself and its regional proxies. For the rest of the world, it creates the prospect that Tehran can hold global energy markets hostage whenever it chooses.

Before the war, the Iranian regime’s longtime ambitions to drive the United States from the Middle East and end Israel’s existence appeared fanciful. Today, the growing number of American politicians and commentators who have called for the United States to end its aid to Israel and wind down its regional military presence has emboldened Tehran to believe it is winning the ideological war. “POTUS clearly recognizes that the vast majority of Americans are disgusted with Israel,” Iranian Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi said in a post on social media in November. He continued: “Americans are evidently sick and tired of Israel bamboozling US administrations into Forever Wars. They had enough. So has our region.”

The Islamic Republic’s newfound confidence is partly rooted in its ability to inflict enormous damage at comparatively little cost. Its Persian Gulf neighbors have spent decades and trillions of dollars transforming themselves into global hubs of energy, transportation, finance, and technology. And Iran can now threaten all of it with cheap Shahed drones against which the United States and its partners must field multimillion-dollar interceptors—a costly undertaking that Nick Carter, a retired British general and former chief of the British defense staff, likened to “Ferraris being thrown at Frisbees.” Building is costly and slow; destruction is fast and cheap. Rezaei has pledged to target undersea cables, data centers, and artificial intelligence facilities across the Gulf in the event of renewed fighting.

The Islamic Republic’s theory of victory does not require defeating the United States militarily. It only requires persuading two audiences—the American public and Iran’s Gulf neighbors—that the U.S. military presence in the region and the partnership with Israel bring more harm than good. The attacks on American bases, Gulf data centers, and ships passing through the Strait of Hormuz are a message that the road to regional security must go through Iran, not the United States or Israel. “The nations and territories of the region will no longer serve as a shield for American bases,” Mojtaba Khamenei has declared.

But this Iranian aggression is likely to produce the opposite of what it intends. Tehran’s threats against the Gulf only stoke the anxieties that sustain Washington’s military presence in the region. The result is a self-perpetuating cycle in which Gulf governments seek American protection against Iran, Tehran attempts to evict Washington from the region, and each new round of conflict deepens the Gulf states’ dependence on the United States.

Iran’s confrontation with Israel poses a different, potentially more explosive danger. The regime’s hostility to Israel has survived the destruction of much of Tehran’s military infrastructure and its regional proxy network. Iranian leaders remain determined to rebuild their missile and drone arsenals, restore the capabilities of allies such as Hezbollah, and avenge the killing of Ali Khamenei. Yet Israel is equally determined to prevent Iran from reconstituting the capabilities that threatened it before the war. Each side may therefore interpret the other’s attempts at deterrence as preparations for another attack.

As for Trump’s original justification for the war—to avert an Iranian nuclear bomb—his actions and words have persuaded a growing number of Iranian officials to openly advocate nuclear arms development. The president himself supplied Tehran’s talking points. “If they had a nuclear weapon,” Trump told a crowd of supporters six months into the war, “I’d be calling the supreme leader and I’d be saying: ‘Mr. Supreme Leader, how are you, sir? Is there anything we can do for you?’—as opposed to bombing the crap out of him.”

Since the 1979 revolution, Washington has tended to underestimate the Islamic Republic’s agency and overestimate its own ability to shape Iran’s future. Advocates of engagement often blame American hostility for Tehran’s intransigence, while advocates of confrontation have blamed American weakness. One side has overestimated the Islamic Republic’s flexibility; the other, its fragility. Both have been susceptible to what the former U.S. national security adviser H. R. McMaster has called “strategic narcissism”: the assumption that adversaries are primarily reacting to the United States rather than acting on their own interests, identities, and ambitions.

Trump’s war exposed the limits of trying both to bomb Iran into submission and to bribe it into moderation. In the span of four months, Washington employed overwhelming military force to weaken or overthrow the Islamic Republic, then offered sweeping economic and diplomatic incentives to alter its behavior. Neither altered a revolutionary stance that has been remarkably consistent for decades. As Henry Kissinger wrote in 1957, “The distinguishing feature of a revolutionary power is not that it feels threatened . . . but that nothing can reassure it.”

The seeds of the next war have already been planted. Regardless of what the United States chooses to do, the Islamic Republic’s renewed confidence, along with its resolve to rebuild its military might and retaliate for the death of its supreme leader, portends another showdown with Israel. Each side now views the other as an existential threat, and there is no direct channel of dialogue to prevent miscalculation and a potentially even more destructive war. For Washington, the implication is clear: The United States may seek to disengage from the Middle East, but it cannot insulate itself from the consequences of another war between Iran and Israel.

The Islamic Republic’s most consequential compromises came in 1988, when Khomeini reluctantly accepted a cease-fire ending the eight-year war with Iraq, and in 2015, when Iran agreed to stringent restrictions on its nuclear program. Although separated by nearly three decades, both decisions followed a similar logic. Faced with overwhelming pressure, international isolation, and a diplomatic off-ramp that does not require it to change its identity, the Islamic Republic has demonstrated a capacity to make concessions—but only in the service of living to fight another day.

In a 2013 speech foreshadowing Tehran’s nuclear compromise, Ali Khamenei described this approach as “heroic flexibility.” The supreme leader likened the regime to a wrestler who “shows flexibility for technical reasons” but must never “forget who his opponent is and what he is doing.” Tactical flexibility, Khamenei insisted, must never come at the expense of Iran’s “revolutionary nature”: “The fact that the world has changed cannot give us an excuse to change our behavior, our ideals, and our goals.” Ali Khamenei had the legitimacy to push for heroic flexibility. The current regime is filled with men who will pursue escalation long before they ever call for compromise.

Today, the challenges posed by Iran are much greater than a decade ago. Washington must not only prevent it from acquiring nuclear weapons but also deter it from attacking its neighbors, disrupting the global energy trade through the Strait of Hormuz, and waging AI-assisted cyberwarfare and influence operations against the United States and its partners. At the same time, Washington has fewer allies and less diplomatic leverage than it did in 2015, and the trust gap between the two governments may prove unbridgeable. The Iranian regime believes that global public opinion is on its side, not Trump’s.

The 48-year history of U.S. policy toward the Islamic Republic shows that grand designs, whether for regime change or rapprochement, have failed. On two occasions, in 1988 and 2015, both sides reached narrow agreements that addressed Tehran’s conduct rather than its character. Lasting change, however, can come only from within Iran, when the pillars that sustain the Islamic Republic—a cohesive elite, a loyal coercive apparatus, and the absence of rival centers of power—crack. Rather than widening those cracks, Trump’s war may have narrowed them.

Iran’s rulers see the regime’s survival as proof of their own strength. Yet each crisis they weather leaves the country poorer, its people more estranged, and the regime more dependent on brutality to endure. Like Washington before it, Tehran now overestimates what force can achieve; its hubris may help produce the next war, just as the repression that quelled the last uprising will invariably fuel another. The revolutionary ideology that keeps the regime afloat will continue to weigh Iran down, leaving in place a leadership even more intent on resisting its adversaries, at home and abroad, than on governing its people. The regime may declare victory, but for the proud people of Iran—an ancient civilization and an energy superpower with a worthless currency, a shunned passport, and a leader in hiding—there is little reason to celebrate. What has not killed the Islamic Republic is slowly killing Iran.

KARIM SADJADPOUR is a Senior Fellow at the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace.

#5724. Trump pauses turkey pardon to salute US troops guarding Strait of Hormuz: 'Watch over and protect'

President Donald Trump thanked U.S. service members enforcing the naval blockade in the Strait of Hormuz during Tuesday’s annual White House Thanksgiving turkey pardoning ceremony, asserting no Iranian ships have gotten through and vowing Tehran will never obtain a nuclear weapon.

President Donald Trump paused Tuesday's annual White House Thanksgiving turkey pardoning ceremony to offer a special tribute and thanks to U.S. service members defending the Strait of Hormuz and enforcing a naval blockade against Iran.

Speaking from the White House South Lawn during the 79th National Thanksgiving Turkey presentation, Trump thanked American troops deployed overseas and praised those working to prevent Tehran from acquiring a nuclear weapon.

"We want to say a special thank you to them, the people that are guarding the strait know or the blockade we call it," Trump said. "It’s a blockade, the greatest naval blockade in history."

TRUMP DARES IRAN TO BREACH US PORT BLOCKADE AS TEHRAN REPORTEDLY READIES 14 OIL TANKERS

President Donald Trump speaks prior to pardoning the National Thanksgiving Turkey during a ceremony on the South Lawn of the White House in Washington, D.C., on Tuesday. (Bonnie Cash/UPI/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

Trump asserted that the U.S. maritime cordon has stopped every Iranian vessel from passing through since its establishment, save for a brief pause earlier in the standoff.

"Not one Iranian ship has gotten through to the blockade since we established it, other than we had a little moratorium. And we learned that you can’t trust them," Trump said. "But not one ship from Iran has gotten through. Our naval blockade is the most incredible naval blockade, probably in the history of the world. It’s amazing."

IRANIAN ‘GHOST FLEET’ FILMED EVADING US BLOCKADE EN ROUTE TO CHINA, UANI SAYS

President Donald Trump grants a presidential pardon to Freedom during the 79th annual National Thanksgiving Turkey Presentation on the South Lawn of the White House in Washington, D.C., on Tuesday. (Finn Gomez/Getty Images)

Trump also contended that U.S. forces have severely degraded Iran's conventional military capabilities and restored oil shipments through the Strait of Hormuz to levels higher than before the conflict began.

The U.S. naval blockade targeting vessels entering and leaving Iranian ports began in April amid the Strait of Hormuz crisis and resumed in July following a temporary pause.

Before returning to the holiday ceremony, Trump offered a Thanksgiving prayer for deployed U.S. troops and expressed pride in the country.

President Donald Trump, center, pardons Freedom the turkey as, from left, Deans Gap Farm co-owner Scott Rightsell, National Turkey Federation chairwoman Christy Puffenbarger, first lady Melania Trump and House Speaker Mike Johnson, R-La., look on during the 79th annual National Thanksgiving Turkey Presentation on the South Lawn of the White House. (Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images)

"And we send them all our best wishes, Thanksgiving, and is very special. It’s a very special time for me for a lot of reasons," Trump said. "And I ask God to watch over and protect all of our military and all of our people. I’m so proud of our people, and I’m so proud to be your president because we’ve never had a time like this."

Following his remarks, Trump pardoned this year's National Thanksgiving Turkeys, Freedom and Feathers — though Feathers briefly ran into the bushes during the presentation — before sending them to retire at Texas A&M University.

Brittany Miller is a Fox News Digital breaking news writer and former Independent reporter who graduated from Syracuse University in 2023.

#5722. The Real Nvidia Scandal

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

Emily Kilcrease is Senior Fellow and Director of the Energy, Economics, and Security Program at the Center for a New American Security. From 2019 to 2021, first in the Trump administration and then in the Biden administration, she was Deputy Assistant U.S. Trade Representative for Investment.

Vivek Chilukuri is Senior Fellow and Director of the Technology and National Security Program at the Center for a New American Security. He previously served as Legislative Director to Senator Michael Bennet and as a policy adviser at the U.S. Department of State.

How China Exploited a Massive Loophole in U.S. National Security—and How Congress Can Close It

The reinstatement of Jensen Huang as CEO of Nvidia this week added another twist to a scandal that has transfixed Washington and Silicon Valley. The stakes go beyond the swings of the stock market or the salacious details about Huang’s affairs, including with Alice Huang, a former executive at the Singaporean firm Megaspeed and a potential Chinese asset. At its core, the scandal has exposed something that matters far more than any one man or company: American export-control policy remains riddled with loopholes, hobbled by incapacity and infighting, and divorced from clear strategy. Unless Washington fixes this, it risks imperiling the United States’ hard-won advantage in artificial intelligence.

This export policy failure is more than the sum of specific deficiencies. It stems from the Trump administration’s broader posture on China. Washington has embraced unfettered AI acceleration at the same time as it has struck a more accommodating stance toward Beijing. That combination set the stage for the explosive Megaspeed scandal—and perhaps others that have yet to emerge. As a result, China has been able to access an alarming number of advanced chips that have enabled it to narrow the AI gap with the United States and power its AI-driven military modernization. According to the AI research institute Epoch AI, up to a third of China’s AI computing power may come from smuggled chips. China’s top AI models are now just months behind the U.S. frontier. The narrow gap reflects China’s genuine innovation and access to abundant energy resources as well as its relentless efforts to circumvent U.S. controls.

Huang’s return to Nvidia at the behest of President Donald Trump suggests that both the company and the White House seem utterly uninterested in keeping powerful AI chips from China. It will instead fall to Congress to ensure that the United States leverages its compute advantage and maintains its AI edge. The midterm elections and mounting bipartisan concerns over the Megaspeed debacle give Congress new momentum to correct course. Its near-term agenda should include closing known loopholes that played a key role in allowing Megaspeed to supply advanced chips to China, stepping up enforcement of existing controls, and strengthening oversight of the administration’s export-control decisions.

Over the medium term, Congress must also tackle the broader question of how to balance the strategic benefits of exporting U.S. technology to build the world’s AI infrastructure with robust controls to prevent its leakage to China. The long-standing debate over which American AI chips and capabilities Washington should permit to diffuse globally and under what conditions has finally boiled over into the mainstream—and, in a perverse way, the national security community can thank Alice Huang for forcing the issue.

The issue before Congress, however, is much broader than addressing the specifics of the Nvidia scandal. Congress must also contend with the underlying impulses driving the administration’s China policy and install guardrails to avoid the erosion of U.S. export controls. This means that after Congress passes near-term bills to mop up the Megaspeed mess, it must then take the reins on setting long-term export-control strategy.

Congress cannot stand by idly as AI blazes forward. OpenAI’s top model can now perform professional tasks as well as or better than humans 85 percent of the time, up from just 14 percent in 2024. The time an AI model can independently work on a task is doubling every four months. Last week, a researcher at Anthropic announced that AI helped him untangle the Riemann hypothesis, one of the most famous problems in pure mathematics.

As capabilities have grown, so have risks. Frontier models can now complete simulated cyberattacks on networks on their own, and hackers are using AI models to partially automate their attacks. In the July incident involving the AI platform Hugging Face, rogue AI agents escaped their testing environment and plotted to evade human oversight, underscoring the difficulty of aligning model behavior with human intent. The growing power of AI and the risks from misuse or loss of control make U.S. leadership a national security imperative.

At its core, the U.S.-Chinese AI competition boils down to which country can deploy the best chips in the highest quantities to train and adopt advanced AI models. The Biden administration’s controls slowed Chinese AI developers by leaving them with fewer, worse chips. Nvidia’s top Blackwell chips are nearly seven times better than Huawei’s Ascend equivalents. By the end of 2026, Huawei will likely have produced less than four percent of the compute that Nvidia will have over the same time. As the founder and CEO of DeepSeek, one of China’s top AI companies, admitted in 2024, “Our problem has never been money—it’s the embargo on high-end chips.”

To circumvent Biden-era restrictions, elaborate networks for smuggling and transshipment emerged to divert chips to China from other countries that could legally acquire them, and Chinese companies leveraged export-control loopholes to remotely access U.S.-powered compute through data centers abroad. Chinese firms funded companies such as Megaspeed to facilitate these workarounds. Backed by the Chinese tech giant Alibaba, Megaspeed has purchased $4 billion worth of Nvidia chips since 2023, prompting an investigation from the U.S. government. Chinese companies also used a technique known as adversarial distillation—training their models on the outputs of top U.S. models at a fraction of the compute. All of this needlessly shrank the United States’ AI lead over China, even after the imposition of export controls.

These developments should have spurred the Trump administration to better resource, enforce, and update the export controls when it came into office. It did the opposite. In May 2025, the administration suspended the Biden administration’s AI Diffusion Rule, which had established rules for which countries could access advanced chips and in what quantities. Then, after initially prohibiting sales to China of Nvidia’s H20 chip, which was custom-built as a less powerful product for the Chinese market, the Trump administration reversed course in December 2025 and greenlighted the sale of Nvidia’s H200 chip. Although the H200 is two generations behind top U.S. chips, it remains superior to anything China can produce. An investigation from the House Foreign Affairs Committee in November revealed that between December 2025 and May 2026, the Trump administration approved the sale of more than 800,000 of Nvidia’s H200 chips and AMD’s MI325X equivalent, including to companies that the Pentagon has designated as linked to the Chinese military. This week, under pressure from Congress, the administration added Megaspeed to the entity list, which blocks those listed from buying U.S. products without a license—but before that, the administration had not added a single new individual or business to the list in 2026.

This regulatory vacuum and policy incoherence created the perfect conditions for Megaspeed. The Singaporean firm at the center of the Nvidia scandal seems tailor-made to operate in the seams of U.S. export controls. In 2024, the Commerce Department initiated a formal investigation of the company, which could have led to sanctions such as being placed on the entity list. But the Trump administration dragged its feet in pursuing the investigation. In the meantime, Megaspeed stocked Southeast Asian data centers with thousands of U.S. chips, enabling remote access for Chinese firms. This remote access loophole in U.S. export controls was a well-known and increasingly popular workaround for China-linked firms, but the Trump administration left it open for nearly two years and closed it only last week amid pressure from the Nvidia scandal. Regardless of the Megaspeed specifics, the net result of the Trump administration’s actions and inaction is that China has enjoyed access to far more computing power than it should have during a vital period of the AI competition.

The administration’s long-awaited closure of the remote access loophole is a welcome step. The rule requires foreign data centers to secure a license before offering significant computing power to China-based customers. It also requires a license to export advanced chips to places that could facilitate remote access and chip diversion, including Indonesia, Malaysia, and Singapore. This rule will make it harder for firms such as Megaspeed to import chips in the first place, or to use them to serve China-based customers. But the rule will be difficult to enforce because the U.S. Commerce Department does not have the legal authority under export controls to directly regulate services such as cloud computing. More important, the administration’s lax enforcement of past controls—and internal divisions over the new ones—raise serious questions about its willingness to follow through. Huang’s reinstatement as CEO, with Trump’s backing, will make it harder to vigorously enforce these new controls and move toward a tougher posture on China.

Since Congress cannot rely on the administration to fix export controls and protect the United States’ AI lead, lawmakers should urgently advance bipartisan measures to address the underlying conditions that gave rise to entities such as Megaspeed. As a starting point, Congress should pass the Remote Access Security Act, which would give the administration clear legal authority for controlling remote access to data centers powered by U.S. compute. While implementation will require close congressional oversight, getting the right legal structure in place is an important first step. Next, it should work with industry and technical experts to fund and fast-track “on-chip” technologies—basically, features that can verify a chip’s location and counter smuggling and diversion. Beyond sorting out the technical feasibilities, Congress will need to work with industry to understand the implications of on-chip security mechanisms for global competitiveness and the potential surveillance risks should China develop similar controls since it produces much of the world’s legacy chips.

Along with these technical fixes, Congress should seize on the Megaspeed scandal to strengthen oversight. The AI Overwatch Act, which may be included in the annual defense bill, would codify existing export controls on advanced chips into law, preventing the current or a future administration from reversing them. It would also empower Congress to review and block advanced chips sales to U.S. adversaries. Congress can go even further by establishing a new investigative committee or inspector general to strengthen oversight of sensitive chip export-control policies. Together, these actions would go a long way toward combating the circumvention that let firms such as Megaspeed thrive.

At the same time, closing loopholes and codifying rules is insufficient to fix the underlying issues that led to the Megaspeed scandal in the first place. This mess is directly downstream from Trump’s dovish shift on China that has seen the administration take a softer line on Beijing just as the AI race accelerated. The administration’s own overreach forced Washington into a conciliatory posture. Administration officials failed to realize that China had learned its lesson from the first Trump administration’s trade wars and was poised to retaliate hard in the second term. After China responded to Trump’s “Liberation Day” tariffs and export controls with its own punishing controls on rare earths, the Trump administration found its way into a vaguely defined and fragile truce. This has severely constrained U.S. policymaking, including related to compute controls.

Indeed, Trump became an easy sell for advocates of aggressive AI promotion such as Jensen Huang. This camp argues that U.S. leadership in the global AI race requires pushing American AI technologies to as many parts of the globe as possible—including China. They claim that keeping China “addicted” to the U.S. AI stack gives Washington leverage and outweighs the national security concerns that could come with helping China improve its AI capabilities. Huang even pushed demonstrably false narratives that China’s military was not interested in Nvidia’s chips and labeled his critics as “unpatriotic” for not wanting U.S. chips spread as far and wide as possible and therefore depriving American tech firms of global market share.

Trump, who was interested in stabilizing the relationship with China and prioritizing near-term, transactional victories on trade, bought it. He also broke with decades of U.S. foreign policy precedent to negotiate export controls with China. In a series of “do nothing” leader-level meetings since, the president has emphasized summitry optics over pushing China on any sensitive issues. And Huang’s restoration at Nvidia ensures that the president’s worst impulses on China will continue to be egged on by his favored tech CEO, to the detriment of U.S. national security.

A core part of Congress’s longer-term agenda should be outlining the basic principles to determine who can import or remotely access advanced AI chips and under what conditions. Administrations have gone back and forth over this line-drawing exercise. The Biden-era AI Diffusion Rule would have imposed a global licensing regime that controlled not just the export of chips but also the aggregate U.S.-powered compute that any country could receive. Under Trump, only a handful of countries face restrictions. A smarter approach would condition advanced chip sales on the buyer’s ability to protect them from diversion and guard against misuse by malign actors looking to skirt safety and security rules. Allied and partner governments could demonstrate this by developing robust economic security policies to prevent chip diversion or remote access. Foreign companies would have to show strong compliance and security commitments. In practice, this would mean having internal procedures to ensure that chips are not diverted and maintaining cooperation with Commerce Department officials to verify that such procedures were working.

Congress can also help rebuild allies’ confidence in U.S. export-control policy. There was hard-won, if imperfect, progress on allied alignment in key areas of export controls under the Biden administration, notably with the Dutch and the Japanese on controls of semiconductor manufacturing equipment. The painstaking work needed to get allies on board can be agonizingly slow, but once achieved, it is far more likely to endure than if the United States imposes extraterritorial controls over their objections. The MATCH Act, one of the bills revived following the Megaspeed scandal, requires controls on allied-produced tools if Washington is unable to negotiate allied alignment. It is a rather rude approach at a moment when U.S. export controls seem to be on shaky footing, U.S. tariffs against allies have pushed relations to a near breaking point, and allies rightly question whether the United States still views China as a strategic threat. Instead, Congress should support efforts to create durable frameworks for allied alignment, such as economic security agreements in key strategic sectors.

A longer-term agenda must include fixing the Commerce Department’s Bureau of Industry and Security, the main agency responsible for administering and enforcing export controls. The bureau has been gutted by dismissals and low morale during the Trump administration, but it has been underresourced for far longer. Congress should increase funding and require modernization of the bureau’s antiquated IT system. Just as important, Congress needs to help insulate policymaking decisions from corporate influence, and it can help by insisting that future Commerce Department officials commit to independent and risk-based decision-making to earn confirmation.

None of these steps will be easy. The president’s support of Huang’s restoration shows that stock market fluctuations weigh more heavily in his decision-making than national security, and the administration is unlikely to welcome increased congressional oversight. The Constitution gives the executive wide deference on matters of foreign policy and national security. But Congress has a role in ensuring that the president faithfully executes the laws and is supported by the right resources, oversight, and strategic priorities. The United States’ AI lead may hinge on whether it meets the moment.

The United States—Not China—Dominates Diffusion

#5721. Obama judge's Omar warrant unsealing directly contradicts his own decision in Trump case, DOJ says

Former Acting ICE Director Jonathan Fahey joins 'Fox & Friends' to discuss the newly unsealed search warrant records revealing a decades-long federal investigation into Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn.

Government lawyers argue unsealing documents related to the warrant used to search the Washington, D.C., home of Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., is at odds with a ruling made by the same Obama-appointed federal judge in a high-profile case against President Donald Trump.

Lawyers for the Justice Department pleaded in the filings with Judge James Boasberg, who has frequently found himself at odds with the Trump administration, not to unseal search warrant materials in the Omar case due to the ongoing nature of the investigation,.

The DOJ also argued it has not yet finished executing that search warrant.

Still, Boasberg ordered last week the redacted documents to be released. The DOJ submitted their redacted and the materials were made public Monday night. The only document Boasberg ordered to remain sealed was the government's affidavit presented to a magistrate court judge in order to secure the search warrant.

James Boasberg, incoming chief judge of the US District Court, in Washington, DC, US, on Monday, March 13, 2023. Boasberg, who starts a seven-year term as chief judge on March 17, will oversee the court's secret grand jury proceedings, including pending and future legal fights related to Special Counsel Jack Smith’s probes of Trump, among other duties. mong other duties. (Valerie Plesch/Bloomberg via Getty)

'RADICAL LEFT LUNATIC' JUDGE TRUMP WANTED IMPEACHED TAKES UP ILHAN OMAR WARRANT FIGHT

"Just a few years ago, Chief Judge Boasberg ruled that search warrant proceedings and related documents should not be made open to the public while an investigation is ongoing," the DOJ said in an emergency ex parte brief in opposition to the unsealing.

"The Chief Judge was merely echoing many other courts—like the D.C. Circuit, the Ninth Circuit, the Fourth Circuit, and others when he said that," the brief continued.

The case to which the DOJ referred — where Boasberg ruled that search warrant documents should remain under seal during active investigations — was none other than Special Counsel Jack Smith's probe into Trump for alleged election interference after his 2020 presidential loss, and for the alleged retention of classified documents.

Trump was indicted in both of those investigations. All the charges were eventually dropped.

Judge James Boasberg, President Donald Trump (Valerie Plesch, Kevin Dietsch/Getty Images)

ILHAN OMAR RECORDS SHOW JUDGE, DOJ OFFICIALS DEBATING BEFORE RAID OF HOME

In that case, like the Omar case, media organizations sought to unseal a warrant related to the search. Specifically, the press wanted to view a warrant obtained by Smith related to Twitter's records associated with Trump's account, @realDonaldTrump.

Boasberg's tune was much different in that case, when, like in the Omar case, the government asked him to keep the warrant under seal due to the ongoing nature of the case.

Notably, Trump had already been indicted when Boasberg ruled that the records needed to remain sealed while Omar has not been indicted.

"Ultimately, the undeniable need to protect an ongoing criminal investigation tips the balance toward the Government’s request to continue sealing," Boasberg wrote in his Nov. 29, 2023 ruling.

The White House did not immediately respond to Fox News Digital's request for comment on the apparent u-turn from the judge.

Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., is denying that federal agents have told her what she is under investigation for and speaks to Fox News Digital about the status of her items that were seized after her Washington, D.C., home was raided Oct. 12, 2026. (REUTERS/Elizabeth Frantz)

In the Omar case, government lawyers said they weren't opposed to releasing the documents at an appropriate future date, but did not want them unsealed while the case is ongoing. Boasberg didn't find that argument compelling.

"There may yet come a time when press access becomes appropriate, but at present the Application is a premature bid for sensitive information pertaining to an active investigation," he wrote in 2023.

Throughout his 2023 ruling in favor of the government's bid to keep the warrant in the Trump case sealed, Boasberg continually noted the government's ability to keep warrants under seal.

ILHAN OMAR HUDDLES WITH HER DC-BASED LAWYERS DURING HER FIRST DAY BACK ON CAPITOL HILL AMID FEDERAL PROBES

He said in a second instance: "When it comes to an active investigation, the Government 'has always been able to restrict access to warrant materials by requesting a sealing order, which courts have granted freely upon a showing that a given criminal investigation requires secrecy,'" Boasberg's ruling said.

In response to the media's claim that the First Amendment should be a deciding factor in unsealing the Trump warrant, Boasberg disagreed.

"The historical practice is somewhat muddled, but it suggests that the First Amendment does not open search warrants and related documents to public examination when an investigation is ongoing. Search-warrant applications traditionally have been filed ex parte and thus out of the public’s view."

The DOJ confirmed in a brief statement to Fox News Digital on Tuesday that the characterization that Boasberg contradicted himself in unsealing the Omar documents "is accurate and a point we have made in our court filings."

One issue distinguishing Boasberg's ruling in the 2023 Trump case from the Omar case is the Constitution's Speech or Debate Clause, which shields lawmakers from certain government intrusions into their legislative work.

In the Omar case, the Justice Department argued that searching her home did not involve those protections. Magistrate Judge Matthew Sharbaugh disagreed and required safeguards for potentially privileged materials.

Boasberg later questioned why the legal arguments over those constitutional protections should remain sealed when sensitive investigative details could be redacted. He nevertheless agreed to keep the government's warrant affidavit sealed to protect the ongoing investigation.

Boasberg has also faced scrutiny over investigative secrecy during the Biden-era Arctic Frost probe, when he signed nondisclosure orders tied to subpoenas for Republican lawmakers' phone records. Unlike the Omar and Trump rulings, those orders restricted telecommunications companies from revealing the subpoenas rather than deciding whether court documents should be made public. Court officials have said Boasberg may not have known lawmakers' records were involved.

The District Court for the District of Columbia declined to comment. The U.S. Attorney’s Office for the District of Columbia did not return a request for comment.

Omar's home was raided in the early morning hours of Oct. 12, 2026. Fox News Digital confirmed that the investigators are probing whether Omar or her father fraudulently filled out her citizenship paperwork, though the full scope of the investigation remains under wraps.

The unsealing of last night's documents revealed that investigators are seeking evidence dating back as far as 1980, and that investigators believe Omar committed "crimes over decades— beginning long before she was ever in Congress."

Peter D'Abrosca is a politics reporter at Fox News Digital.

#5718. Mullin celebrates Kilmar Ábrego García's self-deportation to Spain: 'We are delighted'

On Fox News, Laura Ingraham and former Yuma Sector Border Patrol chief Chris Clem analyze these costs and expose a proposed 10-year expiration on deportation orders. Clem warns that granting green cards to those with final removal orders will destroy enforcement.

Kilmar Ábrego García, the illegal immigrant from El Salvador who became a rallying point for critics of President Donald Trump's immigration enforcement policies, voluntarily left the United States on Tuesday to avoid deportation, Fox News has learned.

Ábrego García departed for Spain on a commercial flight Tuesday, according to Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin, who celebrated the departure.

"We are delighted to announce that Kilmar Abrego Garcia has self-deported from the United States to Spain," Mullin said in a statement Tuesday night. "We promised the American people that this human trafficker, wife beater, gang member, and child predator would not remain in our country. Now, this El Salvadoran man cannot terrorize innocent Americans."

He ultimately plans to make his way to Mexico, The New York Times reported, citing his attorneys.

TRUMP’S SPECIAL STRATEGY TO DEPORT ILLEGALS GIVES HIM ANOTHER TOOL FOR ENFORCEMENT

Kilmar Ábrego García arrives with his wife Jennifer Vasquez Sura and his attorney Simon Sandoval-Moshenberg at U.S. District Court for the District of Maryland on December 22, 2025, in Greenbelt, Md. (Alex Wong/Getty Images)

His departure caps a months-long legal battle that began when he was initially deported to his home country and detained there.

A federal judge in Maryland ordered Ábrego García to leave the U.S. by Tuesday, according to two sources familiar with the matter. That order remains under seal, and the exact terms of his release were not immediately clear.

The Department of Homeland Security (DHS) emphasized that Ábrego García did not receive any financial incentives through the Trump administration's voluntary self-deportation program, which offers migrants travel assistance and financial support to leave the country.

"To be clear: Kilmar Abrego Garcia departed the United States pursuant to a removal order. He paid for his own flight and received no incentives,"DHS posted on X. "He will NEVER be allowed back inside the United States."

DHS also rejected descriptions of Ábrego García as a Maryland resident.

"Kilmar Abrego Garcia is not and will never be a Maryland Man—he is a criminal illegal alien from El Salvador and was a public safety threat to American citizens. He is not coming back, despite the efforts of sanctuary politicians who spent massive amounts of time and money defending this low-life," the department said.

The move comes after sustained efforts by the Trump administration to remove Ábrego García, who entered the U.S. illegally more than a decade ago. At one point during the legal proceedings, federal authorities sought to deport him to several African nations.

Ábrego García had been living in Maryland with his wife and children when immigration authorities detained him in March 2025. He was subsequently deported to El Salvador in error and held in the Central American nation's maximum-security CECOT prison despite a standing court order barring his removal there due to the risk of gang persecution.

Kilmar Abrego Garcia, a Maryland man who was deported to El Salvador earlier this year, and his wife Jennifer Vasquez Sura, left, arrive for a check-in at the Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) field office in Baltimore, Maryland, on Monday, Aug. 25, 2025. (Graeme Sloan/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

Democrats rallied around him despite his suspected ties to the MS-13 gang.

"A year and a half ago, the Trump Administration wanted to leave Kilmar Ábrego García to rot in a Salvadoran gulag. Today, Kilmar is traveling to a country of his choice, a free man — unharmed and with dignity," said Sen. Chris Van Hollen, D-Md., who visited Ábrego García while detained in El Salvador.

"While the Trump Administration had been pursuing their vindictive prosecution against Kilmar and desperately trying to ship him against his will to countries known for human rights abuses, the Administration was forced to come to this agreement that respects Kilmar’s will and the due process rights guaranteed to him and to all in our nation. I am relieved that he will be safe, in a country of his choosing, and free – including from this Administration’s constant persecution."

MIKE DAVIS: KILMAR ABREGO GARCIA, THE DEMOCRATS' PATRON SAINT OF HUMAN TRAFFICK

Kilmar Ábrego García, right, and his wife, Jennifer Vasquez Sura, attend a prayer vigil before he enters a U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement field office Aug. 25, 2025, in Baltimore. (Anna Moneymaker/Getty Images)

Federal officials eventually returned him to the U.S. to face human smuggling charges from a 2022 traffic stop in Tennessee. He pleaded not guilty to the charges, which a judge later dismissed.

Throughout the legal fight, the administration maintained that Ábrego García was a gang member and pointed to a 2021 protective order his wife had filed against him over domestic violence allegations.

Kilmar Ábrego García and his wife, Jennifer, are seen outside the ICE Field Office in Baltimore on August 25, 2025. (Breanne Deppisch/Fox News Digital)

#5716. After midterm losses, Republicans debate what's next for the party

In the wake of their midterm election losses, Republicans are discussing what happened and what the future might be for their party.

#5713. California prosecutors, victim advocates challenge Newsom's mass death sentence commutation

Victim advocates and California prosecutors are pushing back against Gov. Gavin Newsom’s blanket commutation of all 548 death row sentences, arguing the move overrides the will of voters, bypasses individual case reviews and could eventually open a legal pathway to freedom for convicted killers.

California Gov. Gavin Newsom justified his blanket commutation of 548 death sentences to life without parole by arguing that reviewing cases individually would create "two classes of murder victims," a rationale victims' advocates and at least one district attorney called "morally offensive. "

Modoc County District Attorney Nina Salarno said Newsom's sweeping action denied the loved ones of murder victims any voice in the clemency process.

"I found it really offensive as a victim myself, and [for] my other victims," Salarno told Fox News Digital from Washington, D. C., where she and fellow advocates met with President Donald Trump this week. "There should be individual scrutiny. The individual victims should have a voice. And that's what he has avoided by doing a blanket commutation."

CALIFORNIA PROSECUTORS SLAM NEWSOM'S DEATH ROW COMMUTATIONS, ACCUSE GOVERNOR OF PUTTING 'POLITICS OVER PEOPLE'

Gov. Gavin Newsom speaks on March 26, 2025, in Los Angeles. (Frazer Harrison/WireImage)

"The blanket commutation on a moral level as a victim I find very offensive, but I find the whole thing offensive to our entire rule of law," she added.

Topo Padilla, president of Crime Victims United, a nonprofit that advocates for stricter laws against violent offenders, noted that California voters have repeatedly upheld the death penalty at the ballot box, including approving a 2016 measure aimed at speeding up executions.

"Here's a guy who throws rocks about kings and protests throughout the streets," Padilla said, referring to Newsom's past criticisms of Trump. "I will promise you, this is a move that is looked at and viewed as a king. He is trampling on the voters."

On Monday, Trump vowed to direct the Justice Department to seek the death penalty for child murderers and expedite federal executions as victims’ relatives spoke out at the Justice Department against Newsom's order.

Lieutenant Sam Robinson, a public information officer at San Quentin State Prison, knocks on the door to the East Block for condemned prisoners during a media tour of California's Death Row in San Quentin, Calif., on Dec. 29, 2015. (Stephen Lam/Reuters)

"Gavin Newsom and the radical left look at these heinous acts, and their sympathy is not for the children and their parents, but for the monsters who extinguished innocent young lives," Trump said. "He stood with the murderers and child slayers."

Justice for crime victims shouldn't be a partisan issue, Padilla said.

"Whether you're a Democrat or a Republican, you take politics out of this," he said. "The president of the United States stood up and sounded the alarm. And I hope the country hears it."

Newsom's office referred Fox News Digital to news release from Monday featuring victims and survivors who support his decision.

#5711. Democrats win House by largest margin in 50 years. And, COP31 ends with no deal

Vote counts are concluding for the 2026 midterm elections, revealing a better picture of just how much Democrats dominated. Fifty-six percent of voters chose the Democrats in House races compared to 42% for Republicans. The 14-point gap is the largest margin won by Democrats in 50 years. The party has officially won the largest majority in Congress since the early 1990s. Take a look at NPR's breakdown of the U.S. House races, by the numbers.

Artificial intelligence is causing significant swings in the stock market as investors speculate about the future of the technology boom. While AI stocks have driven markets to record highs and bolstered the economy, concerns are growing about a potential bubble. Investors anticipated that AI giant Anthropic's stock market debut last week would lift markets, but instead Wall Street experienced one of its worst weeks in recent times.

Poland has called an emergency NATO session for today after Russia fired three cruise missiles into its territory. The incident highlights the stakes for European countries in Russia's war on Ukraine, prompting nations along NATO's eastern border to seek enhanced air defense support. Poland plans to present proposals to increase surveillance capabilities and deploy more F-35s to intercept incoming missiles and drones.

Delegates at COP31 in Antalya, Turkey, couldn't agree on a plan over the weekend to address fossil fuels, the main cause of climate change. The event ran two days longer than expected, making it the longest in the history of these summits. Nearly 200 nations failed to finalize targets for electrification and financial aid for developing countries, or to address the impacts of climate change. The European Union rejected any conclusion that didn't advocate for a transition away from fossil fuels, while oil-producing countries like Saudi Arabia opposed this position. Fossil fuels contribute about 73% of global emissions.

by Maria Godoy, senior science and health editor and correspondent

Clinical services supervisor Dale Weiser poses for a portrait at the Chester County Health Department in West Chester, Pa., on Oct. 28, 2026. Daniel Vasta for NPR hide caption

People had warned me it might be tough to get Amish people to talk to me as a journalist — and probably impossible to get their permission to record their voice. Not a great omen for compelling radio storytelling.

Yet as I headed off to Lancaster County, Pa., last month to report on the biggest and deadliest measles outbreak in the U.S. in decades, I knew I wanted to hear directly from the community hit hardest.

So I turned to some good old shoe-leather reporting. My producer and I mapped out Amish-owned businesses in and around Lancaster County and spent a day driving around from shop to shop. At every stop, we introduced ourselves and explained the focus of our story. We asked Amish people (easily identifiable by their traditional dress) about their views on the measles outbreak and vaccination. While some declined to talk, others opened up about how measles had affected their families and communities, and why they had or hadn't decided to get vaccinated. To my surprise, one woman even let me record her voice.

Outside a farm supply store in a rural town, I met an Amish woman who was visibly pregnant. Measles can have devastating consequences during pregnancy. She told me her husband and two children had gotten vaccinated to protect her and her unborn child. She declined to give me her name at first, then got in a car driven by her hired non-Amish driver. As they exited the parking lot, the driver stopped to ask us what media outlet we were with. "Oh, NPR!" the driver said when we replied. Then she turned to her Amish passenger and told her, "Don't worry, you can trust them."

Part of gaining trust is about showing up, showing respect and really listening. Word gets around that you're trustworthy. Public health officials in Pennsylvania have also been using a similar approach to win the trust of Amish people as they seek to boost vaccination rates in the current outbreak. You can read more about it here.

Bottles of Roundup weed killer are displayed on a shelf at a Home Depot store on June 25, 2026 in North Hollywood, California. Justin Sullivan/Getty Images North America hide caption

#5684. California Democrat Rep. Gomez resigns following ethics report on sexual misconduct allegations

U.S. Rep. Jimmy Gomez, seen here at a Congressional hearing in March, announced Monday that he would resign following a report from the House Ethics committee on allegations of sexual misconduct. Heather Diehl/Getty Images North America hide caption

Rep. Jimmy Gomez, D-Calif., says he is resigning from Congress after a bipartisan investigation released Monday found "substantial evidence" he engaged in sexual misconduct toward a House staffer.

The House Ethics Committee concluded Gomez violated House rules by "making unwelcome sexual advances and engaging in unwelcome sexual conduct towards a House staffer and disregarding the power imbalance inherent in his relationship with House staffers."

The report also says the behavior could constitute criminal sexual assault.

Gomez has served in Congress since 2017 representing parts of Los Angeles. He lost reelection this fall to fellow Democrat Angela Gonzales-Torres. California uses a system where the top two vote-getters no matter the party advance to the general election. Gomez says his resignation will be effective Dec. 1.

Lisa Banks and Sarah Nesbitt, attorneys for the unnamed House staffer, wrote in a statement that their client testified to the committee truthfully and at personal risk.

In a statement announcing his plan to resign, Gomez wrote that he cooperated with the investigation, but has strong disagreements with the report and its conclusions.

"Among other significant deficiencies and omissions, the report does not include key portions of my testimony that are essential to a full and fair understanding of what occurred," he wrote.

"I also have serious concerns about the process. In my interview, I answered the Committee's questions only to see my statements selectively quoted, disparaged, and disregarded throughout the final report, to which I was given no opportunity to respond."

The House Ethics Committee, which includes five Democrats and five Republicans, opened its investigation into Gomez in August following a press report of Gomez kissing a staffer from another Congressional office at a party. Gomez denied those allegations.

The committee asked Gomez to disclose whether he "ever engaged in a romantic or sexual relationship or made sexual contact with a congressional staffer." Gomez disclosed interactions with two women — telling the committee the encounters were all consensual. Both women spoke to the committee.

One denied "she had ever had a nonconsensual sexual encounter" with Gomez. The other, described as Witness 1 in the report, said she had more than one encounter with Gomez and she did not consider them consensual.

She told investigators that Gomez made sexual contact without her consent at a bar in February 2024 and then was forceful with her and tried to kiss her at his congressional office in July 2024.

Gomez denied the interactions were nonconsensual. The staffer, described as Witness 1 in the committee report, eventually left her job on Capitol Hill.

"Here, the House lost Witness 1 as a competent and able staffer because of Representative Gomez's misconduct and because of a broader culture of disrespect and toxic sexual behavior toward staff that permeates life on the Hill and is long overdue for correction," the committee wrote.

The report comes amid a broader conversation about how Congress deals with the behavior of its members, especially when it comes to sexual misconduct.

In April, Democrat Eric Swalwell of California resigned his House seat amid allegations of sexual assault and misconduct. He also denied the allegations. Rep. Tony Gonzales (R-Tex.) also resigned from the House after admitting to an affair with a former staff member.

Rep. Chuck Edwards (R-N.C.) dropped his reelection bid earlier this year after the House Ethics Committee recommended he face a censure vote for allegedly harassing two young female staffers. Edwards denied the allegations.

Republican Reps. Max Miller of Ohio and Cory Mills of Florida also face House Ethics investigations stemming from allegations of domestic violence. Both deny wrongdoing.

Miller lost his reelection bid this fall and Mills lost his GOP primary.

Last week, the House Ethics Committee adopted new rules with the aim of speeding up investigations into sexual misconduct and improving transparency.
