#5680. Democrats probe whether Trump Jr.-aligned businesses benefit from government access

Donald Trump Jr. speaks on stage on the second day of the 2026 Republican National Convention on Sept. 10, in Dallas. Brandon Bell/Getty Images hide caption

Congressional Democrats, led by likely House Judiciary Committee Chair Rep. Jamie Raskin of Maryland, are expanding their investigation into the investment firm 1789 Capital, which brought on Donald Trump Jr. as a partner days after his father's reelection to the White House.

Since then, investigators say the firm has grown from "a middling venture capital firm" with $150 million in managed assets to "the darling of the venture capital world" with $3 billion in managed assets.

Investigators want to determine whether the growth was boosted by the president's son leveraging political influence or government information to secure federal contracts and regulatory changes for companies with connections to the investment firm.

Trump Jr. told Axios last week that the timing of his hiring at 1789 Capital was coincidental, noting that he had already been working with founder Omeed Malik for years. He also said he draws a clear line when marketing and raising money for the firm.

"I make it very clear [to foreign investors] that I have nothing to do with government," Trump Jr. said. "[The president is] my father, but he's also busy and I probably haven't spoken to him in three weeks."

He also suggested he doesn't use or need government connections to make smart investments. "Trying to do the things that we're alleged to be doing every day, I think that would last for about two seconds before it was out there very publicly," Trump Jr. said. He added that the firm relies on teams of analysts to make investments in industries where there are obvious public and national security demands but relatively few companies who are able to meet those needs.

NPR reached out to Donald Trump Jr. for comment and did not hear back.

Regarding Raskin's request for documents from 1789 Capital, Bill Burck, an attorney representing the firm, told NPR, "We responded to Congressman Raskin back in September pointing out in detail the many flaws in his understanding of how 1789 has operated and a number of basic factual errors. He never bothered to reply. Facts are facts and Congressman Raskin has them wrong here but persists in his tall tales against 1789. These are personal attacks untethered to actual evidence."

On Monday, congressional investigators sent letters to four companies — military contractor Hadrian, electronic cigarette company Juul, prediction market Polymarket and online weapons dealer GrabAGun — asking them to preserve their records and turn over a broad array of records to the committee.

The committee is interested in Hadrian's contracts with the armed forces, signed in March of this year, worth close to a billion dollars. Investigators allege that, in 2024, the company had a single manufacturing facility and "no history of fulfilling government contracts." They also noted that CEO Chris Power donated tens of thousands of dollars to Trump-aligned political organizations in advance of the 2024 election.

Political donations are constitutionally-protected speech and not necessarily evidence of wrongdoing. 1789 Capital first invested in Hadrian in July 2025. The company is also backed by a number of bluechip investors, including Morgan Stanley, T. Rowe Price, Andreessen Horowitz and JPMorganChase. Hadrian did not reply to a request for comment from NPR.

Investigators also allege that Trump Jr.'s firm took a stake in e-cigarette company Juul in March 2025, as the company teetered near insolvency following rulings during the first Trump administration limiting the sale of various flavor cartridges. Four months after that investment, though before it was publicly disclosed, investigators say the company's fortunes rebounded after the Trump administration authorized five new Juul e-cigarette products. The company has announced plans for more flavor authorization applications moving forward.

In a statement to NPR, a Juul spokesperson said the company had received Rep. Raskin's letter and are "reviewing it closely. As always, we intend to cooperate with any Congressional effort to understand our company, our commitment to combating underage vaping, and our mission to help adult smokers of combustible cigarettes transition completely away from smoking."

Investigators are also interested in betting site Polymarket. They say that following a meeting between Polymarket leadership and Trump Jr. at the 2024 Republican National Convention, 1789 Capital invested in the company — which at the time was banned in the United States. In July 2025, after President Trump took office, the Justice Department and the Commodity Futures Trading Commission notified the company that they had closed investigations launched during the Biden administration, according to reporting from Bloomberg News. The CFTC later authorized a U.S. version of the platform to launch. Investigators allege 1789's investment resulted in a 70-fold return.

GrabAGun, a company that "believe[s] people should be able to use their computers, phones, and tablets to shop for firearms the same way they purchase everything else," became, according to investigators, a publicly-traded company with the help of 1789 Capital leadership, including Trump Jr., who serves of its board.

In May 2026, the Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco, Firearms and Explosives (ATF) proposed a draft rule that would make it easier to ship guns directly to people's homes. At the time, GrabAGun claimed that the company is "uniquely positioned to capitalize on this potential opportunity." Investigators are seeking to understand what knowledge and involvement Trump Jr. had of the proposed rule change.

In a statement to NPR, GrabAGun said the investigators' letter is "a political document, and its allegations are baseless." It added that the ATF's review of its regulations was well known across the firearms industry, and the agency published numerous proposed rules for public comment.

"GrabAGun's only input came through public comments on several of those proposals," the company said.

#5673. For workplace culture champions, Jensen Huang's return is 'unfathomable'

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang speaks to media after a roundtable with South Korean AI and robotics startups on June 8, 2026, in Seoul, South Korea. Chung Sung-Jun/Getty Images AsiaPac hide caption

Sign up for NPR's Up First morning newsletter for the stories you need to start your day.

On Monday morning, a couple hours after Nvidia's board announced it had reinstated co-founder Jensen Huang as CEO, President Trump shared an AI-generated image on social media.

It's based on that striking photo of the president from 2024, just after he survived an assasination attempt. Surrounded by Secret Service agents, his face bloodied, Trump hoists a defiant fist in the air.

In the doctored photo, instead of Trump, it's Huang. An Nvidia flag flies behind him. And in his hoisted fist, he holds a red bra.

"I was absolutely flabbergasted," says Bo Young Lee, who worked as chief diversity officer at Uber for five years starting in 2018.

To Lee, it seems Trump is suggesting that the Nvidia CEO is as important to the country as he is.

"But then also the red bra is an indication that it's open hunting season for women," she says. "It stirs up all sorts of stereotypes and misogynistic perspectives about women."

To others, the image drives home the idea that in Silicon Valley, as long as you're successful, anything goes.

The White House has not responded to NPR's questions about what Trump was trying to say with the image.

Even as Huang's return to Nvidia has been cheered by investors, some employees and Trump, it has sparked shock and disbelief among others, including those who have long worked to improve workplace culture in Silicon Valley.

"It's actually unfathomable to me," says Lee, who now serves as an adviser to AI4ALL, a nonprofit dedicated to increasing diversity, inclusion, and responsible practices in artificial intelligence.

After The New York Times revealed that Huang had engaged in an extramarital affair with a woman the U.S government believes is a Chinese intelligence asset, Lee watched as the broader conversation was dominated by concerns about national security, corporate governance and Nvidia's stock price.

That scandal overshadowed allegations of other problematic behavior in the workplace, specifically Huang's alleged affair with Kimberly Powell, Nvidia's vice president of healthcare, one of Huang's many direct reports.

"Everyone is minimizing that alleged relationship there because of who she is," says Lee.

The fact that Powell is herself a senior leader who makes a lot of money shouldn't matter, Lee says. She argues that people in subordinate roles, even powerful ones, can't properly consent, "simply for the fact that there is this power differential."

Nvidia's board did find that Huang's behavior was "inconsistent" with the company's code of conduct. By Monday morning, that didn't seem to matter.

"The Board of Directors voted unanimously to reinstate Mr. Huang following a series of discussions and meetings with Mr. Huang, after which the Board was satisfied with respect to Mr. Huang's candor and ability to lead Nvidia," the board said in a statement.

Lee says that response sends a message to Nvidia employees and all of Silicon Valley that it's OK to break the rules as long as you come clean.

"'Just be transparent with us,'" she imagines the board saying.

Nvidia did not respond to NPR's questions about Lee's concerns.

In Silicon Valley, there have been private conversations about Huang's alleged workplace transgressions and the power dynamics at play, but women don't appear eager to speak publicly.

NPR reached out to two dozen women who work in the tech space. Freada Kapor Klein was one of the few willing to talk. The social impact investor says conversations about workplace behavior are much quieter than they have been in other eras, such as in 2017 and 2018, when a number of venture capitalists resigned amid allegations they were sexually harassing female entrepreneurs.

In 2017, Kapor Klein and her husband, Mitch Kapor, used their positions as early investors in Uber to publicly call out the company over its toxic culture, one they said tolerated bullying and harassment. The co-founder and CEO Travis Kalanick ended up resigning. Kapor Klein hoped it'd be a teachable moment.

But now, she feels Silicon Valley has gone backwards.

"Tech bro culture is stronger now than I have seen it in decades," she says, adding that Trump's attacks on DEI have amplified that culture.

Among women and people of color in Silicon Valley, she says there's now real trepidation about the anti-DEI sentiment — the axing of programs, chief diversity officers and employee resource groups.

"All of the supports or mechanisms have been pretty much stripped away," she says.

Screens display news of the reinstatement of Jensen Huang, seen outside the NASDAQ MarketSite, in New York, on November 23, 2026. Angela Weiss/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

To be clear, Huang has not been accused of predatory behavior. Still, Kapor Klein says his swift comeback is troubling.

"It is a resounding message that all that matters is money," she says. "If you are thought of as a singular talent, you can violate corporate policy, you can possibly threaten national security, you can make up your own rules as you go, you can create toxic workplace culture — as long as you bring in the money."

By the end of last week, Nvidia's stock had fallen almost 49% from its peak in October, and about 27% from before Huang's resignation. On Monday, it rebounded significantly.

Lee says Silicon Valley has always had an unhealthy relationship with its "genius founders."

"We elevate these leaders to god-like levels, and that's been done to Mark Zuckerberg, and that's being done to Sam Altman, and that's definitely [been] done to Jensen Huang," says Lee. "You become untouchable at that point."

Kapor Klein says what's unfolded at Nvidia has made abundantly clear that AI companies need guardrails for their CEOs, not just for the tools they're creating.

"We see a board that ignored that," she says. "There are no guardrails on this CEO's behavior."

NPR's Scott Horsley contributed to this story.

#5668. Solar lease holdouts still see value in privately-owned residential projects

The One Big Beautiful Bill Act drastically reduced the lifespan of federal subsidies available to certain segments of the solar market, and among the first to go was the investment tax credit for residential projects. The ITC is a historic renewable energy subsidy that provides a 30% tax credit against the total cost of an eligible project, and it is currently available to the commercial and utility-scale solar segments.

A residential solar project in New York installed by Nickels Energy Solutions. Credit: Nickels Energy Solutions

After 2025, homeowners hoping to install solar on their house couldn’t subsidize the project’s cost with this federal subsidy if it was privately owned. However, a loophole in the tax credit language has allowed leased or “third party-owned” (TPO) solar projects to still receive the ITC (25D) on residential solar projects. In this scenario, it matters who owns the solar project, not necessarily where it’s built. The project can be subsidized because it is owned by a commercial company, and not the homeowner.

Some residential solar installers are still strictly pursuing and installing privately-owned solar projects. Nickels Energy Solutions (NES), a residential solar contractor working in Central New York believes a privately-owned PV system is still the better deal for the customer.

“We don’t have the same offers as some of the bigger companies,” said Kevin Nickels, VP of sales with NES. “If you can offer leases, it’s like the ‘haves and have-nots.’ I don’t have that loophole or that trick up my sleeve, so it forces me to get the project sold, while I’m as sharp as I can be with margins to make it somewhat competitive.”

TPO solar transactions are typically arranged at a lower up-front cost than a wholly owned solar project. Lease contract lengths vary by provider, and financiers have surety in these transactions because the projects will be built with products from an approved vendor list (AVL). Products on an AVL can qualify for a domestic content subsidy, another tax credit in addition to the ITC, because they are manufactured using a certain percentage of domestic components.

These are premium products vetted for AVLs. But it can also mean that installers are paying premium prices for components in building TPO solar projects.

Nickels believes that, while a privately owned residential solar project will cost more at the start, the combination of expensive components and interest rates for financing over a lease term — with factors like rate increases — can cost a customer more in the long run.

“I don’t think all leases are bad,” he said. “I just don’t think it’s what’s best for the industry or the homeowner.”

Rudy Wright, owner of Wright-Way Solar of Tyler, Texas, has shifted the company’s focus from building new projects to servicing existing systems. As the ITC exited the residential solar market, so did many of the lenders financing these projects. Without lending options, Wright-Way’s installation output has slowed down, and those clients who can afford solar are paying with cash or using a home equity loan to cover the cost.

Despite that, Wright said he isn’t very interested in building leased solar projects.

Wright-Way Solar built a solar plus storage project at a Ranch in Texas featuring arrays that are facing east, west and south. Credit: Wright-Way Solar

“I do think it cost me some of those entry level clients, but every time I analyzed the financing end of it, I felt like I was benefitting my company and the industry more than the customer,” Wright said. “I haven’t seen the angle where bringing a pre-purchase agreement or bringing a lease agreement has enough ironclad return for the customer.”

Tyler is a unique market for solar because many of Wright-Way’s customers are trying to make their properties more resilient, and less reliant on the grid, by installing solar and storage, and not just seeking cost savings for their energy use. Wright has installed solar plus storage systems on Texan ranches at a scale close a small commercial project.

That promise of resilience is still enough reason for some people to commission a solar project in Texas, where the state’s independent electrical grid operator has faltered and experienced dangerous outages in recent years.

“Folks realize money in the bank doesn’t do you any good if the power goes out,” Wright said.

NES is still building a steady pipeline of residential solar projects in New York. Nickels said that has been possible in part because many of its clients are upper-middle class; but New York also maintains an incentive program for solar projects, NY-Sun, which includes a 25% state tax credit for residential systems.

“The OBBBA caught a lot of states flat-footed,” Nickels said. “Without federal support, it puts a lot of pressure on what the state is offering.”

Some states’ solar subsidy programs have stepped in where federal support has diminished since the OBBBA was passed. The North Carolina Clean Energy Technology Center maintains the Database of State Incentives for Renewables & Efficiency, which analyzes which states currently have policies and incentives supporting clean energy developments. Every state has policy enabling solar projects, but not necessarily cost incentives for individual projects.

While traditional residential solar projects have lost a significant subsidy, the reality is that energy costs have risen in response to new grid demands from hyperscale data centers and oil scarcity from the United States’ war in Iran. Sure, a privately owned residential solar project won’t receive the ITC, but it can still stabilize energy costs without them.

“We lost a big incentive, but the rate increase is proportional,” Nickels said.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5659. Three Routes to VPP Revenue for Installers, EPCs, and Developers Utility programs, wholesale markets, and bilateral contracts each come with different eligibility rules, payment structures, and commercial responsibilities.

By Intersolar & Energy Storage North America

In June 2026, Google and Voltus announced a three-year agreement to unlock up to 100 megawatts (MW) of distributed capacity annually in PJM. Under Voltus’s Bring Your Own Capacity model, Google funds procurement while Voltus aggregates qualifying distributed energy resources (DERs) and compensates participating customers.1

The deal is one of three distinct paths to earn revenue from DERs. Across the U.S., utility programs, wholesale electricity markets, and bilateral contracts each have their own eligibility requirements, payment structures and commercial responsibilities.

Utility programs offer an established pathway for compensating customers and asset owners who reduce demand, shift consumption or discharge stored energy during periods of grid need. Payments may be tied to enrollment, committed capacity, measured performance or a combination of these factors. Massachusetts, for example, offers residential battery customers $275 per kW of average summer performance through ConnectedSolutions2.

Beyond customer incentive programs, utilities also procure distributed flexibility through contracts to address local grid constraints or broader capacity needs. Unlike standardized incentives, these arrangements can establish specific delivery requirements and compensation terms, creating opportunities for developers and service providers to assemble and manage DER portfolios. During a July 2026 heat event, Green Mountain Power dispatched 90 MW of stored energy capacity, helping customers avoid an estimated $6 million in peak-related costs during one peak hour3.

Aggregated DERs can earn revenue from energy, capacity, and ancillary services in wholesale electricity markets, subject to regional market rules and resource qualification. Unlike utility-specific programs, wholesale markets can provide access to broader regional revenue opportunities and multiple grid services. For developers and installers, participation typically occurs through an aggregator or other qualified market participant rather than through direct market registration.

While Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC) Order 2222 established a broader framework for DER participation, uneven implementation across regional markets continues to constrain commercial opportunities for aggregated resources. Market access does not automatically translate into revenue, as individual resources must still meet qualification requirements and comply with market-specific rules. New York Independent System Operator (NYISO), for example, requires DER aggregations to meet minimum capacity and real-time telemetry requirements, which can increase participation costs and limit the economic viability of smaller resources.

Wholesale participation can also expose VPP portfolios to fluctuating market prices, performance obligations and restrictions on receiving compensation for overlapping services. In Texas, ERCOT’s Aggregate Distributed Energy Resource (ADER) pilot allows aggregated DERs to participate in wholesale energy and ancillary services markets, subject to qualification, telemetry, and dispatch requirements.

Bilateral contracts allow utilities and large electricity consumers to procure distributed flexibility directly from aggregators and service providers. Growing interest from data center operators and other large energy buyers is expanding the potential market for VPP capacity beyond traditional utility programs. Unlike standardized programs, bilateral agreements can be tailored to a buyer’s specific needs, with negotiated terms for capacity, delivery periods, compensation, and performance obligations.

The appropriate route depends on existing customer relationships, market access, and the responsibilities each partner assumes. Developers and EPCs working on commercial storage projects may find opportunities through utility procurement or capacity contracts, while residential installers with established battery customer bases may be well positioned to incorporate VPP enrollment into utility programs. Wholesale markets can expand revenue opportunities through aggregation partners, and bilateral contracts can create opportunities to develop resources around specific buyer needs.

Sponsored Content by Intersolar & Energy Storage North America

#5655. The U.S. government is investigating Iranian ties to as many as 50 hacked commercial vessels

In an aerial view, a container ship arrives at the Port of Oakland on Oct. 10, 2025 in Oakland, Calif. Justin Sullivan/Getty Images hide caption

The U.S. government has intelligence suggesting that up to 50 commercial shipping vessels were recently compromised by cyberattacks, activity federal officials are investigating as potentially linked to Iran.

That's according to a U.S. official familiar with the matter who was not authorized to speak on the record, a former military cyber operator and an industry executive briefed on the attacks. The series of breaches was first reported by the The Wall Street Journal.

The attacks against the maritime sector, if officials' suspicions are correct, may be the latest example of Iranian cyberattacks against global critical infrastructure during the ongoing war with the United States.

NPR has reported that cybersecurity experts and U.S. officials believe Iranian hackers were responsible for a series of digital attacks against U.S. water treatment systems across the country in August as well as a pattern of attacks targeting refrigeration cooling systems that led to spoiled food at grocery stores on U.S. military bases and all across Europe as recently as early November.

The Coast Guard and the FBI first partnered in August to send a "highly specialized team" to board two different foreign commercial vessels "in the Gulf of America" heading to U.S. ports, according to a statement provided by the FBI and the Coast Guard. "The captain, crew, and shore-side corporate staff were critical partners in helping to ensure the threats were mitigated," according to the statement.

The FBI and Coast Guard press offices also told NPR that there are no current "reports of operational disruptions, vessel instability, physical danger to crews, or environmental impacts."

However, there are dozens more ships in international waters that the U.S. government believes have been compromised by hackers and many others who might be vulnerable to similar attacks.

According to the U.S. official familiar with the investigation, bad actors took advantage of known vulnerabilities in Fortinet FortiGate devices, which are frequently used in the maritime industry. The Coast Guard and FBI released a cyber bulletin in September warning the industry that bad actors were breaking into these devices, which help manage firewalls and virtual private networks. "Threat actors routinely scan for specific, unpatched vulnerabilities to gain a foothold," the bulletin read.

According to the U.S. official, in some cases, employees aboard the ships suspected something nefarious was going on when they noticed email disruptions or couldn't log in to their accounts, or, in at least one case, an extended communications blackout. The U.S. official declined to comment on how the U.S. government knows about the extent of breaches across the sector.

However, according to the U.S. official, hackers were able to break in using the Fortinet vulnerability and then pivot from the ships' IT systems over to their network of devices controlling physical processes on the ships, what's called operational technology.

The U.S. official said the bad actors "could have impacted the systems on board that control propulsion, steering, navigation, and cargo management," though they did not share any examples of that kind of activity occurring. In at least one case, one of the ships affected was an LNG tanker designed to transport liquified natural gas. It's important to keep systems at a certain temperature to prevent dangerous accidents if a ship's cargo includes explosive materials –from chemicals to fuel.

While commercial vessels are accustomed to physical security reviews entering the United States following the Sept. 11 terrorist attacks, digital threats are becoming all the more important to track as adversaries can break in and cause damage from afar. "It's a totally new paradigm that these tankers are networked in this way," the U.S. official said.

Even so, national security officials have feared something like this might happen for years. A former Navy official who requested anonymity to discuss internal operations tells NPR that "we had been tracking the possibility to exploit and take control of [operational technology] systems on Navy ships and Merchant [Marine] ships for a long time, and now this is coming to fruition."

While the U.S. government is wary of advertising Iranian hacking efforts during the war, officials also want to spread awareness of the vulnerability of internet connected devices, particularly those that are linked to the technology that controls critical processes. "These are not sophisticated attacks," the official said. "This is a known vulnerability that could be mitigated with some pretty basic cyber hygiene."

"Modern ships are highly integrated systems. A cyberattack presents significant risks, including the potential for a collision, grounding, oil spill, or even an explosion. The Coast Guard regularly publishes bulletins on cyber vulnerabilities and actions vessel owners and operators can take to secure their networks," according to a statement from the Coast Guard.

Ahead of the winter and a busy holiday season for commercial shipping, officials are bracing themselves to investigate any ship entering U.S. ports that could be compromised.

"People never think about what a modern blessing electricity is until it goes out. People take the marine transportation system for granted until there's a problem and their stuff doesn't get there by Christmas," the U.S. official said. "I wish more Americans knew how complex this system is."

NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews. Anyone who makes such an offer or request is not an NPR employee and does not represent NPR.

#5653. ES Foundry ships more than 1 GW of U.S.-manufactured solar cells

ES Foundry, a U.S. manufacturer of high-efficiency solar cells, is at RE+ 2026 this week to showcase its American-made solar cells and announce a milestone in its operating capacity.

According to the company, it is the first and only crystalline silicon solar cell manufacturer to ship more than 1 gigawatt (GW) of domestically manufactured solar cells in the United States.

As one of the few U.S. solar cell manufacturers with a fully operational facility producing and shipping cells to customers, ES Foundry is helping move American solar manufacturing to commercial reality.

In July, the company announced it reached its full 3-GW manufacturing capacity. The company began production on its first 1-GW line last year and has now completed its 2-GW expansion line.

Solar Power World attended the opening of its Greenwood, South Carolina facility last year, reporting on its manufacturing outfit and its unique approach to supply deals that include working with large-scale project developers and utilities to guarantee domestic content supplies.

At RE+, attendees can see its U.S.-manufactured high-efficiency monocrystalline bifacial PERC solar cells and meet with company representatives to discuss domestic sourcing, manufacturing capabilities, and the evolving needs of the U.S. solar market.

#5647. enSights brings AI-powered business management to solar and storage operations

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 16, 2026

As solar and energy storage portfolios grow in both size and complexity, managing performance is just one part of the equation. Owners and operators must also keep track of power purchase agreements (PPAs), warranties, service contracts, billing, compliance requirements and project-level profitability.

enSights is looking to bring those responsibilities together with the launch of its AI Studio, a new addition to its Energy Business Management (EBM) platform. The company announced the launch Nov. 10.

The AI Studio is designed to give solar and storage owners, operators, and asset managers access to a customizable team of AI specialists that can support business and operational workflows. Through no-code tools, users can create AI dashboards and reports using natural-language instructions, while still establishing their own workflows, routines, data-access permissions and approval requirements.

The approach here reflects a broader shift toward renewable energy software, it’s moving beyond monitoring individual assets toward managing the financial and operational performance of entire portfolios.

According to enSights, its EBM platform brings together energy management systems (EMS), production data, billing, PPA information, operations and maintenance, contractual obligations and compliance on a unified data foundation. That integration is central to the company’s AI strategy.

Rather than limiting AI applications to identifying equipment issues or answering generation-related questions, the platform is designed to connect energy production with the contracts and financial obligations associated with each project.

The AI Studio includes an orchestrator that coordinates specialized AI agents focused on areas like monitoring, operations, revenue, warranties, permits, lenders, insurers, and asset management. For portfolio managers, those capabilities could help reduce the time spent reviewing disconnected systems and manually reconciling information across departments. Automated reviews of contractual obligations, revenue performance and operational alerts could also help teams identify issues earlier and direct attention to matters that still require human intervention.

The enSights AI Studio dashboard provides solar and energy storage operators with AI-powered tools for asset management, operational workflows, and financial oversight. Credit: enSights

enSights emphasized that people remain in control of the AI Team. Users determine which workflows the agents handle and where approvals are required. Physical asset controls remain governed by established EMS and site-level systems.

The AI Studio also builds on an expanded financial management offering within the EBM platform. Its Financial Asset Management Module includes revenue assurance, contract, and obligation management, and profit-and-loss reporting.

The module is intended to provide project-level financial visibility, helping companies identify and prevent revenue losses while generating reports for investors.

The platform also includes real-time EMS, weather and tariff forecasting, along with dispatch planning intended to optimize revenue while maintaining grid safety and local compliance.

enSights says its platform is deployed globally across more than 100,000 sites, representing over 5 GW of assets. The company plans to demonstrate the AI Studio and its broader EBM platform at RE+ 2026.

As AI gains traction across the clean energy industry, the next competitive advantage could come from connecting operational intelligence with the financial and contractual decisions that determine how successfully renewable energy portfolios perform.

#5640. Soneil Spark introduces its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System

The company, offering bespoke, modular energy solutions, is introducing Mobile BESS at RE+ 2026. It delivers temporary backup and off-grid power, working with what’s available by taking power from the grid, a generator, or solar.

“Wherever your business goes, the power goes with it. Simply tow it where energy demand grows,” said Sonya Jain, CEO of Soneil Spark, in a statement. “Start with one unit and add more as your needs change.”

On sites that already run generators, the Mobile BESS syncs with them to cover peaks and start-up gaps, so the generators run fewer hours. Where diesel is restricted, it offers a quiet alternative.

The Mobile BESS is built for generator and equipment rental, construction, emergency and backup power, utilities, electrical contractors, events, film and entertainment, and off-grid sites such as mining and agriculture. A hybrid option adds EV charging.

#5634. OMCO Solar offers solar-powered tracker control system

OMCO Solar now offers a version of its OMCO Star Tracker Control System powered entirely by solar energy, allowing tracker controls to operate without AC power at the site.

The system, first launched last year, is designed for OMCO Origin Factory-Direct Trackers. Its tracker controllers, array controllers and sensor bridges use all-weather battery packs charged by solar modules.

According to OMCO, the technology eliminates the need for AC power to tracker control components and an uninterruptible power supply. It also allows tracker controls to operate and protects modules against wind, snow and hail before AC power is available.

“Our industry has solved so many problems as we have grown – we’re now the leading source for new electric energy capacity in the US. This addition to the OMCO Star product line solves one more – how to protect modules if there’s no AC power to the site even for a short time. We’re excited to offer this solution to our existing customers and to new partners,” said Matt Kesler, vice president of solar technology at OMCO Solar, in a statement.

The OMCO Star system uses LoRa, or long-range, wireless technology with a communication range greater than 1 mile. According to OMCO, this reduces the number of network controllers required and allows direct communication with every tracker without relying on a mesh network.

The company says LoRa is 100 times less susceptible to electromagnetic noise than Zigbee and better able to connect to distant trackers despite obstacles.

OMCO says its all-weather battery packs allow the solar-powered controls to operate reliably in cold climates. The system includes field-replaceable batteries, a phone app that eliminates the need for buttons on controllers and a remote monitoring and control portal.

The portal allows operators to move trackers for activities such as mowing, snow plowing and module cleaning. The system is developed, manufactured and supported by OMCO Solar in the United States.

“We are excited about bringing this innovation to the Utility Scale and DG tracker markets. Over the past few years, OMCO has expanded its capabilities beyond just being a US steel manufacturer to now being a true technology leader in our space. We look forward to supporting our customers/partners with the OMCO Star Tracker Control System, improving reliability, and reducing the labor and materials needed to build their projects,” said Eric Goodwin, vice president of business development at OMCO Solar.

OMCO Solar will display the all-DC tracker control system at RE+ in Las Vegas, November 17–19, in the North Hall, booth N516.

#5625. RISE St. James, Sierra Club Sue LDEQ to Vacate Hyundai Steel Air Permit

LDEQ issued air permit to the facility despite serious flaws in permit.

Donaldsonville, Louisiana — Today, RISE St. James Louisiana and Sierra Club filed a lawsuit challenging Louisiana Department of Environmental Quality’s decision to issue an air permit to Hyundai-POSCO for its proposed steelmaking facility. The groups claim that the air permit must be vacated because LDEQ did not conduct an environmental justice analysis, nor did the agency give full and careful consideration to mitigation measures like electrified components and green hydrogen, or to the environmental costs of Hyundai’s added pollution in an already overburdened area, as is required by the Louisiana state constitution and the Clean Air Act.

Throughout 2026, Sierra Club, working with other experts and local groups, submitted a series of comments to LDEQ in an effort to bring to light the errors, miscalculations, and omissions in the permit application. Despite some improvements to the permit application, made by Hyundai Steel following public pressure, the final air permit issued by LDEQ authorizes the facility to emit substantial quantities of greenhouse gases, nitrogen oxides, particulate matter, carbon monoxide, lead, mercury, ammonia, and other pollutants. Many of these pollutants cause significant and irreversible damage to human health even at low levels.

The area around the planned facility already bears a toxic air pollution burden greater than 96% of the rest of the country, and ranks high across a range of environmental and socioeconomic indicators. The Hyundai Steel Plant is one of multiple polluting facilities planned as part of the 17,000-acre Riverplex MegaPark in an area known as Cancer Alley in Ascension Parish, Louisiana.

In response, the petitioners issued the following statements:

Andrea Issod, Sierra Club Senior Attorney, said: “LDEQ must ensure the Hyundai Steel Plant permit complies with the legal requirements to reduce harmful pollution impacts as much as possible on the surrounding community, which already bears a 96% percentile burden of toxic releases as compared to the national average. By refusing to conduct an environmental justice analysis, consider cleaner technologies like electrifying components and replacing dirty gas with green hydrogen, or consider any environmental costs of Hyundai’s pollution, LDEQ has failed to perform the most basic function of its role and deprived the community of a transparent, thorough, and accurate assessment of the facility’s potential negative impacts. For that reason, we are asking the Court to reverse LDEQ’s final decision and vacate the permit for Hyundai Steel’s proposed facility.”

Mekaylyn Lavigne, Modeste Coordinator for RISE St. James, said: “I was born and raised in the small and peaceful community of Modeste. Although this region is now known as Cancer Alley because many chemical plants have polluted our air and water, my family and neighbors still enjoy sitting on our porches, watching birds and wildlife, and fishing. Now, LDEQ is allowing a steel mill to move in next door to my home, and tells us the air quality is fine and this massive iron and steel making facility will not have any negative impacts. Modeste residents deserve the truth. The court should make LDEQ go back to the drawing board on this permit to disclose all the impacts and use the cleanest technologies to reduce pollution.”

#5623. Is the Energy Crisis Accelerating the Clean Energy Transition? 4 Takeaways from the IEA’s World Energy Outlook 2026

Energy expert Nick Wayth unpacks findings from this year’s report—and what needs to happen next.

The International Energy Agency has published its World Energy Outlook 2026 amid an unprecedented global energy crisis that is reshaping how countries think about energy security. The report finds that the push to reduce dependence on imported fossil fuels is accelerating electrification and the shift toward domestic energy sources, including renewables. Yet electricity demand is surging, grids are struggling to keep pace, and concerns about energy security are also giving coal a longer life in some countries. The IEA warns that the pursuit of energy security alone won’t deliver the emissions reductions needed to meet global climate goals.

What do these findings mean for the energy transition, and where should policymakers focus their efforts? Nick Wayth, global director of the WRI Polsky Center for the Global Energy Transition, explains four key takeaways from this year’s report—and what needs to happen next.

Q: How close will current and proposed policies get us to the COP31 pledge to raise electricity’s share of final energy consumption to 35% by 2035? What changes are needed to bridge the gap?

The crisis in the Strait of Hormuz has reinforced the case for electrification as a way for countries to diversify their power sources and reduce dependence on imports, improving energy security and affordability. While the world’s reliance on fossil fuels hasn’t changed substantially, there’s been a real shift in how countries think about the risks of that dependence—and the benefits of electrification. But progress is too slow. The IEA projects that under current and proposed policies, electricity will account for only 29% of final energy consumption by 2035. That’s only half the increase needed to reach the COP31 target of 35%.

Much of the technology is already available. The IEA estimates that nearly half of fossil fuel demand in road transport and more than a third of demand for low- and medium-temperature industrial heat could be electrified at a competitive cost today. The challenge is creating the conditions for businesses and households to make that switch.

To reach the target, countries need to develop detailed plans for shifting transport, heating, and heavy industry toward electricity and invest in transmission and distribution. Governments can help keep their industries competitive by making it more attractive for businesses to switch from gas to affordable electricity. Some countries are doing this successfully already. Germany, for example, has reduced electricity taxes for manufacturers and has subsidized grid charges, helping lower electricity costs for businesses.

Q: The IEA projects rapid growth in electricity demand from both air conditioning and data centers. How do the challenges posed by these two sources of demand differ?

Energy demand from AI and data centers has dominated public attention this year, but by 2035 the global growth in electricity demand from cooling is forecast to be about 70% greater than from data centers. Notably, the IEA finds that global demand for cooling buildings is overtaking demand for heating them for the first time in history.

Energy demand from data centers is concentrated in the U.S., China, and the EU. By contrast, demand for cooling is widespread, especially in emerging and developing economies where much of the population is vulnerable to extreme heat, an increasing threat to human life.

Under current and proposed government policies, cooling is projected to account for 12% of global electricity consumption by 2035. But during periods of peak demand, when millions of air conditioners are running at once, it could reach around 30% of the electricity needed. Electricity grids must be able to cope with these spikes in demand, even if they occur only occasionally. Without better planning and more efficient cooling, countries could face costly grid upgrades—or risk power outages during extreme heat.

This is why policymakers and utilities need to anticipate future electricity demand and plan investments in grid infrastructure accordingly. But building more isn’t the only solution. Governments and utilities should also invest in energy storage and other grid flexibility measures that enable them to adapt to fluctuating supply and demand as needed.

Q: The energy crisis is accelerating clean electrification in some countries but also encouraging greater reliance on fossil fuels in others. What does the IEA’s outlook tell us about the future of oil, coal, and gas—and the risks of investing in new fossil fuel infrastructure?

The report shows that global oil demand is expected to level off by 2030 under current and proposed policies. The slowdown in China’s oil demand, driven largely by electric vehicle adoption, is expected to continue. Other fast-growing economies, such as India, despite electrifying transport, are nonetheless projected to increase their demand for oil.

The current energy crisis is reinforcing these differences in strategy. Some countries are investing in electrification to reduce dependence on imported fossil fuels, while others are turning to domestic fossil fuels.

The energy crisis has given coal demand a short-term boost, and the IEA now expects coal use in 2035 to be higher than it projected just a year ago. Even so, under current and proposed government policies, global coal demand is expected to fall by 14% between 2025 and 2035, driven largely by a decline in coal-fired power generation, particularly in China. But coal use in industry is projected to fall by just 5%, highlighting the challenge of reducing dependence on the fuel.

Countries need to be cautious about investing in infrastructure that may not be needed. The IEA estimates that about 400 billion cubic meters (bcm) of new liquefied natural gas (LNG) export capacity is under construction and could come online by 2035. Yet under current and stated government policies, export capacity could significantly exceed demand in 2035 by70 bcm. That surplus raises the risk of underused infrastructure and investments that fail to pay off.

The IEA estimates that the world will need at least 25 million kilometers of new power lines over the next decade to meet growing electricity demand and connect new sources of power, including renewables. Another 15 million kilometers of existing lines will need to be upgraded or replaced. Both will require substantial investment.

The biggest challenge is delivering these changes fast enough. Major grid projects can take 5 to15 years to plan, secure permits for, and complete—far longer than many renewable energy projects. Governments need to speed up permitting and grid connections, improve coordination between utilities and developers, and encourage investment before demand materializes. One encouraging example is the EU’s recent provisional agreement to streamline permitting for grid projects.

Equally important is to invest in ways to use existing assets more efficiently. Grid-enhancing technologies can speed up connections by allowing existing power lines to carry more electricity, reducing the need to build new infrastructure. And increasingly, governments and utilities are seeing the importance of making grids more flexible—building in capability and strategies to balance supply with demand in real time. Brazil, for example, pays large electricity users to temporarily reduce consumption when the grid is under strain. Businesses that fail to deliver the agreed reductions can lose payments or face suspension from programs. By combining energy storage, demand-response programs, smart grid controls, distributed energy generation, and other flexibility strategies, energy planners can meet growing demands and increase resilience while reducing the need to build more grid infrastructure.

Courtesy of WRI. By Sarah George, Senior Manager, Strategic Communications, WRI Polsky Center for the Global Energy Transition; and Francisco Boshell, Global Lead, Decarbonized Energy Consumption, WRI Polsky Center for the Global Energy Transition.

WRI is a global research organization that spans more than 50 countries, with offices in Brazil, China, Europe, India, Indonesia, and the United States. Our more than 450 experts and staff work closely with leaders to turn big ideas into action to sustain our natural resources—the foundation of economic opportunity and human well-being. Find out more at www.wri.org

#5621. Yozma IN 11 Review — Offroad Dirt Biking with 4800W Peak Power

As a teen, I loved riding — anything, my skateboard, my bike, whatever had wheels and I could get moving on, I was there for it. Then I spent a few years racing mountain bikes in the NORBA circuit when I was in my mid-20’s. I was never very good — always pretty mediocre, in fact, but I’ll be damned if it wasn’t some of the funnest experiences I’ve ever had. The feel of a trail, the woods, cruising through nature … it’s intoxicating. It always came with a lot of work — mountain biking pre e-bikes was a frikkin workout. Pedaling a bike up a combined 3,000 feet worth of climbing during a race would knock me out for days. If only I was in my teen years or 20s now — LOL.

With bikes like the Yozma IN 11, you can cruise mountain trails at speeds up to 45 mph, and do it completely powered by solar energy, to boot. Compared with noisy, smelly gas bikes, the Yozma can cruise on sunshine and offers the quintessential instant torque of the all-electric motor, making racing competitions with gas bikes an absolute joke — the Yozma will crush them.

Check out the lineup of Yozma bikes here.

The Yozma combines some impressive stats into one good looking dirt bike capable of handling a lot of challenging terrain:

It also has a number of other features, including wheelie assist, reverse gear and high visibility brake lights.

I think one of the things I’m most floored by is the price. My full suspension mountain bike I used to pedal up hills in the mountains of southwest Virginia as a kid cost about $1500. So does this all-electric dirt bike capable of going 44 mph and helping me do assisted wheelies to impress the ladies. I mean … wtf.

Yozma has an official Amazon store, so you can get yours through that retailer if you like.

#5617. How Can Energy Improve Lives? Why Counting Connections Isn’t Enough

Global tracking of energy access should expand beyond connections to include affordability, reliability, adequacy, and development outcomes.

Access to energy is a cornerstone of development—it can improve health, education, incomes, and overall quality of life.

But access is only the first step. A health clinic can’t perform surgery if frequent power cuts make its equipment unreliable. An electric irrigation pump is unlikely to raise a farmer’s income if crops can’t reach buyers. And a small business may gain little from electricity if it lacks financing to buy the equipment it needs to expand.

For energy to improve people’s lives, it must be affordable, sufficient, and reliable—and people and businesses must be able to put it to productive use.

The United Nations Sustainable Development Goal 7 (SDG 7) calls for “universal access to affordable, reliable, and modern energy services” by 2030. But the two indicators used to track its universal-access target focus only on whether people have access to electricity and whether they primarily rely on clean fuels and technologies. They do not address reliability, affordability, or economic impacts.

Other approaches already offer a fuller picture. The World Bank’s Energy Sector Management Assistance Program developed the Multi-Tier Framework to measure electricity access beyond connections, including whether electricity is available when needed, adequate for the equipment people want to use, reliable and affordable. While the framework is used by some countries to assess these broader dimensions of energy access, such measures are not the default, as many countries rely on the U.N.’s official global indicators.

As governments consider what should follow the SDGs after 2030, they should make multidimensional energy-access measures a regular part of national surveys and use the results to track progress globally. Since access alone does not necessarily deliver better livelihoods, improved measures of access could also be paired with indicators of whether energy is delivering the development outcomes it is intended to support. These could include whether clinics and schools can function as intended, farmers can increase yields and incomes and businesses can grow revenues and employment.

Together, these measures could shift energy planning from the largely supply-side question of how many people have access toward the demand-side questions of what people and institutions need energy for and whether the energy system enables them to meet these needs.

The examples below show why looking beyond connections matters in practice.

National electrification rates can mask large differences in where electricity is available within a country. Addressing those gaps requires looking beyond national averages to identify the households, businesses, and public facilities that lack access.

In 2023, WRI, working closely with Uganda’s Ministry of Health, used WRI’s Energy Access Explorer (EAE)—an online mapping tool that combines energy data with information on infrastructure, public services, and economic activity—to assess the electricity needs of health facilities. The analysis found that 675 facilities serving more than 3 million people had no electricity, either grid or off-grid, yet all were in areas with significant solar energy potential. The analysis helped identify places where solar power could fill the energy gap.

In Ethiopia, the Ministry of Water and Energy and the World Bank used EAE to help design a financing program for off-grid electricity. The analysis identified 145 priority woredas, or districts, where investments could reach more than 19 million people. Having this location-based data helped translate national energy-access goals into decisions about where public financing should be directed.

Ethiopia also illustrates why simple access figures can overstate the level of service people receive. A 2025 national energy-access survey found that 65% of households had access to at least one source of electricity. However, just 44% of the population received at least a basic level of electricity service, enough for uses such as lighting and charging a phone for at least four hours a day.

Simply having an electricity connection is not enough if the supply lacks the capacity or voltage stability to operate the equipment people need.

In the Gumla district in Jharkhand, India, villages that had gained household grid connections still lacked enough power for many businesses to function. WRI found that the single-phase electricity supplied was insufficient for productive uses such as milling and oil pressing. Beginning in 2015, Mlinda Charitable Trust, a nonprofit, installed solar mini-grids that supplied three-phase electricity, allowing local enterprises to run equipment, including rice and wheat hullers, oil expellers, flour mills, and pulverizers.

Governments should assess not only whether communities are connected but also whether the electricity supplied can meet the capacity and power-quality requirements of the services and equipment people need—and plan grid upgrades or complementary systems accordingly.

A connection provides limited benefit if households, businesses, or public facilities cannot pay for enough energy to meet their needs. Measuring meaningful access therefore also means asking whether the energy is affordable for the people and institutions expected to use it.

In Ethiopia’s Berbere district, farmers initially used petrol-powered pumps to irrigate their fields, but high fuel and maintenance costs meant these were too expensive for many to keep using. Petrol cost $91 to $203 per hectare a year, while repairs and the cost of transporting fuel added further expenses.

WRI and the Danish nonprofit DanChurchAid later installed two solar-powered pumps serving 48 farming households. The solar systems have no fuel costs, while maintenance costs are less than $10 a year, compared with more than $1,300 for petrol pumps. But the two pumps’ combined $19,200 upfront cost was beyond what most farmers could afford out of pocket. Governments and funders should therefore assess both the ongoing cost of using energy and the upfront cost of the technologies needed to use it, and provide financing or targeted support where high initial costs remain a barrier.

Being connected to the grid does not mean electricity will be available whenever it is needed. WRI researchers documented power cuts lasting three to 12 hours a day at two health facilities in Jharkhand, with outages worsening during the monsoon season. The facilities relied on diesel generators and small battery backups during outages.

In 2017, after solar photovoltaic systems and larger batteries were installed, the facilities were able to keep wards and offices running and power other essential equipment during grid outages, reducing their reliance on grid electricity.

Tracking the frequency and duration of outages would give a clearer picture of whether electricity is dependable enough to support essential services.

Even when energy is available, adequate, affordable, and reliable, it may not translate into better livelihoods. People also need equipment, financing, and access to markets to put energy to productive use.

In Uganda, WRI Africa and partners used EAE to identify $15 million in potential investments in renewable energy technologies, including solar-powered irrigation systems and equipment for cooling and milling, that could benefit more than 28,000 farmers. Some organizations identified through that work have since secured funding for related projects. Masaka District Farmers Association, for example, received an IKI Small Grants award for an irrigation project that equipped 150 hectares of coffee-growing land with drip irrigation and worked with 1,500 smallholder farmers.

In Berbere, solar-powered irrigation has enabled farmers to harvest three to four times a year and grow higher-value crops. In their first season, many farmers earned four to 10 times as much from onion, pepper, and cabbage sales as they had previously earned in a year from staple crops.

Tracking these outcomes alongside measures of energy access can help show whether investments are translating into higher productivity and incomes.

Making greater use of existing multidimensional measures, alongside complementary indicators of development outcomes, could change not just how governments report progress but also how they plan and direct investment.

Rather than starting with how many new connections a project can deliver, governments and funders would ask what people and institutions need energy for, what prevents them from using it effectively, and which investments could remove those barriers. In some places, that may mean extending electricity supply. In others, it could mean improving reliability, lowering costs, or helping farmers and businesses obtain the equipment and financing needed to use energy productively.

Energy is essential to improving human well-being, but it is only one part of what drives development. Like water, health care or education and energy is necessary but not sufficient. Treating access as a starting point rather than an end goal—and measuring whether people can use, afford, and rely on the energy they receive—would give governments a clearer picture of whether energy investments are improving people’s lives.

Courtesy of WRI. Experts: Dimitris Mentis, Harsha Meenawat

WRI is a global research organization that spans more than 50 countries, with offices in Brazil, China, Europe, India, Indonesia, and the United States. Our more than 450 experts and staff work closely with leaders to turn big ideas into action to sustain our natural resources—the foundation of economic opportunity and human well-being. Find out more at www.wri.org

#5613. SolarEdge unveils U.S.-made optimizer for C&I solar

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 17, 2026

SolarEdge’s new Duo Power Optimizer promises to simplify commercial and industrial solar installations with 50% fewer optimizer units, independent module-level power tracking, and enhanced safety features.

The commercial and industrial (C&I) solar market is getting a new hardware option aimed at making installations simpler without sacrificing system performance. SolarEdge Technologies has unveiled its new Duo Power Optimizer, a U.S.-manufactured solution designed to deliver module-level energy harvesting and monitoring while cutting the number of power optimizers in half.

Announced November 16 ahead of RE+ 2026 in Las Vegas, the new product introduces a 2:1 architecture that allows one optimizer to connect two solar modules. Notably, each module retains its own independent maximum power point tracker (MPPT), which preserves the module-level performance benefits associated with the traditional one-to-one optimizer configurations.

For developers, EPCs and installers managing large-scale rooftop and distributed generation projects, the value here lies in fewer components to purchase, wire and install, with no trade-off in module-level optimization.

Simplifying installation without sacrificing performance

SolarEdge’s optimization tech addresses the challenge of conventional string inverter systems, which is that an underperforming module can affect the output of an entire string. But by allowing each module to operate independently, the system limits the impact of shading, degradation or performance differences between panels.

The Duo brings demonstrates that capability in a two-module configuration. By reducing the number of optimizers by 50% compared with one-to-one designs, SolarEdge is targeting an opportunity to streamline balance-of-system requirements and reduce the installation labor.

SolarEdge’s U.S.-made Duo Power Optimizer connects two solar modules while maintaining independent module-level power tracking for commercial and industrial solar installations. Credit: SolarEdge

The hardware also incorporates design changes intended to improve jobsite efficiency. Each optimizer features two input pairs: a shorter connection for the nearest module and a pre-extended connection for the adjacent module. The arrangement reduces excess wiring and eliminates the need for field-made jumpers.

The configuration also accommodates modules with different orientations, making it particularly relevant for east-west and dual-tilt racking applications.

Beyond installation efficiency, SolarEdge is emphasizing electrical safety as a central feature of the new product.

The Duo extends the company’s Sense Connect technology to its input and output connectors, monitoring connector temperatures to identify developing issues before they potentially escalate into overheating or electrical arcs.

Also, the optimizer introduces what SolarEdge describes as an industry-first approach to arc fault detection: every optimizer acts as an individual arc sensor. Rather than relying exclusively on detection at the inverter, the system distributes monitoring throughout the array, allowing potential arc faults to be identified closer to their source.

SolarEdge says the technology provides coverage across strings with up to 3,280 feet of total cable length between the inverter and optimizer. It can also help pinpoint problems to the optimizer area, potentially making troubleshooting and maintenance more efficient.

Manufactured at SolarEdge’s Florida facility, the Duo is designed to meet U.S. Domestic Content and Foreign Entity of Concern (FEOC) requirements, according to the company. SolarEdge says its domestic-content eligibility levels are consistent with those of its previous U.S.-manufactured power optimizers, subject to applicable tax guidance and project-specific requirements.

For SolarEdge, the Duo represents an effort to combine three priorities that are becoming more and more important in commercial solar: installation efficiency, system-level safety, and domestic manufacturing.

The technology does not fundamentally change the value proposition of module-level optimization. Instead, it looks to make that architecture more economical and easier to deploy at scale.

SolarEdge will showcase the Duo Power Optimizer at RE+ 2026.

#5611. New ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept: This Electric GTI Is Ready For The Racetrack

270 kW (367 PS), top speed of 240 km/h and 4.7 seconds from 0 to 100 km/h: With the ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept, Volkswagen is marking the 50th anniversary of the GTI by showing how uncompromising an electric GTI with motorsport ambitions can be. The concept car will be presented to the public next week at the Essen Motor Show (27 November to 6 December 2026). Its performance figures meet a two-door silhouette and flared wings as well as a large front splitter, diffuser and rear wing. The result: a powerful appearance and maximum downforce for fast laps. At the same time, the concept vehicle provides a preview of how the GTI idea can be further developed in the electric age.

Kai Grünitz, Member of the Board of Management for Technical Development, Volkswagen Brand and Brand Group Core: “The GTI has been one of Volkswagen’s great success stories for 50 years. This year, we launched our GTI as an electric vehicle in series production for the first time. With the ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept, we are showing how much more potential there is in the electric GTI world. Our platform strategy and MEB+ give us the technological freedom to make even extreme concepts possible.”

Designed for performance. The design of the new ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept is based on the production model, the 166 kW (226 PS) ID. Polo GTI. In both models, the drive power is transmitted to the front axle. In the 270 kW (367 PS) ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept, this power delivery is controlled – thanks in part to an electronically regulated front differential lock (VAQ) – so that the model reaches 100 km/h from a standstill in just 4.7 seconds with Launch Control. Like the drive system, the electrical infrastructure has also been adapted to the high performance of the ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept. The 52 kWh high-voltage battery can provide up to 290 kW at short notice, enabling a top speed of 240 km/h. The DCC (adaptive chassis control) has also been tuned for use on the racetrack and ensures precise handling and high traction in conjunction with the front differential lock. The new ceramic brakes of the one-box brake system are also designed for high performance.

Dynamics are visible in the design. On the exterior, the ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept follows the powerful clarity of the ID. Polo GTI production model, while at the same time incorporating design elements that underline the motorsport characteristics. Particularly striking features are the two-door instead of four-door silhouette, pronounced flaring of the wings by 50 millimetres in each case, a large motorsport splitter at the front, a wider rear end, a new diffuser and a rear wing in carbon fibre that protrudes above the roof line.

Two-seater. Reduced to racing. The interior is also tailored to the racetrack, and only the basic layout of the overall cockpit has been adopted from the production model. This means there is no infotainment system, for example. The focus is solely on ergonomics and driving. The driver and front passenger sit on Recaro racing bucket seats with red 5-point harnesses. The rear and luggage compartment have been completely cleared. Instead of a rear seat system, a rollover bar painted in the GTI colour Tornado Red is integrated above and behind the front seats. The dash panel, side trims and many other details are covered in dark ArtVelours microfibre fabric with red topstitching – work that has been carried out with great precision by Volkswagen trainees. In many areas – in keeping with Volkswagen tradition – the young employees were directly involved in realisation of the concept vehicle by the Development department and designers.

550 instead of 290 newton-metres. The ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept features the high-torque APP550 motor. The special technical challenge here: this motor is actually intended for the rear of large models such as the ID. Tiguan or ID.7. It therefore had to be fitted into the smaller ID. Polo as front-wheel drive and additionally combined with an electronic front differential lock. Thomas Schmall, Member of the Board of Management Volkswagen AG for Technology, and CEO of Volkswagen Group Components: “The ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept shows just how emotive electric mobility can be: This is made possible by our APP550, one of the most efficient electric drives in its class. Manufactured over one million times at the Group Components plant in Kassel, this drive was actually developed for larger vehicle classes. Integrating it into the Polo required millimetre precision and is a powerful example of engineering skill and teamwork at Volkswagen, from Development and Design through to our trainees who worked on the project.”

GTI from the master of modular platforms. The ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept is based on the modular electric drive (MEB+) platform with front-wheel drive. Volkswagen has used the basic technical layout of the production model and taken it to a new level of performance with a 104 kW (141 PS) more powerful electric drive. The result is the most dynamic interpretation of a front-wheel drive ID. model to date. At the other end of the scale is the Mission Efficiency, which is also based on the ID. Polo and set four world records as a particularly efficient near-production electric vehicle. Both concept vehicles show the large bandwidth offered by electric drive systems from maximum sportiness to outstanding efficiency.

2026 is the year of the GTI. Following the Golf GTI EDITION 50, the new ID.3 GTI (sales start early 2027) and the new ID. Polo GTI (available to order since October), the ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept is making its debut this year as the fourth new model under the umbrella of the iconic GTI product brand. No other compact sports car family has been as successful worldwide for half a century now – with more than 2.5 million units sold – as the GTI models.

#5607. energyRe Agreed to Sell 100% of a Portfolio of Utility-Scale Solar Projects in South Carolina to Enel Green Power North America, Totaling 317 MWdc

energyRe Will Continue as Construction Manager Through Commercial Operation; Sale Reflects Strength of the Company’s South Carolina Development Platform; Transaction Expected to Close in First Quarter of 2027

energyRe today announced that it has agreed to sell 100% of a portfolio of utility-scale solar projects in South Carolina to Enel Green Power North America, Inc. (EGPNA). The projects have a combined total capacity of roughly 317 MWdc of new generating capacity across the Palmetto State. The transaction is expected to close in the first quarter of 2027, subject to customary conditions, including required U.S. regulatory approvals.

“This transaction is a strong validation of the projects we have built in South Carolina and of the team that developed them,” said Ryan Brown, Chief Operating Officer at energyRe. “Enel Green Power is a world-class owner and operator, and we are pleased to hand these projects to a partner that shares our commitment to delivering reliable, affordable power to South Carolina homes and businesses. Our team will remain on the ground as construction manager to see these projects through to commercial operation.”

As part of the transaction, energyRe will continue to serve as construction manager through commercial operation.

energyRe has broken ground on approximately 450 MW in the last year and has nearly 700 MW in operation or late-stage development. The company’s 1.8+ GW portfolio spans 20 projects in South Carolina, North Carolina, Nebraska, and Oklahoma.
