#5606. The grid’s hidden bottleneck: engineering capacity

The power system is undergoing an unprecedented transformation. Many of the reasons driving that change are well recognized and frequently discussed. Electricity demand from data centers, manufacturing and the electrification of transportation and building heating and cooling is surging. In fact, earlier this year the North American Electric Reliability Corporation (NERC) warned that grid reliability risks were increasing because demand was outpacing the new generation and transmission needed to serve it.

Another challenge: the grid is old. The most recent American Society of Civil Engineers’ Infrastructure Report Card gave the nation’s energy system a D+, in part because of the age of the grid. Utilities have responded to an aging grid and skyrocketing electricity demand by making significant investments. The Edison Electric Institute (EEI) recently forecast that investor-owned utilities would spend $1.4 trillion through 2030 to meet customer demand for power.

However, increased capital investment does not automatically translate into increased project execution. While funding, equipment availability and construction resources are critical, the engineering processes required to move projects from planning into construction represent another significant constraint. Utilities must not only invest in additional infrastructure but also rethink how that infrastructure is engineered and delivered.

Utilities are under pressure to move more projects through engineering and into construction, but experienced engineering resources are increasingly difficult to secure. While utilities can expand internal teams or engage additional engineering, procurement and construction (EPC) partners, scaling traditional engineering processes often introduces additional challenges. Different design tools, inconsistent engineering standards and repetitive manual work can limit productivity and increase coordination demands. That leaves utilities with a pressing question: how can they execute significantly more capital projects without a proportional increase in engineering resources?

Substation engineering provides a compelling example. While utilities have extensive engineering standards and established design practices, many continue to rely on traditional drawing-based workflows, manual design modifications and disconnected engineering information, particularly in protection and control engineering, where changes can impact multiple interconnected drawings and documents. These processes can become increasingly difficult to scale as project volumes grow, particularly when coordinating across internal engineering teams and external EPC partners.

Engineering doesn’t have to slow down important capital projects. Standardized, data-driven engineering can help utilities move faster while maintaining robust standards at scale. Reusable engineering libraries, automated documentation and connected digital design models streamline engineering work, improve consistency and productivity and reduce repetitive tasks common in traditional workflows, allowing utilities to reuse proven designs rather than repeatedly engineering similar systems from scratch.

Software plays a critical role in navigating the multi-stage, multi-stakeholder complexity of power infrastructure projects, including substations. Eplan, a global provider of electrical engineering software, has spent the past few decades helping utilities and manufacturers standardize their electrical system design and documentation. Its shared digital platform gives utilities, EPCs, panel builders and equipment manufacturers access to the same engineering data, from planning to long-term operations. That shared data supports every phase of a project:

Like many distribution grid operators, naturenergie netze in Germany faces a confluence of challenges. Demand from heat pumps and electric vehicles (EVs) is climbing. At the same time, Germany is working to generate about 80 percent of its electricity from renewables like wind and solar by 2030, which means the grid must handle growing two-way power flows.

For naturenergie netze, that means quickly modernizing existing substations and building new ones. Standardization is central to how the utility plans to keep pace. It began by using Eplan to standardize how it engineers the control systems inside its substations. That groundwork laid the foundation for the utility, Eplan and software supplier entegra to build a digital twin of a highly complex substation, which the utility is using to plan a full replacement of the substation’s control systems while it stays in service.

“It would normally take two to three years to plan and implement the modernization, but the new planning methodology will really speed things up,” said Rainer Beck, grid development coordinator for naturenergie netze. The approach also demonstrates how standardized engineering practices can lay the foundation for greater efficiency and design reuse in future substation projects.

Standardized design has even more value when it’s paired with standardized hardware and highly integrated production tools. Rittal, Eplan’s sister company, manufactures modular power distribution systems and automated fabrication equipment that can work directly from Eplan’s engineering data to extend efficiency directly to the production floor. It’s a combination that supports speed and quality across design, build and delivery.

Building on its experience in Europe, Eplan is now supporting North American utilities and their engineering partners in modernizing protection and control engineering workflows, establishing scalable engineering standards and improving engineering productivity to support expanding capital programs with a growing USA-based manufacturing and warehousing footprint.

The grid is being transformed from how power is generated to how it flows, but the engineering processes behind it must also change. Modernizing them is how utilities can turn unprecedented investment into completed projects without needing an equally unprecedented number of engineers.

Accelerate grid infrastructure: Eplan software and Rittal hardware and automation enable growth, scalability and speed — all with One System.

#5604. Mission Solar Energy puts supply chain transparency in the spotlight at RE+ 2026

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 18, 2026

With new high-efficiency residential and commercial modules and an integrated supply chain spanning polysilicon to power conversion, Mission Solar Energy is making a case for U.S. manufacturing, domestic content and FEOC-compliant sourcing.

As the solar industry continues to navigate shifting trade policies, domestic content incentives, and supply chain scrutiny, Mission Solar Energy is bringing a message of manufacturing strength and transparency to RE+ 2026. The San Antonio-based manufacturer showcased its latest commercial and residential modules while highlighting the advantages of its relationship with fellow OCI Holdings subsidiaries OCI TerraSus and OCI Power.

The announcement puts the spotlight on a growing priority for solar developers, installers and distributors: knowing exactly where components originate and having the documentation to prove it.

The three companies collectively cover significant portions of the solar value chain. OCI TerraSus produces high-purity, solar-grade polysilicon in Sarawak, Malaysia; Mission Solar designs, engineers, and assembles modules in San Antonio, Texas; and OCI Power manufactures inverters and power conversion systems in South Korea.

According to Mission Solar, all three companies are compliant with Foreign Entity of Concern (FEOC) requirements and are not classified as prohibited foreign entities. The integrated structure offers customers access to a traceable supply chain backed by OCI Holdings, a global clean energy group headquartered in Seoul.

For an industry that’s becoming more and more focused on sourcing eligibility and domestic manufacturing credentials, that positioning could prove commercially important.

On the product front, Mission Solar is highlighting two modules aimed at different market segments.

For commercial and industrial (C&I) applications, the company is featuring its 595W N-Type Bifacial 144HC module. The dual-glass design captures sunlight from both sides, potentially increasing energy production without requiring additional project footprint. Designed and assembled in the United States, the module carries 11.5% domestic content, meets FEOC requirements and supports front-side loads of up to 5,400 Pa.

Residential installers will get a look at the 440W N-Type 108HC rooftop module, which Mission Solar says will be the first of its kind on the market with 12% domestic content. Building on an existing 6% domestic-content version, the module combines N-type cell technology and a multi-busbar design to deliver efficiency of up to 23%. It is also approved on residential financing platforms including LightReach and GoodLeap.

Looking further ahead, Mission Solar plans to preview two next-generation G12R modules, with commercial launches anticipated in late 2027. The company will also display a lower-domestic-content U.S.-produced C&I module option expected in 2027.

Although module performance is still critical to the industry, supply chain credibility is increasingly part of the overall purchasing decision.

As developers evaluate project economics, tax credit eligibility and sourcing risk, manufacturers that can offer transparent documentation alongside competitive technology may have an advantage.

#5602. JA Energy Storage Launches an Internal Battery Passport Platform to Facilitate Compliance with EU Standards

JJA ESS has launched its own battery passport data platform to support lifecycle information management and help companies prepare for upcoming EU regulations. The platform is hosted in the company’s own cloud environment, giving the team direct responsibility for data policies, access permissions, and future updates.

According to the EU Battery Regulation, from February 18, 2027, battery passports are mandatory for batteries used in light vehicles (LMTs), electric vehicles, and industrial batteries with a capacity exceeding 2 kWh that are placed on the market or put into service. Further requirements will follow.

A battery passport is a digital data record linked to a single physical battery. A QR code and a unique identifier based on the ISO/IEC 15459 series connect the data record to the battery, enabling access to information throughout its entire lifecycle. This can include product identity, material composition, CO₂ footprint, recycled content, performance and durability, condition, supply chain due diligence, and end-of-life disposal. Taken together, these data points support consistent information management from product identification to the end of the product lifecycle.

For manufacturers, the challenge lies not simply in creating a QR code. The data must be traceable back to its source, up-to-date, and accessible in accordance with regulatory and business requirements. Companies must also manage access to sensitive information and comply with regulations governing cross-border data transfers and trade secrets. This balance is particularly relevant for companies operating in multiple markets. When companies utilize a third-party platform, its functionality, access to data fields, and interoperability can be determined by the provider’s configuration and service model.

JA ESS developed the platform internally and hosts it in its own cloud environment. Data policies, access permissions, and future platform updates are managed by the JA ESS internal team. This team ensures compliance with EU regulations regarding data access, presentation, and updates, while also giving JA ESS greater control over data management. The platform can also evolve as regulatory requirements change and real-world applications provide further insights.

JA ESS is the energy storage business unit under the JA brand. As a central component of JA’s energy storage strategy, JA ESS offers a wide range of FTM and BTM applications with safe, efficient, intelligent, and customized solutions for the large-scale, commercial, and industrial sectors as well as the residential market.

#5601. FERC approves MISO plan to speed legacy interconnection study process

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Without the rule changes, the Midcontinent Independent System Operator’s queue cycles — totaling about 125 GW of generation and storage — would have been delayed, according to the grid operator.

The Federal Energy Regulatory Commission on Friday approved the Midcontinent Independent System Operator’s plan for clearing out its legacy generation and storage interconnection queue, in part by giving project developers a chance to withdraw nonviable projects without paying a penalty.

The plan is designed to speed up MISO’s review of its “Definitive Planning Phase–2022” interconnection queue, which contains about 35 GW of active projects. Without the approved changes, that queue cycle would have been delayed by roughly 18 months, affecting other pending study cycles, according to MISO.

In total, MISO has almost 600 projects representing nearly 125 GW in its interconnection queue, which consists of four study cycles. The queue contains 44 GW of solar, 25 GW of batteries, about 20 GW of wind, 19 GW of gas and 10 GW of hybrid projects.

“MISO’s proposed tariff revisions accomplish the purposes of the commission’s final rules on generator interconnection … by helping to ensure that interconnection customers are able to interconnect to the transmission system in a reliable, efficient, transparent, and timely manner,” FERC said.

MISO’s rules for its 2022 study cycle — which was not affected by more recent interconnection process reforms — created incentives for nonviable projects to remain in the interconnection queue because by doing so they avoid potential withdrawal penalties, according to the grid operator. This can lead to withdrawals later in the process, which trigger restudies that delay the process for everyone in the queue, MISO said in its Sept. 21 proposal.

To eliminate that problem, the proposal for the 2022 queue cycle would allow projects to exit the interconnection queue penalty-free before “Decision Point II,” which occurred on Oct. 20. MISO’s plan also limits the number of restudies performed in Definitive Planning Phase Phase III to a single restudy for the DPP-2022 cycle and all future DPP cycles.

FERC approved MISO’s plan with an Oct. 19 effective date. Some details remain outstanding pending FERC decisions in two other pending dockets at the agency.

In its decision, FERC approved MISO’s plan to raise the bar for allowing a penalty-free exit later in the review process. Currently, a project can drop out of the queue without penalties if MISO finds it faces a 35% network upgrade cost increase. The proposal increased that threshold to 50%.

FERC rejected arguments from Ranger Power, a renewable energy developer, that MISO’s penalty-free withdrawal proposal rewards unwanted behavior and is discriminatory.

“MISO has demonstrated a need to expeditiously process its queue and reduce the risk of cascading withdrawals and restudies that have delayed previous queue cycles,” FERC said.

FERC also dismissed an assertion from Pathway Power, a clean energy developer, that MISO failed to justify the increased 50% threshold for penalty-free withdrawal. The threshold is consistent with FERC’s approval of a 50% threshold for MISO’s queue process starting with the 2023 study cycle and it doesn’t unfairly burden small project developers, the agency said.

#5592. Six new ways to simplify solar installs: Top Rack Technology brings mounting innovation to RE+ 2026

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 18, 2026

From smarter waterproofing to 360-degree rail adjustment, Top Rack Technology is putting installer efficiency, rooftop flexibility, and cost control at the center of its latest product lineup.

As the solar industry continues to look for ways to reduce installation costs and streamline residential projects, mounting hardware is getting a fresh dose of innovation. Top Rack Technology, Inc. (TRT) is making that case at RE+ 2026, where the solar racking manufacturer is showcasing six new mounting solutions designed to simplify rooftop installations, improve flexibility, and help installers work more efficiently.

Its latest lineup reflects a practical approach to product development: addressing everyday installation challenges while keeping system performance, aesthetics, and affordability in focus.

Six Products, One Common Goal: Better Installation Efficiency

Among the new offerings is TRT’s Full-Coverage Skirt System, which targets an important consideration in residential solar: appearance. By concealing exposed array edges, the system delivers a cleaner, more finished look that can help installations better complement residential properties.

The new TRT-G2 Bracket takes aim at installation simplicity and competitive pricing. Designed around practical field requirements, the bracket can be paired with TRT’s ButyLok waterproofing component, creating an integrated attachment and roof-sealing solution.

Waterproofing is particularly important in rooftop solar, where roof penetrations are still a critical consideration for installers and property owners alike. ButyLok introduces a shoe-like component containing pre-applied, gel-like butyl material. When compressed by the G2 bracket, the material spreads across the intended sealing surface, potentially reducing manual sealant application and simplifying the installation process.

TRT is also introducing SAVRIX, another residential attachment option developed with manufacturing efficiency, installation practicality, and cost competitiveness top of mind.. Meanwhile, the RF-2 Rail Fastener is designed to simplify rail-to-bracket connections.

Top Rack Technology’s FreePivot Bracket features a 360-degree rotatable rail fastener and built-in height adjustment to accommodate varying rooftop conditions and simplify solar panel installations. Credit: Top Rack Technology

But perhaps the most versatile addition is the FreePivot Bracket. Its 360-degree rotatable rail fastener allows installers to accommodate attachment points that aren’t perfectly aligned. Built-in height adjustment helps compensate for uneven roof surfaces, while its all-metal construction supports durability. The one-orientation design and single-socket installation concept are also intended to reduce tool changes and keep crews moving.

Beyond the six new products, TRT is showcasing its established portfolio, including the TRT 01 Rail-less Base System, TRT 02 Rail-Base System, and TRT 03 Tile Hook System.

The company is also highlighting its Online BOM Design Tool, which supports project setup, structural design inputs, module layouts, engineering verification, and automated bill-of-materials generation. Connecting digital planning with physical mounting solutions could offer installers and distributors a more streamlined path from project design to field execution.

Installation hardware doesn’t need to be flashy to make a difference. Improvements in attachment flexibility, waterproofing, assembly time, and aesthetics can translate into meaningful operational advantages.

#5590. Jackery & Every Electric Transform Urban Energy Storage With Plug-In VPP

Earlier this week, Jackery announced a strategic partnership with Brooklyn-based consumer energy aggregator Every Electric to offer the first plug-in Virtual Power Plant (VPP). The program starts with New York City apartment residents. I went to the RE+ in Las Vegas to meet with leaders from Jackery and Every Electric to learn more.

The plug-in VPP costs customers nothing but earns them money by turning their household appliances into grid stabilizing assets. For the customer, the process is simple:

That’s it. As Every Electric puts it: “Get paid to plug in.” Alternatively, a customer can buy the batteries for a discounted price through the program of $650 and receive a higher payback per year, up to $250 per battery. Over the 11-year rated lifespan, purchasing the battery could increase the total net payback, although the upfront cost would be a little higher.

It seems almost too simple and convenient for the customer. However, a lot of complex work and coordination is happening behind the scenes. The Jackery battery is in communication with Every Electric, who automates power management to charge when electricity demand is low and discharge to run the air conditioner when electricity demand is high. This kind of active demand response can go far beyond the typical TOU plan to directly mitigate utility demand shifts. Every Electric monitors data for each battery. The demand response of the combined storage across thousands of individual batteries is aggregated and sold to the utility, in this case Con Edison. Then the benefits are divided up and distributed to customers based on their contributions. Over 2000 batteries were in the initial trial phase, with a larger scale rollout planned next year. I am already on the waitlist, along with over 15,000 other NYC households.

Because it does not feed electricity back into the grid, implementation is much simpler. No electrician needed. The system monitors the amount of energy being stored and deployed at the point of use with no need for user intervention. Unlike traditional demand response that turns off appliances, there is no inconvenience to the user. Thermostats do not need to be adjusted. Smart appliances are not needed. The battery simply provides power when grid demand is high, shaving off the peaks.

You may still be asking “what’s the catch?” Currently, the system needs to coordinate a large number of relatively small batteries under the same utility to have enough combined capacity to sell to the utility. To start, it is only being offered in New York City, which sets the blueprint. In the initial application, the batteries will be used with 120V air conditioners, up to around 12,000 BTU. This is a common size for NYC apartments, letting one battery size serve a wide range of customers, simplifying initial rollout. It also offers clear return on investment based on how much electricity is used, when that electricity is used and when demand peaks for the utility. However, I wouldn’t expect it to stop there.

The batteries being used are led by the Jackery Explorer 2000 v2. This battery has 2042 Wh of capacity and up to 2200 W of output with 4400 W of surge power. A total of three outlets are on the front panel, as well as two USB-C and a USB-A port. Due to the UPS function, the portable power station adds protection and would be ideal to plug in a computer or sensitive electronics, in addition to the air conditioner. Although the VPP is based on air conditioner use, using all the outlets can potentially add to the amount of load shifted by the VPP and, as a result, increase the amount of payback for the customer. Plugging in other forms of energy consumption could also extend the benefit beyond the times of year when the air conditioner is being used.

At 40% smaller and 34% lighter than the previous generation model, the Jackery Explorer 2000 v2 weighs 39.5 lb. That’s less than a 5-gallon water cooler bottle but less bulky to carry. Due to the plug and play nature, people changing apartments within the city can easily take it with them at 13.2” x 10.4” x 11.5”. Multiple levels of safety protections and a ruggedized portable design using automotive-grade LFP batteries ensure safe operation even if apartment occupants bump into it.

That portability also contributes to the emergency preparedness capability of the battery. For the months of the year when the air conditioning isn’t running, the battery could provide backup power for a refrigerator in a power outage, for example. Of course, Jackery also offers batteries specifically for refrigerators. Those batteries are not a part of the initial program… yet.

This also gets to a limitation of the current program. To keep the program manageable as it scales up, only one size of battery in one use case is initially being offered. Right now, the biggest challenge is scaling up. Making it too complicated at the start would make scaling up more difficult. However, once the program reaches scale, more options can become available. Jackery also offers batteries with greater aesthetic appeal, like the slim FridgeGuard, which may be a better fit for the homes of some customers. As the program scales up, the applications have the potential to diversify.

Much of RE+ focused on supplying power and storage for data centers. While residential clean energy tax credits (25D) have been cut, data centers can still benefit from the Clean Electricity Investment Tax Credit that can add up with bonuses to cover half the cost of the project. With additional state and federal incentives piled on top, the majority of the cost of data center ESS can be covered by subsidies. With the heavily subsidized storage and large-scale contracts with the utility, data centers can access benefits not typically available to residential customers.

However, the plug-in VPP changes the energy storage dynamic. People get credit for energy storage deployment at the point of use. AI technology, rather than just being a consumer of resources, is being used to automate the VPP system and let the benefits flow to more families. By starting with small window air conditioners, the system also tends to provide relief to people living in typically older apartments, rather than the sleek new high rises with multi-million-dollar apartments.

There is no need for home electrical upgrades. In fact, by taking some of the load off the circuit, the potential for my wife to trip the breaker from ironing in the living room while the TV and air conditioner are running on the same breaker is reduced. As the energy monitoring comes directly from the battery, it also isn’t dependent on smart meters.

For the system to have enough total capacity to sell to the utility, the plug-in VPP needs to connect thousands of batteries to provide multiple MWh of storage. This scale can easily be accomplished in urban areas. In addition, the value of the system will heavily depend on the local price of electricity and the variability in price. In NYC, electricity is significantly more expensive than in most of the US.

New York City apartments are a perfect starting place. While apartments tend to be far more energy efficient than detached houses and have lower net emissions overall, many of us living in apartments have traditionally felt a bit left out on clean tech. We don’t have roofs for rooftop panels. We typically do not have attached garages to place large permanent batteries or access to modify the electrical panel. If we do have the ability to make electrical modifications to the apartment, they can be prohibitively expensive in NYC. As such, a plug-in VPP is an exciting development for those of us living in apartments.

A plug-in VPP also makes the benefits of battery energy storage more accessible to urban areas. Often, there is not room for container-sized batteries. In places like New York City, the fire regulations are so strict, and the permitting is so complicated that large-scale BESS becomes exceedingly difficult to execute. Large-scale BESS, if approved, could take years to deploy.

According to NREL, the national average full installed price of utility scale BESS was $334/kWh of capacity in 2024, with hardware making up roughly two thirds of the total. However, the cost would undoubtedly be higher in NYC. For comparison, the discounted Explorer 2000 v2 in this program (which avoids retail markup) comes in at $318.32/kWh, with no added permitting or installation cost. That is also significantly less than the installed cost of many home batteries used in traditional VPPs, like Tesla Powerwall. Once delivered, it can be online within minutes.

Currently, plug-in VPP is the fastest, easiest and least expensive way to add battery capacity that shaves peak demand on urban grids in the US.

As high draw usage like air conditioning increases, more stress is placed on the grid. Transformer and other electricity transmission and distribution infrastructure upgrades can be expensive. Having storage at the point of use reduces the amount of electricity that needs to flow through the grid during peak periods. As plug-in VPP is deployed more broadly, the benefits will be experienced more broadly. And CO2 emissions can drop in the process.

While the benefits of the program are already attractive, the initial program may just be the tip of the iceberg. In New York City, buildings over 25,000 square feet must reduce their greenhouse gas emissions under Local Law 97 (LL97). This includes many apartment complexes and offices. The deployment of batteries at the point of use has the potential to reduce emissions.

While the current batteries and air conditioners in the program are 120V, Jackery also makes batteries that are 240V. For appliances that have intermittent use but high draw, like heat pumps, hot water heaters, ranges, etc., 240V connections are often needed to provide the necessary power. The electrification of applications that were previously run on gas or fuel oil can greatly reduce building emissions. But many apartment buildings do not have 240V outlets available. However, larger Jackery models can charge on 120V, accumulate charge and discharge at 240V for intermittent use. There is potential for batteries to support the higher voltage electrification without expensive electrical upgrades while also providing VPP benefit. The math would have to work out and it hasn’t happened yet. But the potential is clearly there.

Overall, the potential applications for this approach are massive. Already, I can see the potential to transform energy storage in NYC. In talking with Jackery and Every Electric, I started thinking about where the technology could go from here. Plug-in VPP could apply far beyond just New York City. However, that is for a follow-up article.

Larry lives in Queens, NY, with his wife and cats. While he has spent much of his professional career leading global marketing efforts, his passions focus on clean technology and the automotive industry. He believes in Green Free Trade and the potential for the application of engineering talent to create solutions to the world’s most challenging problems.

#5587. Europe’s Modelled €5.70/kg Hydrogen Faces Industrial Buyers Who Can Afford Far Less

Europe’s industrial hydrogen plans have a buyer problem. A factory cannot make its products competitive by paying whatever a new hydrogen plant needs to recover its investment. Its maximum hydrogen price is set by the cost of making the same product, or delivering the same useful heat, another way.

The central 2050 scenario in my European hydrogen assessment puts on-site production at €5.70/kg. That starts with electricity at €60/MWh and 53 kWh of electricity per kilogram of hydrogen, so the electricity alone costs €3.18/kg. Electrolysers, capital recovery, maintenance, stack replacements, water and other plant costs bring the total to €5.70. Getting hydrogen to a customer can cost more.

At a factory using hydrogen for process heat, €5.70/kg is an expensive fuel. With 94% heating efficiency, delivering one MWh of useful heat requires roughly 31.9 kg of hydrogen. The fuel bill reaches €182/MWh before the burner, maintenance and other equipment costs. In the model’s central 2050 comparison, suitable direct-electric heating sets a hydrogen price ceiling of €2.07/kg. At that ceiling the same hydrogen heat costs about €66/MWh. The hydrogen production case overshoots the customer’s competitive input price by €3.63/kg.

The mismatch is not a prediction that hydrogen makers will never lower costs. It is the actual commercial test investors should apply before financing supply. Reducing the cost of production from €7 to €5 a kilogram achieves little if the relevant industrial customer can pay €2. Production-cost targets are meaningless without a corresponding market price ceiling.

The International Energy Agency’s Global Hydrogen Review 2026 has made customer cost acceptability part of its assessment. Across most sector and region comparisons, the IEA finds maximum acceptable hydrogen prices below US$2/kg without policy support. Some incumbent-steel comparisons yield negative acceptable prices. The IEA examines a 2035 horizon and a different set of competitors; the same buyer-side arithmetic exposes the problem.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment calculates the maximum hydrogen input price for industrial heat, ammonia and ironmaking and compares it with distinct European supply cases. It also links to the underlying European hydrogen research and interactive model.

European ammonia illustrates how demand for a product differs from demand for European-made hydrogen. Ammonia remains necessary for fertilisers and industrial chemicals. The buyer’s real choice is where to produce it. In the central 2050 TFIE model, making qualifying green ammonia inside Europe can compete with imported green ammonia only when its hydrogen input costs roughly €2.62–€4.00/kg, depending on the origin and production route. The €5.70/kg onsite supply case misses those thresholds. Europe can keep consuming ammonia without building every electrolyser locally.

Ironmaking has a similar sourcing decision. The model’s hydrogen input-price ceiling versus qualifying imported hydrogen-reduced or hot-briquetted iron ranges from €2.28 to €4.45/kg. Against a European electrochemical iron process, the central ceiling is approximately €1.43/kg. Where enough suitable scrap can deliver the required steel grade, the hydrogen-based route can lose even at a zero price for hydrogen. Steel quality, industrial integration and the commercial maturity of electrochemical iron matter to those comparisons. They do not entitle a hydrogen supplier to ignore the alternatives.

Hydrogen used as a chemical reagent deserves a different assessment from hydrogen burned for heat. Chemical production may need the molecule. Industrial heating needs temperature, pressure, reliability and useful energy, all of which can be delivered directly from electricity for many applications. Manufacturing hydrogen from electricity and then burning it adds conversion losses and fuel-system costs before the plant receives the heat it needs.

The supply cases have defined boundaries. The central onsite cost is €5.70/kg. A generic delivery allowance takes that case to €6.95/kg. The model’s separately configured hub-and-pipeline case is €5.81/kg. Those are different cost structures, not interchangeable price quotes for the same customer. Even the favourable 2050 case, at €3.46/kg with its specified delivery allowance and €28/MWh electricity, clears some industrial thresholds but still misses others.

Policy can create demand that market prices do not support. Carbon pricing, procurement mandates, subsidies and carbon contracts for difference can raise willingness or obligation to pay. That may be a deliberate industrial or climate choice, but governments should disclose the premium, who will finance it and the contract duration. Describing a regulated purchase as competitive demand hides the cost rather than eliminating it.

The EU energy regulators’ market monitoring points in the same direction. ACER reported 4.2 GW of committed electrolyser capacity in November 2026, including operating plants, projects under construction and those with final investment decisions. Roughly 49% was associated with refining, where renewable-fuel mandates provide a strong demand signal. The remaining industrial sectors face a harder test when policy does not compel the same purchases.

For lenders, the consequence is immediate. A proposed hydrogen plant costing €5/kg has no viable commercial case when its intended industrial buyer can afford €3/kg. A memorandum of understanding cannot close that €2/kg gap. Developers need buyers with enforceable contracts at prices that cover supply costs, lower production costs, or identified support durable enough to finance the difference.

Industrial and public procurement should start with the product the customer must deliver, calculate the best eligible competing route, and determine the maximum hydrogen price that route permits. Only then should investors consider electrolysers, pipelines and capacity targets. Suppliers do not set the market by drawing cheaper future cost curves. Industrial customers set the ceiling.

The full TFIE Briefing and open European hydrogen assessment contain the industrial route comparisons, scenario assumptions and research submitted to the European Commission. The next Briefing examines the competing routes for sourcing green ammonia and iron.

#5583. EV Share Jumps to 68%(!) and Small BEVs Rise to Power — China EV Sales Report for October

High gas prices and a never-ending wave of new BEVs have allowed October to reach 68% EV market share, a new record!

This record achievement is thanks to a significant ICE (internal combustion engine) crash. The overall market dropped 27% year over year (YoY), to around 1.6 million sales. Plugins were down 14% YoY, but pure ICE sales were much worse off, crashing 44% YoY!

Adding 45% BEV share to PHEVs (23% share) meant that in October a record 68% of all cars sold in China had a plug! If this trend continues in the future, the Chinese market will be fully electrified by 2030, and will be 100% BEV before 2035!

This great result pulled the 2026 share up, to 59%, already 5% above the full year result of 2025. BEVs on their own were up to 40% (versus 33% in 2025).

At this pace, I expect the final number for 2026 EV share in China to be above 60%, with BEVs alone north of the 40% mark. And when the largest global automotive market gets this electrified … then the ICE industry is in serious trouble.

The ICE extinction is also visible in the overall ranking. The overall top 10 was fully made by EVs. Again.

Looking at the best sellers in several size categories, the ICE extinction is also quite visible. Although, once again, the C-segment/compact category is the exception, with the only ICE representative showing up there — the Toyota RAV4, in 2nd, behind the leader Nevo Q05 and just ahead of the BYD Dolphin.

Having a quick look at the five size categories, the highlight is the B-segment/subcompact category, which is the hottest segment right now, with three of the top four top sellers in October belonging to this category.

Considering that 56% of the new SUVs launched this year were bigger than 5 meters/197 inches, there is a clear disconnect between what people want (small hatchbacks/crossovers) and what OEMs offer (large SUVs).

Another highlight is the fact that Xiaomi had a gold plus silver win in the full size category, with the BYD Great Tang closing out the podium while the midsize category podium was 100% BYD Group.

Here’s more info and commentary on October’s top selling electric models:

A BYD Dolphin for BYD Seagull money ($10,000). This was Geely’s internal memo to describe the formula for the Geely Xingyuan when developing its star model. With an interesting name — Xingyuan translates as “wishing upon a star” — it seems that Geely had its wish granted. However, the Geely model will have a tough adversary soon, as the new generation Seagull will land with only one goal — to steal the Xingyuan’s best seller status. But while the new Seagull didn’t land, the small Geely scored a record performance in October, with its 50,190 registrations representing a 14% increase YoY. Besides the new BYD Seagull, Geely’s hatchback now has to deal with Leapmotor’s B-segment contenders, the A10 and A05; the new-generation Wuling Bingo; Chery’s new QQ3; and let’s not forget about BYD’s Yuan Up, too.

BYD’s midsize SUV continues to transition into the new generation. Thanks to the ramp-up of the new Ultra body, BYD’s star player was 2nd, scoring 30,772 registrations. Sales were up by 6% compared to the same month last year. The new Ultra generation features lidar and 1,500 kW DC charging, and these two features aren’t even the most impressive aspects of the model! That would be the price. It starts at 152,000 yuan (or $22,000) with the 76 kWh battery, and it goes up to 180,000 yuan (or $26,000) with the 83 kWh battery. For comparison, the Tesla Model Y starts in China at 259,000 yuan ($38,250)…. Expect the Song to experience a second youth from here onwards, and while it is impossible to displace the Tesla Model Y from the runner-up spot, the last place on the podium is doable this year. And maybe higher up in 2027?

BYD’s small crossover continues to surprise, thanks to recently improved specs. BYD’s team player rose to 3rd in October, scoring 28,514 registrations, which was a 44% increase YoY and a new record score. Despite not being the most exciting vehicle to look at, or drive, the truth is that it is surfing the wave of small, affordable EVs better than most, scoring high in value for money with prices starting at $11,000 and higher end versions having BYD’s driver-assistance system. With small vehicles currently having a moment in China, expect the small crossover to show up in the top 5 more regularly.

Things continue to go well for the startup brand, with its new baby A10 now established as the star player in an already strong lineup. Thanks to 27,247 registrations, the small crossover managed another top 5 presence. The model has the usual value-for-money focus of the brand, and a low, low price of 66,000 yuan ($10,000). On top of that, however, the A10 offers something close to a distinct personality, as the design eschews the white product standard design of Leapmotor for something more personal, mostly thanks to the front and back lights and a floating roof effect. With B-segment BEVs now being the hottest category in China, Leapmotor is well prepared to benefit from the subcompact rising tide, thanks to the recent arrival of the A10 small crossover and its sibling, the A05 small hatchback. Talk about perfect timing….

After the SU7 and YU7, Xiaomi has another success on its hands. This big fat SUV — or shall I say SUVs, as the lineup is broken down between the 7-seat N90 and the 5-seat N70 — scored 27,132 registrations in only its second month on the market, pulling it immediately into the overall top 5. Even with the number of competitors growing every passing day, Xiaomi’s new land yachts have been selling like hot cakes, while drying up sales of the competition. Proof of that, is the fact that there were only two full size models in October’s top 20, with the other being … the Xiaomi SU7.

Looking at the rest of the best seller table, there were a number of models hitting record scores, starting with the #8 Nevo Q05 (20,022 units) continuing its success story. The #10 Bestune Xiaoma (17,887 units) benefitted from a recent refresh to score a surprise record score. Could this mean that small city EVs are (finally) seeing the light at the end of the tunnel?

As for the second half of the table, the highlight is the ramp-up of the Xpeng L03, with the attractive looking crossover, which kind of reminds of a mini-Lotus or small Polestar, reaching 13th thanks to 15,653 sales. This EV looks to be the startup’s star model thanks to a competitive price (124,000 RMB) that is half the price of the Tesla Model Y!

Other models with record performances were the #14 MG 4 (15,503 units), with the new generation proving to be much more popular and adaptable to local tastes than the previous one, and the #17 Chery QQ3 EV, which continued its slow climb in the ranking thanks to 14,041 sales, which made it the 5th B-segment model in the top 20.

Still in the top 20, we should also highlight the best selling foreign model, the Tesla Model Y VW ID.Aura T6, the new China-made (and China-developed) German crossover that has allowed Volkswagen to beat the Texans for the first time in years (the Tesla Model Y was down 60% YoY, to 7,807 units, while the Model 3 was down 85%, to 1,010 units). The Aura T6, VW’s take on the Model Y formula, rose to the 20th position in its first full month on the market!

A promising sign, or just hype? Only time will tell, but at least for now, VW seems to be getting some traction on the EV side of the Chinese market, a point also highlighted by the fact that it wasn’t just the Aura T6 getting decent sales. The also new VW ID.Era 5S (think Chinese VW Jetta) got 7,100 registrations in October. Is there hope after all?

Outside the top 20, the main event was the ramp-up of Nevo’s new baby, the Q06, which is looking to replicate the Q05’s success in the category above. With some 10,000 registrations in only its second month, it looks like it could join the top 20 soon. The same can be said about the Geely Galaxy TT, the make’s answer to the Xiaomi SU7, which had around 10,500 deliveries in October. Then there’s also the BYD Yuan Plus, which, after a long cross through the desert, seems to be picking up steam again. It scored 11,658 registrations last month, a new year best.

Looking at the 2026 ranking, the podium remained the same, and unless something unexpected happens, it should stay as it is until the end of the year, with the small Geely repeating its 2025 win. That also means the Tesla Model Y will return to silver after getting bronze in 2025, and the BYD Song will get back onto the podium after a one-year absence.

Below the podium positions, though, there was some news. The BYD Yuan Up and Leapmotor A10 are surfing the B-segment wave and both climbed a position, to 5th and 6th, respectively.

If the BYD Yuan Up continues on its current record streak, then the small crossover could even aim to surpass the #4 FCB Ti7 before the year ends.

Still, with three subcompacts currently in the top 6 (Geely Xingyuan, BYD Yuan Up, and Leapmotor A10), small is hot right now, in China.

Elsewhere, the BYD Sealion 05 was up two positions, to 16th, while the Wuling Mini EV is hoping to bring tiny city cars back into the spotlight, and in October, it jumped two positions, ending the month in 13th….

Looking at the overall manufacturer ranking, it is a sea of red. Everyone is losing sales — big time. The exception is #5 Leapmotor (+31% YoY), while #8 Xiaomi (+3% YoY) and #10 Xpeng (+0%) are just hanging on.

So, now, not only are foreign OEMs suffering (Nissan was down 70% in October, Honda was down 51%), but even domestic ones are being affected by the EV shift.

Some may be asking, “But what about #9 Nevo and its 125% growth rate?” Well, it isn’t so much that they are winning sales, but they are effectively replacing the sales of their mother brand, Changan, which in October was only 19th after a 60% drop in sales YoY.

Looking at the auto brand ranking, BYD is firmly in the leadership position (17.2%), while runner-up Geely held to its 7.1% market share.

Rising 3rd placed Leapmotor (6.7%, up 0.2%) is fast approaching Geely. It wouldn’t be surprising if the startup brand became the new silver medalist in the final days of the year.

One thing is for certain though — Leapmotor will win its first medal this year. The only question is the material that it will be made of….

Speaking of position changes, Tesla had a horrible month (4.4%, down from 4.9% in September), which allowed Wuling (4.6%) to climb one position in October, into 4th. At the end of the year, expect Tesla to keep the #4 position, as its December peak should help it surpass Wuling.

Looking at OEMs/automotive groups/alliances, BYD is leading with 21.9% share of the market, up 0.1% in October.

Meanwhile, Geely remained in second with 11.8% share. The multinational conglomerate has the runner-up spot secured.

#3 SAIC was stable (8%) and remained firm in the last place on the podium, while #4 Leapmotor (6.7%, up 0.2%) remained on the rise. Will we see the startup challenge SAIC in 2027 for the 3rd spot?

Always interested in the auto industry, particularly in electric cars, Jose has pioneered on documenting the plug-in sales evolution through the EV Sales blog, allowing him to gain an expert view on where EVs are right now and where they are headed in the future. Extending that work and expertise, Jose was co-founder of EV-Volumes and currently works with the European Alternative Fuels Observatory on EV sales matters.

#5581. New Jersey utilities have less than a year to implement virtual power plants

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Data from the two-year interim version of PowerUp NJ will inform the development of a long-term, open-access virtual power plant tariff, state utility regulators said on Nov. 12.

PowerUp NJ is a “first-of-its-kind statewide virtual power plant program that will give ratepayers more control to drive down their own energy costs,” Sherrill said in a statement that also referred to the plan as a “voluntary community energy program” that allows customers to “lend power from their homes” to support the grid.

The BPU’s order this month cements the program features and goals outlined in a July straw proposal from the agency.

Among other things, the order envisions a market-based framework that compensates distributed energy resources for grid services, rather than subsidizing their deployment; has standard program design elements across all four regulated utility territories; is technology neutral, with eligibility extended to net-metered solar-plus-storage systems; and requires utilities to test localized dispatch with an eye towards developing grid services that could defer or avoid infrastructure upgrades, according to a program factsheet.

The order also establishes technical working groups on measurement and verification, cost-benefit analysis, and program marketing; and aims to coordinate existing utility and state programs and proceedings involving distribution system planning, net metering, energy efficiency and electric vehicles, according to the factsheet.

The BPU expects smart thermostats, stationary home batteries, internet-connected electric water heaters and vehicle chargers and electric vehicles themselves to be eligible for PowerUp NJ at launch. Participating utilities and manufacturers will notify customers of specific eligible devices and models in mid-2027.

PowerUp NJ will be an opt-in program, meaning customers must affirmatively choose to participate. Once enrolled, customers will be able to “choose which devices and events to include” to avoid inconvenient or uncomfortable situations, like ceding control of a thermostat on a hot day, the BPU said.

Like other state-level VPPs, such as California’s Demand Side Grid Support program, PowerUp NJ aims to reduce grid stress and mitigate power price spikes by shifting load away from demand peaks. It will target both “distribution-level grid stress events” and PJM capacity and transmission coincident peaks, according to the program factsheet.

A preliminary staff analysis found that a 3% cut to peak demand could avoid about $60 million in annual system capacity costs while cutting local and greenhouse gas emissions from fossil-fueled peaker plants in New Jersey, the BPU said.

In a statement, BPU President Ben Hertz-Shargel said his agency would apply lessons from the two-year interim version of PowerUp NJ to the development of a long-term framework for the program.

“We will develop this program carefully, learn from real-world results, and expand it over time, growing the earnings opportunities for residential and business customers,” Hertz-Shargel said in a statement.

#5578. Who Needs Automatic Driving? You Love to Drive! However, for Many Handicapped, Tesla’s Full Self Driving Is the Only Option. It’s Use It, or Don’t Drive!

Editor’s note: In my opinion, a person should not be using Tesla FSD to drive somewhere if they aren’t capable of driving the trip fully themselves. Fritz makes the argument here that it is easier to supervise FSD and that is why someone with a handicap can use it when they don’t feel like they can drive fully on their own — they just have to pay attention and be ready to intervene if needed. However, my experience and opinion is that it can definitely be harder to intervene when needed than to just drive oneself, so if someone isn’t capable of driving fully themself, they shouldn’t be driving somewhere relying on FSD. As he is showing here, though, there are people — himself included — relying on FSD to drive when they feel they can’t drive a trip fully on their own for various physical reasons. Feel free to chime in below with your opinion on this matter. — Z.S.

Warning! Tesla’s Full Self Driving Supervised is an extremely capable automatic driving system. However, to use it you need to be an unimpaired legal driver who can intervene (Supervise) in a fraction of a second if needed. With that caveat, I will discuss in this article how FSD can take over driving for drivers with various kinds of handicaps.

I love to ride my ebike! I ride 15 to 20 miles every day. A few days ago, just after shoulder replacement, I remembered that Vasa Fitness has stationary bicycles. Just two days after surgery with my right arm in a sling, I knew that driving there would be difficult. I thought about asking my wife to drive me. But I got into my Tesla Model Y with Full Self Driving V 14 and said Hey Grok! Navigate me to Vasa Fitness. My car drove me out of my garage and without any intervention by me, touching the brake pedal, steering wheel or accelerator drove me to Vasa Fitness and parked me in the parking lot as you see in the lead photo.

Numerous situations have come to my attention where Tesla FSD can or could drive you safely, where the only other option would be to not drive.

For more on these matters, also read these pieces:

Elon Musk is a despicable person who no longer cares about clean energy. Also, he apparently no longer cares about his stockholders. His choice of supporting Donald Trump and spending almost half a billion dollars supporting Republican candidates in the last election have greatly damaged Tesla and reduced its stock value.

Many of my readers have been very critical of my support for Musk’s company. Unfortunately, in this early stage of EV development, no other company has the hardware, software, and Superchargers that make an electric car a reasonable alternative to a gas car for both city and cross-country driving anywhere in the US.

For those of you who wouldn’t consider buying a car from Musk’s company, I will present the next best alternative: You can buy a Rivian! It is a more traditional non-aerodynamic type of vehicle — a pickup truck or SUV. But it also has its benefits.

Rivian has the best non-Tesla driving automation available for consumer cars in the US at this time. I had planned to drive 40 miles down to the Rivian Demo Center in Salt Lake City to give their automatic driving a test drive. However, my research tells me that Rivian’s automatic driving is not nearly as good as Tesla’s at this point. It won’t drive you point to point in the city and on the highway without intervention. However, Rivian’s sensor suite is more comprehensive than Tesla’s. Tesla has made the bet that since human drivers can drive using only optical sensors, their eyes, it should be possible to drive automatically with only cameras. Rivian’s sensor suite includes 11 cameras and five radar sensors. Human drivers need to pull off the road when the visibility is very bad, but Rivian will eventually be able to continue driving during 0% visibility conditions.

Final comprehensive analysis: you may hate Musk, but if you want the latest technology in automatic driving and the freedom to drive electric on all the major highways in the US without careful planning, you’re going to have to choose a Tesla with Hardware V4. Tesla began delivering cars with HW4 in mid 2023.

According to Musk, 90% of traffic deaths would be prevented by using Tesla’s Full Self Driving. If true, you’d be crazy not to pay for it and activate it.

If you find any of my articles helpful to you, please use my referral link when buying a new Tesla: https://ts.la/arthur73734 (Be sure to use it when you make your order).

Tesla keeps changing the program, but here’s the latest: If you are buying a new Tesla Model 3 or Y and use my link, you will get 2 months of free Full Self Driving (a ~$200 value) on top of the one month that comes free with every new Model 3 or Y right now. This will get you a total of 3 months of free Full Self Driving. It will cost $99/month after that. At this moment, if you trade in a gas car, Tesla is offering you two years of free Supercharging with the purchase of a new Tesla.

#5574. Ford Begins Producing Its Own LFP Batteries

Ford is now a battery producer. Yes, it’s using battery technology licensed from CATL, but it is now producing EV batteries it is going to be using in its own vehicles. In fact, it is reportedly doing so in a really fast, advanced way. “The BlueOval Battery Park Michigan assembly lines can deliver 40 cells every minute, making it among the fastest LFP prismatic battery assembly lines outside of China.2 That pace helps drive down battery costs, and the facility has capacity for more. But speed must go hand in hand with quality,” Charles Poon, vice president of hardware engineering at Ford, writes.

“The first lithium iron phosphate (LFP) prismatic battery cells are rolling off the line at BlueOval Battery Park Michigan and headed to Louisville Assembly Plant to power Ford Fathom prototypes. With this milestone, Ford is now in the battery business and will be the first to deliver LFP batteries in the U.S. for mainstream consumer automotive use,” Poon writes. He goes on to explain further in a recent article why Ford chose to produce LFP battery cells and a bit on how it is doing so. Here’s a bit more from that article:

“The shipment caps years of work by the team that designed, built, and launched the plant on schedule. Ford now delivers cells at a plant it owns and operates, building expertise to support more affordable electric vehicles (EVs) and Ford Energy’s battery energy storage business in Kentucky.

“Why Start with LFP Prismatic for Fathom?

“When Alan Clarke, vice president, Advanced Development Projects, first joined the development team that created the Universal Electric Vehicle Platform, he issued a clear challenge: find an efficient LFP prismatic battery. The goal was to make EVs significantly more affordable.

“We talked to battery companies around the world. We wanted a technology that was proven in the field, ready to manufacture at scale, and suited to what we were building. That led us to the world’s leading battery manufacturer, CATL, whose batteries power 30 million vehicles across the globe.

“A battery is one of the biggest contributors to an EV’s cost and weight. So, we asked: How do we get more out of the battery without simply adding more cells? Our answer was efficiency over excess.

“LFP chemistry uses no nickel or cobalt, which helps hold down costs. It also holds up over years of charging and use. That durability is valuable as we give customers ways to use their battery beyond driving. For example, AC bidirectional power lets a vehicle receive electricity from a properly equipped home and supply power back to it.1

“This milestone is about more than shipping our first cells. We’re building the expertise to produce high-quality batteries at scale — for more affordable EVs and a new energy storage business.”

It looks like a serious effort and serious progress toward making more affordable, more competitive electric cars at Ford. The aim has been to get as good as making electric cars as Chinese companies are. How effective and successful will this new path be for Ford? We’ll see.

#5570. Memo To Musk: Denial Is Just The First Step On The Long Road To Recovery

Tesla CEO Elon Musk made headlines yet again last week, when he publicly denied any affiliation with US President Donald Trump’s “MAGA” movement. If Musk intends to stage a sort of political recovery, he still has a long way to go. Denial is just a precursor to recovery, which begins in earnest when you stop denying the reality of your situation and start admitting you need help from a higher power or some such. And in fact, a higher power is coming for Musk —  if not in spiritual form, then in the form of Congress, where the incoming Democratic majority plans to exercise its authority, and call Musk to account for manipulating SpaceX satellite services in Ukraine.

During an interview for the New York Times DealBook Summit last week, Elon Musk claimed that he was not affiliated with MAGA, though he did not apologize for supporting MAGA candidates this year, or for supporting Donald Trump in 2024. Musk simply said he “felt foolish” for wasting his money on losers.

In the same interview, Musk insisted — falsely — that Russia was the aggrieved party when Russian President Vladimir Putin invaded the Donbas region of Ukraine back in 2014. Justin sayin’…

For those of you wondering why Elon Musk’s politics should be a matter of concern to CleanTechnica, that’s a good question, and here’s the answer: Elon Musk sailed into clean tech history on the coattails of the EV startup Tesla Motors (soon to become simply Tesla) in the early 2000’s, only to emerge in more recent years as a champion of the far right alongside President Donald Trump’s notoriously racist, fossil fuel-supporting “Make America Great Again” movement, thereby setting back the US decarbonization movement by years if not decades.

Musk has also made his own contribution to the resurgence of fossil fuels in the US. His new xAI data centers are well known for deploying loosely regulated (or illegal) on-site gas generators, and his SpaceX venture has been gobbling up natural gas for rocket fuel while laying plans for future expansion.

So much for Elon Musk’s footprint on the US energy profile. Let’s move over to Ukraine, where rich fossil energy and mineral resources in the nation’s Donbas region prompted Russian President Putin to send unmarked soldiers — aka the “little green men” — to annex that portion of Ukraine in 2014 (see clarification/correction below). A ceasefire temporarily calmed the situation until February of 2022, when Putin sent a fully equipped convoy to the Ukrainian capital city Kyiv, aiming to take over the entire seat of government in one fell swoop — literally, in one day.

The effort failed, both militarily and in terms of Putin’s energy goals. Ukraine promptly cut Russia off from its electricity grid and hooked up with the European grid, a complex effort assisted by the Biden administration. Ukraine also set the wheels in motion to join the European Union as a significant source of clean energy, including green hydrogen gas alongside its copious potential for clean power including biomass, wind, and solar power.

To put that into perspective, consider that Ukraine is almost 90% as large as Texas, which has staked out a leadership position in wind and solar power among the 50 US states.

With Ukraine as a leading renewable energy supplier, the EU would have a fresh opportunity to meets its decarbonization goals on schedule while also reducing imports of fossil energy from Russia… And… Well… That’s where Elon Musk enters the picture.

Musk first emerged as a full blown MAGA supporter in 2020, when he helped Trump stir up conspiracy theories and confusion over the federal response to the COVID-19 crisis, in a failed effort to support the President’s bid for re-election that year. Had Trump won, he would have continued to implement a key part of his foreign policy agenda, namely, withdrawing the US from NATO, the North Atlantic Treaty Alliance. NATO formed in 1949 as an effort to curb Russian expansion in Europe, an effort Trump has upended on account of his longstanding friendship with Putin.

The election of Joe Biden in 2020 did not prevent Musk from providing additional aid and comfort to Trump. Musk used his media-grabbing public profile to undercut the authority of President Biden at every opportunity, including on the world stage as well as here at home. The stream of insults extended into January of 2022, even as the reality of a fresh round of Russian expansion into Europe began to materialize.

Musk had an additional opportunity to challenge Biden’s authority in January of 2022, when the so-called “Freedom Convoy” of truckers barricaded government offices in Canada, ostensibly in protest of a vaccine mandate. Musk gleefully cheered the effort, which disrupted transportation systems and distracted public attention in two key NATO allies — Canada and the US — even as Russia began ratcheting up its plans to take over Kyiv.

However, once it became clear that Putin’s one-day takeover of Ukraine was a failure, Musk about-faced. He took credit for providing “free” communications aid to Ukraine in the form of satellite services through the Starlink branch of SpaceX.

JK! A later assessment revealed that other sources shouldered about 85% of the cost of Starlink terminals initially supplied to Ukraine, including USAID here in the US (not a typo — that’s the US Administration for International Development, the same agency that Musk later destroyed in 2025).

Poland and other groups also contributed initial assistance to purchase  the terminals alongside an estimated 30% of connectivity costs. In addition, by September of 2022 Musk was making the case for US taxpayers to foot the bill.

Don’t just take my word for it. In 2023, the Belfer Center for International Affairs at Harvard recapped SpaceX’s negotiations over payment for its services to Ukraine during the previous year, concluding that “we can assume the U.S. government agreed to pay some, if not all, of the costs for continued operation of the technology.”

“SpaceX, and particularly Musk, was able to play the role of wartime hero, showcasing the power and value of the technology products the company developed, while simultaneously avoiding sustained and hefty bills,” Belfer Center observed.

US Department of Defense contract or not, by 2023 SpaceX was already making unilateral decisions that restricted Ukraine’s ability to deploy the technology, with SpaceX CEO Gwynne Shotwell publicly insisting that the company never intended for Ukraine to use it “for offensive purposes.”

Of course, “offensive” is in the eye of the beholder. Since Ukraine is the invaded party seeking to assert its rights over internationally recognized borders against Russia, one could argue that every action Ukraine takes is a defensive one until Putin agrees to concede defeat and cede back his illegal claims over Ukrainian lands.

Of course, that’s not how Musk portrayed the situation in his DealBook interview last week. Nevertheless, one also could argue that the US government, not the vendor, controls the use of contractually obligated resources. Be that as it may, Musk raised the bar considerably in October, when he — or Trump — or both — once again dialed down satellite service in Ukraine, exposing that country to further attacks.

Musk attempted to mollify bipartisan outrage over the debacle with yet another publicity stunt for SpaceX. However, service interruptions have continued into this week. Meanwhile, Trump has spent the same week cozying up to both Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping, aided and abetted by an obedient Republican majority in Congress.

That majority will evaporate, though, in January 3, which is just around the corner. The incoming Democratic majority is already gearing up for a fight. In a letter dated November 20, the incoming Chair of the House Intelligence Committee, Jim Himes (D-CT), demanded an in-person briefing from senior SpaceX officers, seeking an explanation for the disruption of military-grade Starshield service in Ukraine. As leverage, Himes warned Musk that a Democratic majority in Congress will do what the Republican majority failed to do, namely, exercise its constitutional authority over federal contracts.

Referring specifically to SpaceX’s military-grade Starshield satellite services in Ukraine, Himes warned Musk that his entire suite of present and future SpaceX security contracts with the US government is at risk. “SpaceX’s potentially unilateral modification of a U.S. Government contract, combined with its lack of responsiveness to congressional oversight, calls into question every contract the U.S. Government has with SpaceX,” Himes explained.

“For that reason, it is difficult for me to continue supporting the authorization of funding for these SpaceX- developed, government-owned systems until I receive satisfactory answers to my questions about the Starshield service in Ukraine,” Himes continued.

Will Musk simply ignore the request for a briefing? After all, as Himes notes, Musk failed to respond to earlier requests. Stay tuned…

#5566. DOJ closes NextEra-Dominion antitrust probe as state hearings continue

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The decision followed a promise by the utilities to allow ISO-NE’s internal market monitor to investigate proposed plant retirements.

In a July filing with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission, Dominion said the merger needs approval from FERC, the Nuclear Regulatory Commission, the SCC, the North Carolina Utilities Commission and the Public Service Commission of South Carolina.

The SCC has been holding daily evidentiary hearings since Nov. 17, while South Carolina and North Carolina regulators have scheduled theirs to begin in December and February, respectively. Neither FERC nor the NRC have issued a decision.

Dominion and NextEra announced in a Sept. 14 release that they would double a proposed monthly residential bill credit for Virginia customers from two to four years.

In that release, the companies also said they would accelerate solar, storage and other resources in Virginia to “affordably power the Commonwealth’s growth,” advance the Virginia Clean Economy Act and deliver “long-term affordability benefits by leveraging the combined company’s scale to buy, build, finance and operate more efficiently.”

Last week, SCC regulators and attorneys for the state pressed witnesses, including Baine, NextEra CEO John Ketchum and Dominion Energy CEO Bob Blue, on how customer benefits could be calculated and ensured.

Baine said that when Dominion merged with SCANA in 2019, it saw “benefits of scale” where input cost was lowered, adding, “We believe it will be on a much bigger scale with this merger, but just can't do the math for you today.”

John Farmer, a senior assistant attorney general for Virginia, pointed Ketchum to a written response from Dominion and NextEra in which they said they “have not prepared a year-by-year quantification of benefits to Dominion Energy Virginia's existing customers.”

Ketchum said he stands behind “the fact that we've offered up the bill credits” and broadly outlined the potential operational benefits of a combined company.

“Shame on us if we can't realize the scale benefits that I've spoken about,” Ketchum said in Wednesday’s hearing. “That’s what we do.”

SCC Chair Kelsey Bagot asked Blue how he would ensure that “potential issues do not erode the benefits” that are “possible” as a result of the merger.

“Have you started developing a plan?” Bagot said.

Blue said that if the merger isn’t managed well, “it is possible that the longer-term benefits that we're describing won't materialize as quickly.”

“As I mentioned in my answer to you before, I don't believe that would leave our customers worse off,” he continued. “There may be some missed opportunities.”

Some intervenors also criticized the bill credit proposal.

Bloom Advisory Partners partner Teresa Ringenbach testified on behalf of the Retail Energy Advancement League that the bill credit structure “effectively penalizes customers for choosing a competitive supplier,” which she said “is discriminatory and undermines the statutorily mandated retail choice framework.”

“A large general service customer using 72,000 megawatt hours per year would receive approximately $225,000 if it shops, compared to approximately $642,000 if it does not,” Ringenbach said. “That difference is a price signal, and it’s funded by the utility's new parent, and it rewards remaining on the utility's generation service.”

“Shareholder credits should not be viewed as the price of getting a merger approved,” she added. “They should deliver real value to every single wires customer.”

Bagot asked Richard McCann, an energy expert testifying on behalf of Gov. Abigail Spanberger’s office, “Do we have the ability to provide rate credits to a single rate class? For example … only to residential customers and not to any of the other rate classes.”

“I recognize that's not exactly what you're proposing,” Bagot added, “but it does shift potentially what that rate credit would be, maybe closer to $29 per month.”

Raising the credit to $29 a month was a condition McCann proposed in his written testimony, along with a six-year residential rate freeze.

In response to Bagot, McCann said, “I know that at $10, they won't notice if it phases out or not. $30 a month, they might notice that the credit disappears if it's a sudden shock.”

He said that implementing the higher credit for residential ratepayers alongside other methods he proposed — such as raising the debt share of Dominion's capital structure — could lower bills over time and provide a “smooth glide path” for the end of the credits.

SCC Commissioner Jehmal Hudson pushed back on the rate freeze proposal, asking McCann what authority allows the SCC to “freeze future base rate increases irrespective of the future costs” and noting that rate freezes in Virginia “have primarily stemmed from the General Assembly legislation.”

“You are in an extraordinary position right now with this merger,” McCann said. “There are certain things that you have discretion about going forward — you will have to decide if you have that discretion.”

#5565. The Insurance Affordability Crisis: Risks, Resilience, and Solutions

NRDC resources on insurance affordability, market availability, climate risk, resilience investments, and strategies to protect homeowners, businesses, and communities.

For property owners, insurance provides a critical financial safety net that protects homes, businesses, and other assets from unexpected losses, helping owners recover and rebuild when disasters occur. Beyond individual protection, insurance is fundamental to the resilience of communities and local economies, enabling faster recovery, supporting continued investment, and reducing the long-term financial impacts of shocks. As risks become more frequent, severe, and costly, access to affordable, available, and reliable insurance is increasingly essential, not only for safeguarding property owners but also for maintaining overall property affordability, economic stability, and the resilience and prosperity of the communities in which they live and operate.

The frequency and severity of losses are increasing faster than insurers can sustainably price and absorb them, leading to higher premiums, reduced coverage availability, and, in some cases, the withdrawal of insurers from certain markets. This trend is eroding the affordability and accessibility of insurance, increasing the total cost of property ownership, and placing additional pressure on housing and business affordability while leaving individuals, businesses, and communities more physically and financially exposed.

However, the insurance sector is increasingly recognized as a key component of resilience, incentivizing risk reduction through pricing and support for adaptation measures that can help moderate long-term insurance costs. When functioning effectively, insurance enables communities to not only recover from shocks but also to better prepare for future risks while preserving access to coverage.

Linked below are reports, expert blogs, and other resources that examine the insurability crisis and how insurance can play a key role in advancing housing resilience and adaptation.

NRDC’s director of insurance and financial risk, Alfonso Pating, breaks down how homeowner’s insurance works and why climate change is making it unaffordable.

More reports and resources on this topic here.

Article originally published on NRDC website.
